mr president, before we get to the agenda, i would like a little clarification on an item on the agenda for tomorrow morning, which you have already mentioned: the address by the grand mufti of syria. his will be the first of a series of speeches by religious leaders before the plenary of this house in the context of the european year of intercultural dialogue. i would like to know why the conference of presidents has chosen to interpret intercultural dialogue exclusively as an interreligious monologue and whether it feels a part-session is an appropriate platform for religious messages. (applause) finally, i would like to know whether the conference of presidents is willing to consider widening the list of guest speakers and making it more representative by including women and non-religious organisations. the alde group has suggested that ms jahangir, the un special rapporteur on freedom of religion or belief, should be invited to address the plenary as well. i hope you can enlighten me. (applause)
mr president, transport emissions account for one fifth of greenhouse gas emissions. the number of cars per thousand inhabitants is increasing rapidly all over the world. so cars really need to become sustainable. but this is a message that is only weakly conveyed in the commission's response to the cars 21 report and in the report itself. the document talks about making cars cleaner and it talks about the competitiveness of the car industry. but the link between the two is not being made, when it is clear that sustainable and fuel-efficient cars are the future. indian and chinese automakers are taking over the world with small, fuel-efficient, clean, and affordable cars. the european car industry is now fighting a back-and-forth battle to get a little more co2 into the atmosphere. so it is a pity for the environment, but also for employment in the european union. the cars 21 high-level group is a missed opportunity to put forward a vision of a sustainable automotive industry, a vision that responds to the real challenges. innovation is the key word here. in the united states, a hydrogen highway is being built and the market is being prepared for electric cars. what about the european initiatives? some car manufacturers have prototypes ready, but there's no hydrogen on the road yet. it is time to join hands to make rapid progress through cooperation between governments and industry. research alone is not enough. the setting up of high-level groups is in itself a very good idea, but unfortunately cars 21 was dominated by a conservative industry. it is really important that the high-level groups have a composition that does justice to the challenges we face. in the years to come, millions of new cars will be coming onto the market, and it would really help if they were clean and economical.
mr president, i think the car lobby's frantic efforts to delay the most necessary environmental measures for as long as possible are succeeding. time and again, members of this house seem to be sensitive to this lobby. first mr davies and now also mr chatzimarkakis. waiting until 2015 to substantially reduce co2 emissions from cars is really irresponsible. in addition, it is technically possible to reach 120 grams per kilometer by 2012, and the current situation is due solely to the automobile industry itself. the commission has already taken a number of measures to reduce emissions. it turns out that only the setting of mandatory standards will work. we expect the european citizens to make sacrifices to save our climate. why not ask the car industry the same? so, up to 120 grams of co2 per kilometer by 2012.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on transport and tourism, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 4064/89 on the common organization of the market in fruit and vegetables (com (97) 0406 - c4-0414/97-97/0141 (cns)) (rapporteur: mr pimenta). major airports with an important role as international connecting points, or as access to a metropolitan area, have become shopping centres, offices and distribution companies, public transport hubs on the ground and toll gates to the surrounding area. they are either an important policy tool in the hands of the government or they have been sold to an international corporation that only seeks profit and expansion. they also make profits outside the aviation sector, are part of an international competition and find themselves in conflicts of interest with airlines, passengers, fellow users and local residents. airports can be abused by forcing anyone who depends on them - from airlines to passengers - to contribute disproportionately to profits. such expensive airports also lead to the emergence of competing, smaller airports in their immediate vicinity, with lower fares, poorer facilities, even more space requirements and even more congested areas. the european union's objective in setting rules for airport revenues should not be to secure profits for these companies or to limit costs for airlines, nor to boost air traffic by offering passengers the lowest possible fares. the aim must be to protect the environment as much as possible against the ever-growing expansion of aviation and to protect safety as much as possible. it should also be transparent, so that cash flows and unjustified tariff differences are no longer covered by trade secrets. the outcome is now that airports with between 1 and 5 million passengers per year will be exempted from these rules. i would not be surprised if this resulted in more investment being made in smaller airports in densely populated areas. the text will also be moderated in relation to the commission's original proposal on other aspects, such as the complaints procedure, the strictness of the requirements and the level of profits. i expect this to lead to continued abuse and within a few years to calls for stricter rules.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the airports are not exploited in the same way as other airports. i am particularly satisfied with three points. first of all, the adjustment of the scope. not all airports are in a position to abuse their position. the concentration on larger airports is therefore useful and i therefore disagree with some of the previous speakers. i am also pleased that the possibility of differentiating rates has been included. given the negative environmental impacts of aviation at and around airports, the differentiation tool could provide an incentive to use cleaner and quieter aircraft. i also share the view that we must be careful not to place too heavy an administrative burden on airports. in the context of transparency, they should disclose certain information, but they should do so with a strict eye on costs and confidentiality. i would like to conclude by making a comment on amendment no 19. while i welcome the existence of an independent supervisory body, i believe that the wording of amendment no 19 may cause problems in some countries, since both the setting up of procedures and their supervision are assigned to one and the same organization. i hope that the discussions with the council will provide an opportunity to resolve this point to my satisfaction. in conclusion, i would like to thank the rapporteur for his work and look forward to the further consideration of this report.
member of the commission. - the madam president, ladies and gentlemen of the european parliament, i should first like to thank and congratulate the european parliament and in particular the rapporteur, mr blokland, the shadow rapporteurs and the committee on the environment, public health and food safety, as well as mrs mann and the committee on industry, research and energy for their excellent reports and the opinion delivered at the first reading. over the last 30 years, european union policies and legislation to protect public health and the environment have made significant progress in reducing the risks from chemicals, not only in the european union but also on a global scale. the european union has always been an important partner in international conventions, including the rotterdam convention on the consent procedure for certain dangerous chemicals and pesticides in international trade. regulation 304/2003 of the european parliament and of the council on the import and export of dangerous chemicals applies the rotterdam convention within the community. in many respects the regulation goes beyond the provisions of the rotterdam convention and goes further by offering a higher level of protection for countries importing chemicals. by judgment of 10 january 2006, the court of justice of the european communities annulled regulation no 304 of 2003 on the ground that it should be based on a dual legal basis. the court of justice has held that the court of first instance did not have jurisdiction to hear the appeal. the aim of this proposal is therefore to adopt a new regulation based on the twofold legal basis referred to above. at the same time, the commission, on the basis of a report on the experience gained so far in the application of the relevant procedures, has proposed a number of technical amendments to the provisions of the regulation. this report was submitted to the european parliament and the council together with this proposal. the main amendments proposed by the commission to the regulation are as follows: first, limited exceptions are proposed to the central condition of explicit consent in importing countries before export. exemptions shall only apply if, despite the reasonable efforts of the commission and the national authority designated by the exporting country, the request is not met and if certain conditions are met. these exceptions seek to provide some flexibility while maintaining a high level of protection, even higher than that provided by the rotterdam convention. secondly, it is proposed to exempt from the requirement of explicit consent in cases of export of chemicals to oecd countries, provided certain conditions are met. thirdly, the rules on the duration of explicit consents are clarified. express consents and the admissibility of alternative evidence shall also be reviewed at regular intervals. fourth, procedures and means are put in place to facilitate customs authorities' access to information, because in most member states these authorities play a major role in ensuring compliance with the regulation, particularly with regard to export controls. madam president, i welcome the efforts of all of us to reach agreement on this regulation at first reading. the european commission can accept the compromise package of proposals to reach an agreement at first reading.
- i'm the reporter. - mr president, as rapporteur on the import and export of chemicals, i am pleased to say that we have achieved a good result in the first reading. i want to thank everyone who has worked on this. i am thinking in particular of the secretariat of the committee on the environment and the shadow rapporteurs and their staff who have been very good to me. i have greatly appreciated the good cooperation with the council, particularly with the portuguese presidency, and with the european commission. it was a technically complicated proposal, sometimes with heated debate about what was best for the environment. i think the most important thing we have achieved is that this new regulation will make the trade in dangerous chemicals ethical. we have tried to apply the principle known as the golden rule: "what you do not want done to you, do not do to another". although this is an old principle, found in the bible as well as in other religions, this aspect has led to the necessary discussions. the commission's original proposal contained a very extensive system for allowing exports of dangerous chemicals to proceed with tacit approval. this was proposed, whereas it is important, particularly for developing countries, that there is complete clarity about what is being imported. we are talking about substances which are banned or severely restricted in the european union. i believe that we have come to a good agreement. this means that the protection and awareness of developing countries regarding dangerous substances is guaranteed. president, this new regulation is a good implementation of the global rotterdam convention, which was signed in 1998 but has officially entered into force since 2004. i would therefore strongly recommend that everyone vote tomorrow before the agreement reached with the council. finally, mr president, i would like to call for the council and the commission to do their utmost to extend the list of substances in the rotterdam convention. at present, there are some 40 substances on the list of that convention. there are at least 200 substances on the nomination to be assessed and possibly added to that list. the other substances must be dealt with urgently, in order to protect the developing countries in particular.
member of the commission. - madam president, i thank all the speakers for their positive contributions. i fully recognize and share parliament's desire for a high level of protection of human health and the environment, particularly in those countries which do not have the necessary infrastructure and capacity to properly manage hazardous chemicals. i also fully share the view that the european community should play a leading role in establishing a community legal framework which ensures a high level of protection. in many respects the regulation goes beyond the provisions of the rotterdam convention and establishes conditions of application which offer a high level of protection for all countries of the world and not just the parties to the convention. i am convinced that this example will encourage other countries to follow suit and accede to the convention. the commission will make every effort to support the rotterdam convention in order to achieve these objectives. in response to the concerns regarding arsenic and mercury, the commission would like to stress that, in accordance with article 22 (3) of regulation 304 of 2003, if metallic arsenic is banned or severely restricted in the community, then a proposal will be submitted to adapt the relevant annex. it also recalls that work is under way in the council and parliament on a proposal for a ban on mercury exports to countries outside the community, which, if it is adopted, goes beyond the requirement of consent imposed by the rotterdam convention and the community regulation implementing it. i would like to thank the rapporteur and the shadow rapporteurs once again for their efforts and their excellent work, and in particular mr blokland. the commission is very satisfied with the outcome of the negotiations and considers all the proposed compromise amendments acceptable. as regards the double legal basis, it would indeed be much easier and simpler if we had only one legal basis, but you know the problem that arises in many cases, here we have - if i remember correctly - articles 133 to 175 and in other cases - most of them - we have articles 95 to 175. of course, whenever we propose a legal basis, we always rely on the opinion of our legal service. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the problem was that when i was rapporteur, i had a visit from the council presidency and the council presidency said: 'we have unanimously in the council major problems with the legal basis 'trade'; we want to make it environmentally friendly, on the advice of our legal service". the european parliament consulted the legal service of the parliament, and our legal service was also absolutely convinced that the legal basis should not be 'trade' but 'environment'. on that basis we came to this conclusion - almost unanimously, at least in the council. it would have been better not to have referred the matter to the court but to have sat down with the commissioner, the council presidency and parliament to try to find a political solution to the problem. what happened now? yes, thankfully the regulation has come into force. it worked; the council was in a hurry, parliament was in a hurry. but there was a lot of debate, and it ultimately led to everyone being wrong: the commission, the council and parliament. the court has delivered a sort of solomon's judgment and said: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. and in a situation like that, you're at the point where we're all wrong, where it's years of delay. fortunately, not to the detriment of the developing countries, for the work has simply continued. anyway, we've been able to deal with this for a while. i think we should avoid these problems together in the future. we also raised this issue with commissioner dimas when he had his briefing with parliament's committee on the environment. we also raised the problem with commissioner verheugen and with the president of the commission, mr barroso, during the three hearings. we certainly got the impression at the time that it was not so much a decision of the european commission to go to the court of justice as a decision of the legal service. i think that it should no longer be left to the lawyers but to the politicians to solve this problem.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. my colleague mr őry has just said that. the coordination regulation we are discussing today is in fact an annual adjustment of the national legislation. the member states should actually - and then there would be no problem - test every piece of legislation or every amendment to social or fiscal legislation to see whether it is also europe-proof. if the result were clear, then there would be no need for adjustments later on. i have tabled a number of amendments with mr őry, and i think that each member state should look at the annual adjustment to see whether all the proposals made in the administrative consultation are in line with the realities of europe. mr president, we have tabled two or three amendments. the first two, 7 and 8, concern the health insurance of members of the family of military personnel resident in belgium or germany. the dutch military are not covered by the health insurance law, which means that their family members could not get insurance and had to join an increasingly expensive fund. the dutch government has written to the house asking the european parliament to adopt the amendments, as this is the quickest solution. the third amendment - amendment no 10 - concerns the dutch law on childcare. a family living in the netherlands and working on the other side of the border was not entitled to childcare allowance. that has now been settled by a change in the law. so that means that for a lot of people, because of our persistence, we've accomplished a number of things. i also thank all those colleagues who were not deterred by all the arguments of the second reading but who followed us, which enabled us to achieve a great deal.
president. - i thank mr őry for his excellent work. i would like to emphasize one thing in the short time i have to speak. not all the lack of coordination of social security systems is due to regulation. many of the problems that arise are the result of the implementation of these rules, which is the responsibility of the member states themselves. the work on the öry report has brought to light some of these practical problems. that's exactly what coordination is all about. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. i am therefore glad that the council presidents consult parliament from time to time, including on annexes xi and vi to regulation 883, for which i am the rapporteur. it is of the utmost importance that the council's work on this regulation and its annexes be completed within this parliament's mandate. i wish the next council presidents every success in this task.

i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr cashman, for his report, which i have always considered to be a great ally in the fight to defend fundamental rights. my disagreement with the rapporteur is not about substance, about content, but about strategy. it may be a strategy to give up a few priorities in exchange for support from the council, but does the council offer us enough to let go of our own desires? not that i've ever seen. and in that case, i prefer to just make a point. the alde amendments propose four additional priorities, namely homophobia, privacy, counter-terrorism policy and fundamental rights, and discrimination against roma. let these be the four main areas where member states are constantly violating fundamental rights. the fundamental rights office should have become a watchdog to keep the member states on the right track. but unfortunately, that did not work out. it's already become a toothless tiger anyway. i think - and i have listened to what commissioner frattini has just said - that the european parliament, as the main partner of the fundamental rights office, should simply set clear priorities of its own. i am not sure that the council is prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. so, ladies and gentlemen, i am afraid that i agree with the rapporteur on the substance - i will discuss it in my group - but i cannot support the strategy.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. we have before us a proposal from the european commission to extend the programme which uses the most modern technologies to monitor our european agriculture. after years of research, the scientists involved can rightly feel proud. satellites can be used to monitor the use and condition of the soil, to study the effects of climate change, and even to predict our harvests. this way we can keep a finger on the pulse and predict and correct problems in time, such as this year's failed grain harvest. mr president, i am an engineer by training and this kind of practical application of advanced technology for our welfare makes my heart beat faster. it's a tomtom system for farming. who would have thought? the question now is whether we as a parliament agree to the continuation of research into and use of this application. how could we possibly answer that in the negative? i would advise my colleagues in the socialist group to vote in favour of this directive, but i would like to make a few comments to the commissioner who is with us at this late hour. firstly, i have my doubts about the inclusion of the budget and the programme in the agricultural guarantee fund. until now, this project had its own budget line, which allowed parliament to keep a finger on the pulse and to be regularly informed. despite the proven benefits of the programme, research is still ongoing. i would be very sorry if the commission did not report back to us on the progress and results of this programme, as well as on the cost sheet. as a representative of the people and as a person with an interest in the subject, i would urge that the separate budget line be retained so that we can be kept informed and be able to think along with the rest of the community in the future. secondly, i would like to see all research results made freely available and accessible to other interested parties, such as universities and research institutes. the data provided by mars and lucas can be of great use, especially in the field of climate research. i would like to conclude my maiden speech in this chamber with this call for transparency at all levels. i hope that mrs fischer boel will give me a positive response.
in writing. − in december 2005, the council granted the former yugoslav republic of macedonia candidate status and in june 2006 montenegro was recognised as an independent state. in an urgent procedure without debate, it is now proposed in article 1 of the regulation to grant a european partnership to albania, bosnia and herzegovina, montenegro and serbia, including kosovo, and in article 1a to grant an accession partnership to croatia and macedonia. the definitions of both constructions are almost identical. the rapporteur supports the commission's proposal and asks the council to consult parliament again if it wishes to depart from this proposal. the possibility of that deviation occurring seems real to me. the recognition of kosovo's independence, which is expected in a few weeks, is not yet settled. it is also unclear whether this means that macedonia will have to wait longer for the start of accession negotiations, or whether serbia will be given a priority accession perspective. as is well known, the netherlands and belgium oppose the latter as long as war criminal ratko mladić has not been extradited to the icty in the hague. so i expect this item to be back on the agenda, but do not vote against it.
in writing. - i voted against the report by mr chatzimarkakis because the commission's proposal is more environmentally friendly. the rapporteur proposes a target of 125 grams of co2 per km by 2015. the commission proposal sets a maximum of 120 grams of co2 emissions in 2012, and i have had this brought back as an amendment. this one just didn't make it, though. i wonder where the rapporteur gets the environmentalally inferior product without being influenced by the automobile industry lobby. for years voluntary agreements between the commission and the automobile industry have been in place to reduce co2 emissions. the commission's objective is not a windfall and, according to the automobile industry, is technically feasible and innovative in terms of an export product. however, they will then make temporarily less profit in order to stabilise their competitive position in the future. the argument of loss of employment is being used improperly. i point out that only 6% of the cost of producing new cars is personnel costs. the choice of labour cost savings is independent of the shift to 120 grams of co2 per km in 2012. if the automobile industry chooses to move to low-wage countries, it will.
in writing. - i voted against the chatzimarkakis report with my colleague kartika liotard, because the commission's original proposal is more environmentally friendly. the rapporteur proposes a target of 125 grams of co2 per kilometre by 2015. the commission proposal called for a maximum of 120 grams of co2 emissions in 2012. my group has brought this back as an amendment today, but it has not succeeded. i wonder where the rapporteur gets this environmentally worse proposal from, if it is not influenced by the automobile lobby. voluntary agreements between the commission and the automotive industry to reduce co2 emissions have existed for many years. the commission's objective does not just fall from the sky. the automotive industry considers it technically feasible and can provide an innovative export product. the car manufacturers will thus temporarily lose profits, which they consider to be a detriment to their ability to strengthen their competitive position. the argument that jobs could be lost is being misused. only 6% of the cost of producing new cars is staff. any lower labour costs are independent of the changeover to 120 grams co2 per km in 2012. if the automobile industry wants to move to low-wage countries, a lower standard will not deter it.
mr president, my congratulations to slovenia, and welcome to prime minister janša: only four years a member of the eu and already the european presidency. it means work and responsibility, but it is also a proof of confidence in your young state, president-in-office. what worries and disappoints me – us, i should say – is that an impressive number of meps from very different countries and groups have still not received a clear answer to their repeated request as to whether slovenia is going to host the first annual roma conference. a big thing, but also a huge challenge – and you are not alone. the european commission has offered financial support. moral support comes from the european council which, in the conclusions under point 50, underlines that the eu should appear with concrete steps on roma issues. logistical support comes from the european roma community, who expressed willingness to cooperate closely wherever they can. political support comes from many meps from different groups in this house who are aware of europe’s forgotten nation, the roma, many of them still victims of the latest balkan wars, living as refugees spread all over europe. slovenia had a narrow escape, but it deeply experienced lack of freedom and safety itself. i hope that slovenia will take it as an honour to host the first annual roma conference. it knows it has many supporters – and maybe, i should say, fans.

- a writer. - mr president, we have also put questions to the commission and i also expect the commissioner to answer them. the main subject of discussion tonight is the commitment of the european union, of the european commission, to tackling the problems of the roma, a large group of whom have been counted among the eu citizens for a number of years and again since last year. they are citizens living in conditions which are, in most cases, unacceptable. we are glad that the european commission will respond to this today. we are also pleased that the european council at the brussels summit has paid attention to the situation of the roma, has asked the commission to come up with more concrete proposals for what the european union itself can do to complement the policies of the member states with large roma populations, what we can do in terms of coordination, the exchange of best practices, and how we can better use the resources available to the european union in those countries through the funds to do something about the situation of the roma. i say this in particular because when a number of countries joined - last year, but also in 2004 - we said: "okay, come on in, one of the things we have to do together is to address the problems of the roma". i myself was rapporteur for slovakia and i remember that the slovak government made all kinds of commitments, but i have some doubts about the effectiveness of the implementation of those commitments. that is and remains an important point. we also say that you cannot regard the roma as a classical national minority, like the hungarians in slovakia or the russians in the baltic states; it is a typical european minority for which a special european policy could also be developed, together with the member states concerned, with its own responsibility for the european union, as also recognized by the statements of the european council in brussels. we ask the commission in particular to ensure greater coordination within the commission itself. how can we work better together? how can we also make someone in the college centrally responsible for roma policy? perhaps we should also consider whether the roma can be recognised as a kind of european minority in order to circumvent the subsidiarity principle which currently applies to the minority policies of the member states. finally, i think that everyone's intentions are good, but that better coordination is needed and more action is also needed.

the importance of the south caucasus as an energy corridor for the european union cannot be underestimated. what else will happen to our goal of energy diversification? we are therefore fully in line with the line drawn by the rapporteur, mrs polfer, in her report. analyst vladimir socor has succinctly formulated this corridor function of the south caucasus: the azerbaijan-georgia-turkey railway will break russia's monopoly on rail traffic to kazakhstan and other central asian states. just as pipelines through the southern caucasus will break russia's monopoly on the transport of oil and gas from central asia. add to this the necessary energy supplies to azerbaijan to carry out the nabucco project. if the european union is to make the most of the southern caucasus as an energy corridor, internal political stability in georgia, armenia and azerbaijan is essential. unfortunately, there is no consensus in the societies of these countries. the european union has a direct task here. the desired political stability is equally true at regional level. the eu can and should call on turkey, the applicant country, to do so, because a military escalation of the nagorno-karabakh conflict is anything but unthinkable in the long term, given the quadrupling of azerbaijan's military capabilities and the increasing external aid that baku is currently receiving in this regard.

the problem of human rights in china is constantly reappearing in these urgent debates. there is always a reason for that. china's economic growth and growing international standing have still not led to a substantially different approach to civil liberties, administrative abuse of power, space for opposition and judicial independence. the council has also adopted a resolution on the situation in the former yugoslavia. china is not the only country that pronounces and executes death sentences, but it is a champion in this regard. the only progress we are seeing now is that more attention is being paid to the certainty of the perpetrator's guilt before execution, so that fewer people are killed who are later found to be innocent. the international attention to the three great dramas in china has now largely disappeared. the 1989 massacre in tiananmen square, the persecution of the falun gong and the changing demographics of tibet and east turkestan are attracting less and less attention. all the attention is now on economic growth, export opportunities to china, cheap import opportunities from china, china's growing role in africa and the upcoming olympic games in china. under these circumstances, the pressure to improve the human rights situation in china is diminishing. the olympics have become an argument for demolishing neighborhoods and driving out the residents. the chinese ambassadors say that their people's national honour is being tarnished by criticism from abroad. the forthcoming olympics could have been a moment of improvement. as it stands, they are good for tourism and for the execution of major public works, but not good for the improvement of human rights. in the netherlands, a debate has now arisen on the possibility of boycotting these olympic games. a resolution on one arrested human rights activist hu jia is necessary, but not enough. in relations between europe and china, it is not economic self-interest that should be paramount, but our concern for human rights in china.
the european parliament has been a very active participant in the process of european integration, and it has been a very active participant in the process of european integration. the democratic republic of the congo, a vast area with a multitude of peoples, did not gradually gain independence during the belgian colonization before 1960 by means of self-government built up over many years. decolonization was not prepared, but became inevitable after britain and france granted independence to their smaller neighbouring colonies in the late 1950s, partly as a result of the wars of liberation in morocco, tunisia, algeria and kenya. since then, the congo has been a constant battleground. the early years were dominated by the struggle between kasavubu from the west, lumumba from the northeast and chombe from the southeast. they each represented different ideologies and interests, but they were above all loved by a limited part of the congolese population. the harsh military dictatorship of the profiteer mobutu has united the country for years in a seeming calm, but the differences have never been bridged. the recent elections have also failed to produce unity or good cooperation. the country remains a battleground with flexible borders, where, as in the 1960s, the issue is the demarcation of ethnic areas and the control of mining areas. foreign companies benefit from this. it now seems to be a question of which people in this multitude of peoples dominates the others. in this form, the future of the congo is without prospects. the horrors to which the resolution refers, including the general lawlessness and the frequent rape of women, are all connected with that chaos. the 2006 law against sexual violence will not solve that problem as long as the struggle continues and many people are homeless. the resolution rightly refers to the role of government representatives, peacekeepers and aid organizations. their people must do everything they can to prevent such atrocities without getting involved themselves. recognition of rape, forced pregnancy, sexual exploitation and other forms of sexual violence as war crimes by the european union and the united nations is a first requirement, but removing the breeding ground for this violence will have to be done on the ground.
the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european parliament is represented in the council. the young soldiers nasser and najib, who overthrew the dysfunctional regime of king farouk more than half a century ago, brought hope of change and progress. nasser gave his people pride and inspiration for a future that would end a backward and humiliated country. his successor sadat offered hope of lasting peace with neighboring israel, after a series of terrible wars. the current president mubarak offers no hope. it has become the symbol of the stagnation. his only merit is the fact that he has organized stability with a heavy hand, which allows him to be seen in the outside world as an ally against terrorism and islamic fundamentalism. but it has no progress and no democratization to offer. opposition is allowed selectively, but it does not have the normal scope of play that is common in a parliamentary democracy. in this way he is breeding a fertile ground for internal strife, both among fundamentalists and among democrats. there is also a growing intolerance of religious minorities, including the christian heads who have long been strong in egypt. on this basis, where problems are not solved but are covered up by force, no lasting stability is created. that is why this critical resolution is justified. the opposition of non-governmental organizations, the intimidation of opponents, the discrimination against minorities, arrests and torture and all the other shortcomings we have noted make us fear the worst for what will follow mubarak's rule. egypt seems to have largely returned to the backwardness of king farouk's time, 60 years ago. european relations with egypt should help to put an end to this situation as far as possible.

president, today we are seeing the impact of europe's climate ambitions. i think the proposals are very well put together, but unfortunately the target is too low. in bali, it was determined that 25-40% less is needed by 2020 to save the climate. and then the european union's commitment of 20% is a little on the thin side. that is a wrong signal to the rest of the world, and furthermore, bulgaria and romania, among others, are allowed to increase their emissions considerably. that's not a good idea, as far as i'm concerned. so there's little reason for china and india to start reducing their emissions. it would be better if the richer countries in the european union helped countries like bulgaria and romania to reduce their emissions. that's a better way to be credible in the world. unfortunately, the renewal of the emissions trading system has been watered down by pressure from the industrial lobby. it is a pity that the commission has fallen on deaf ears and reduced the share of auctioning rights for energy-intensive industries. it is true that some companies operate internationally, but it is much better to counter that with a fair system and border taxes.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0179/97). in the second half of the nineteenth century, european states decided that they themselves should have a monopoly on postal services because private industry was failing. there have always been private companies that tried to evade this measure. they offered cheaper services, but selectively, in the busiest parts of the postal delivery and with poorer working conditions and terms of employment. since the 1990s, a political majority has been seeking to create more and more space for such companies. the decision now proposed gives them almost complete freedom of action. my group expects this to lead to a deterioration in postal services for consumers, a deterioration for workers and additional costs for member states to maintain and restore their universal postal services. even now that the three largest groups have found a compromise on deadlines and accompanying measures, my group regards this as a step backwards. we therefore propose that, in addition to measures to improve the details, such as stricter guarantees for blind customers and for staff, this liberalization should be rejected. this is also in line with the recent rejection of the sale of state-owned companies by the voters in the german city of leipzig.

the credit crisis in the united states has in recent months demonstrated the bankruptcy of a purely capitalist system. we have known for a long time that a communist, or socialist system, as you prefer, does not work. in 1989 this became clear. but now that capitalism has also been shown to be unworkable, it is high time that knowledge was put to good use. the lisbon strategy is an attempt to ensure that europe can remain economically competitive with the rest of the world. this can only be done if everyone who can is actively involved and we take care of a good living environment. the promotion of employment and sustainable development are therefore rightly the main objectives of the renewed lisbon strategy. the solutions proposed by the gue group do not seem to me to be informed and realistic. if we were to take such measures, inflation would rise and investors would lose confidence in investing in europe. and inflation is ultimately theft. in previous debates on the spring break i have already advocated active government in the member states. i'm doing it again today. member states must initiate and implement reforms themselves. and now that there is no recession, it is high time to reform the welfare state adequately. europe can coordinate, but it cannot command.

in writing. − the draft constitution voted down in 2005 in two member states was characterised by its satisfactory reception by big business, national governments and the majority in national parliaments, but its lack of support among voters. in the few countries where referendums have been held, it has been found that intense public debate and high turnout lead to many votes against. the people do not want europe to impose decisions which they themselves would never have wanted to take. they do find europe useful for the peaceful resolution of crossborder problems and for tackling large-scale problems that individual member states cannot solve alone, but not for a europe that increasingly takes the place of the state in which they live. that constitution was a compromise between good and bad things, without the possibility of amending the bad points in the field of economy and armaments. the new treaty still closely resembles that constitution. the rejection of referenda is an expression of fear for the electorate. the adoption today by a large majority of the amended constitution sends a clear message: "people, you stay out of this; this europe is only a matter for professional politicians".

in writing. my group, and also the national parliamentary group of my party, have recently decided to reject the current path towards independence for kosovo. i understand this because at this stage the eu is behaving like a great power that wants to unilaterally impose its will on serbia, a state that has been humiliated many times before, and treats kosovo as its protectorate. this does not mean that i take back what i have said in the past years on this subject. the 1974 yugoslav constitution, which granted kosovo almost as much autonomy as the six constituent republics of the time, might have allowed for the peaceful coexistence of serbs and albanians within a single state. the unilateral abolition of that autonomy in the 1980s made the serbian state useless and even harmful to most of the inhabitants of kosovo. since september 1991, kosovo has been acting as an independent state, with its own president, parliament and education system. if that state, together with the other successor states of yugoslavia, had been recognised by the outside world in time, there would have been no violent kla, the inhabitants of kosovo would not have been condemned to accumulate criminal sources of income and there would have been no reason for the 1999 war.
i have read with interest the reports by mr kallenbach and mr guellec. the convergence of countries in europe remains an interesting theme. internal policy integration and cooperation within and between eu member states and regions is taking shape. the fact that such a strategy is working is clearly stated in the cohesion report. convergence between member states has increased. at the same time, however, it is also reported that convergence between regions within and between member states is declining, particularly between urban and non-urban regions. in my view, the national, regional and local authorities have a key role to play in such situations. i do not understand why the option of member states making more of their own contribution to regional development is rejected in advance. it is too early to say whether the present policy should be continued for many years to come. it is clear that it can meet certain challenges. but let us also see if it can solve the divergence mentioned, i.e. convergence not only at member state level but also at regional level, between and within member states. if not, a different approach with a greater role for member states is desirable. no one knows the national and regional situation better than the individual member states. they can propose solutions that make cohesion policy objectives achievable also at sub-national level.
madam president, madam hübner, the unique and special nature of the fourth cohesion report is that it not only gives a good evaluation of the policies pursued but also looks ahead and tackles new challenges for europe. it is about globalisation and its effects, about climate change, about energy issues and the demographic challenges. it is just as well that we are doing this now, also in preparation for the spring summit in march this year. mr guellec has done that in a very good way. he said that the essence of cohesion policy was not changing - it was also emphatically reaffirmed in the treaty - but that the content of regional action and programmes was changing. this week we also looked at the lisbon strategy and what strikes me is that we are going strong and making a specific contribution to it by committing, by way of appropriation, more than 60-65% of the cohesion money. this shows how much cohesion policy is at the heart of this challenge and the new circumstances. it is also important, as the kallenbach report has pointed out, that we make territorial cohesion a core task of the european union. globalisation is concentrated in a number of top regions, metropolitan areas, and europe's response is to provide opportunities in the transfer of knowledge and the creation of conditions for entrepreneurship for all regions, including rural areas. that's the new schedule. the epp also opposes a renationalisation response to the 2013 debate. the point is that we must establish a new link between r & d, innovation policy, the internal market and the union's instruments and funds, in order to link the knowledge economy - because that is what is at stake in the long term - to jobs and prosperity in our regions. i also welcome the transparency initiative taken by mrs hübner. the citizens must know how close we are to them with this policy.

new place to live. in this area everything is in motion and the point is that this movement should cause as little violence and other atrocities as possible. warlords who gain prestige, power, and wealth by perpetuating conflict add to existing problems and make them more difficult to solve. very recently, on 17 january, we discussed the situation in the congo and adopted a resolution expressing the wish that all the atrocities should cease. i fear that we may adopt many more resolutions on this subject without coming any closer to a solution. anyone who thought that last year's elections would solve the problems in the congo was deceived. the incumbent president, kabila, has won but he represents different views from the movement from which he emerged and the results of both the presidential and parliamentary elections were disputed for the opposition. the question is whether such a large country with poor transport links and a very wide variety of peoples is capable of functioning in a way that is supported by all the different groups of the population and all the different political forces. now that this seems to be unsuccessful, congo is becoming a place where life-threatening diseases are rampant, where the exploitation of nature and landscape is rampant, and where people are left with no rights at all. the question is whether the ceasefire of 23 january for north and south kivu, which is based on the disarmament of the warring groups and the return of refugees to their homes, is feasible and whether the action of the united nations on the ground can contribute to this. this is not achieved by taking sides in conflicts but possibly by persuading the warring parties to refrain from sexual violence and to allow doctors, for example.
for the ind/dem group, by mr de gucht and others it is the primary duty of every state to ensure the safety of its citizens, and this is in full accordance with international law. that explains and legitimizes israel's response to the increasing rocket fire from the gaza strip. this palestinian missile threat dates back to 2001. however, it has increased dramatically since the israeli withdrawal from gaza in 2005. since then, the number of palestinian rocket attacks on the jewish state has increased by as much as 500%. before 2006, there were rarely more than 50 rocket attacks on israeli territory every month. by early 2008, that number had grown to 50 a day! more than 250,000 israeli civilians are thus exposed to palestinian rockets from the gaza strip on a daily basis. the accusation that israel is responding to this deadly threat with disproportionate force ignores once again the cruel tactics of hamas and other palestinian terrorist organizations to launch rocket attacks from populated areas. at the end of february the dutch press reported extensively on this subject under the telling headline - a statement by a palestinian from the gaza strip - "please do not fire rockets at israel here". and incidentally, israel follows strict legal standards for military procedures. an example to be followed, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, for the arab world. meanwhile, hamas, the most dominant power factor in gaza as a member of the so-called axis of destabilization in the region, can count on substantial support from iran, syria and hezbollah. an anonymous hamas commander emphasized this abundantly to the sunday times yesterday. if iran can smuggle its fajr missiles into gaza, hamas can even threaten tel aviv. do the european institutions share this israeli concern and, if so, what is the european strategy in the face of this axis of destabilization? that is what is important for the stabilization of the middle east, for any prospect of peace.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, i wish to begin by expressing my understanding of the frustration of the czech republic and other non-visa-waiver countries. however, it would be highly destructive if the czechs were to go it alone, and we should not fall for us ‘divide-and-rule’ tactics. i hope that the commission and the council have learned an important lesson: that a proper mandate and approval of the european parliament is not an obstacle for negotiations but, rather, strengthens the position of the european union. the us should act as a loyal and reliable ally and respect eu law. concerning pnr, i wonder why it should be included in the memorandum of understanding at all if it is already covered by the eu-us agreement. the ink on the eu-us pnr agreement is not even dry yet, and not all member states have ratified it, so what else is it that the americans want? has the commission been able to establish whether the implementing measures of the memorandum of understanding are in line with the eu-us agreement and, if not, can we then assume that the agreement has become invalid? because, if that is the case, then the agreement no longer exists and we should engage in new talks, and i then assume that there will be codecision for the european parliament. with hindsight, it is even more regrettable that the council and the commission have accepted the eu-us pnr agreement on such vague and open terms, because it leaves much room for the us to further widen the scope of the eu-us pnr agreement. finally, i think the eu will only have a proper position vis-à-vis the united states if we speak with one voice, because if we do not – if we are divided – then our rules are made in washington and not here or in brussels.
the americans' divide and rule strategy in 2008 is the starting point in transatlantic relations. however, first of all it must be clear that we are ultimately doing it to ourselves. this time, the czechs were the first to be approached and succumbed. last thursday the commission was unable to confirm or deny that there was a breach of european law and regulation. how weird is that? when will the member states realize that each of them is more or less a voice crying out in the desert? in political relations with the rest of the world, and therefore also with the united states, it is really crucial that member states speak with one voice. the transatlantic relationship is undeniably of great importance, but no eu member state - no matter how loyal - will ever become the 51st state of the united states. this has got to stop. europe is on the move. and quite frankly, i am very angry that the council is not represented in this house at the moment. this is an absolute lack of sense of urgency.

i would have liked to have had mr frattini sitting here with his colleague neelie-kroes. i think the strict distinction between market rules and privacy rules is outdated. we know that approval of the google/doubleclick merger is coming and of course the european parliament does not want to interfere in the details of that merger, but we do want to know what guarantees there are for privacy. personal data has become big business. information on customers, users and their behaviour and preferences is essential for the competitive position of companies. the protection of personal data can therefore no longer be considered as separate from competition policy. the commission has adopted a very traditional approach to competition, which is no longer suited to the behavioural advertising of the 21st century. competition must include safeguards for the protection of privacy and consumer rights if mergers lead to mega-companies with a lot of information about users, such as google/doubleclick, but also a possible microsoft/yahoo, or yahoo/rupert murdoch, or reed elsevier/choicepoint, etc. personal data can be misused to keep out new entrants, and the point is that in a healthy competition, you can enforce privacy on the consumer, you can discipline a company, as in the case of facebook, for example. we have rules about media concentration, too. why then do we not include the protection of privacy in competition policy? ip addresses may be considered as personal data. and that has potentially far-reaching implications for the industry, but also for the user. the european, but above all global, standards for this sector are therefore urgently needed. the european union must take the lead and develop things together with america, in consultation with industry. so i propose that we put this on the agenda of the transatlantic economic council. finally, companies also have an interest in the public's confidence in good privacy protection. i just mentioned the case of facebook, where consumers have used their power to force facebook to pay more attention to privacy. i would therefore like to call for a different approach by the european commission, combining competition policy, consumer protection and the protection of personal data or privacy.

president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, adaptation to climate change needs to be given much more attention very quickly, because whatever we do to combat climate change, it requires investment to adapt societies to rising sea levels, more water in rivers, or less water and drought. all of this has implications for safety, water management, soil, agriculture and, of course, public health. some areas should prepare for severe drought and increased heat. irrigation systems, but also clean desalination plants are the solutions. other areas are facing waterlogging and need to invest in raising dikes. it is up to the member states themselves to plan and analyse the effects of climate change and prepare for them. but three points are important. first of all, solidarity is really needed; some countries are being dragged along by others, and some countries have more capacity than others. it goes without saying that there is also a shared responsibility within the european union. secondly, it means that there must also be a common source of funding. we must look for sources of funding and it is obvious to look at the structural funds, but also at the proceeds of the ets auctions. thirdly, solidarity must of course also apply to developing countries. many african and asian countries have contributed little to climate change themselves, but are experiencing its harms. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them.

respectively. this reality naturally justifies much more cooperation, consultation and perhaps common strategies between china and europe about africa, but with africa. you said it yourself, i share that view. so i would like to talk to you today about what i think are promising areas to engage china and our african partners in trilateral cooperation. i would also like to share with you my doubts and questions on other points and, finally, to inform you of the action i intend to take on your report. first, africa is living in a time of global economy and globalization. it is increasingly asserting its role as an international and global actor, and is mobilizing on major global issues. it has developed a vast network of partnerships around the world with a strong desire to rebuild its relations. to tell the truth, she now puts, sometimes awkwardly, by the way, but she's right to do so, she now puts the partners in competition. it is a reality, and i would even say that it is fair and legitimate, and it implies that its partners finally adopt a modern, confident and loyal attitude. this new approach, i believe that the european union and china are probably ready to implement it and are eager to do so, perhaps for different reasons, but clearly they are ready to implement it. we are both major partners of africa. our african policy is certainly different in terms of principles and values, but on the ground it is possible to identify a large number of shared interests and points of convergence. for example, we have already started a dialogue on peace, stability and sustainable development in africa. but we must, of course, go further with the important support of our african friends, who must play a leading role in this dialogue. this explains the trilateral approach i proposed last year, which you seem to share in a large measure, particularly when you propose the establishment of a permanent platform. in this context, you rightly stress the importance of equality and mutual respect which must be the basis of this trilateral partnership. and here, at the risk of obviously opening up a controversy, europe must understand that it is not necessarily very effective, at the present stage, and in the face of a partner like china, to set itself up as master by pretending to dictate our conditions unilaterally. we must therefore avoid all generalizations in order to create a dynamic of dialogue and i believe that once we have opened or succeeded in opening the dynamic of dialogue with china, we will be able, through this dialogue, to address the issues of the rule of law, democracy, governance, in other words, everything that in some way underpins the values of europe and all the sources of inspiration for our policies. i agree with you that we must also adopt a european policy which is more effective and perhaps even faster, because that is the great advantage the chinese have over us. it is also more attractive, more in line with the real priorities of our african partners. i just want to give you an example. we are literally powerless, as european institutions, to implement programmes or aid, or interventions in all post-conflict situations. we have had the case, for example, of liberia, i give you this case, where we have a government that came out of legitimate elections, with a president who has a real agenda and who wants change. where are we in the efficiency that will be required to bring this country to show that there are dividends of peace, stability and democracy? nowhere! i say this because i suffer from it every day. this modernisation of european policy towards africa has been my priority since the beginning of my mandate and i believe i can say that we have already made a lot of progress, in particular with the establishment of the strategic partnerships resulting from the lisbon summit, but there is still, of course, a great deal to be done. the reduction of poverty remains the main objective of our development policy, which must be achieved through wealth creation and economic growth. this economic growth, china is living it every day and this experience can, of course, be of benefit to african countries. but of course, any economic growth must be accompanied by an active redistributive policy if it is to be effective in fighting poverty. i have often had the opportunity to say that growth does not guarantee development. we need redistribution, and redistribution, and we need a strong state for that. we also need a vigilant society, we need a free press, we need all of that. i think that the european union and china have a real role to play in this area, but it is a process which will take time and require a lot of effort from both sides. i would like to share with you some of the questions i have. is it, for example, really realistic, i do not know, is it constructive, is it useful, is it even desirable, to begin this cooperation by strongly demanding that china adopt the guidelines and standards set by the cap? i doubt that. if it works out, it's better, but i doubt it. i do not think that the africans are particularly keen to see china go down this road precisely because they are looking for alternatives to how to do development aid in europe. the chinese aid is appreciated precisely because it is faster than european aid, because it does not impose so many constraints that ultimately reduce the political space of africans because, in the background, there is still something. there must be some kind of misunderstanding. very often we hear, "you europeans impose conditions!" that's not true, man. our cooperation, the means we spend, are not bound by political conditions. nature, the modulation of our aid, yes. for budget support, for example, if there is no good governance, there will be no budget support. in a country in conflict, we will not give budgetary aid. but that doesn't stop us, even when we disagree with the power in place or the regime, from doing development aid or doing humanitarian aid. what is fundamentally different, there is a confusion, is that, with the chinese, the responsiveness, the "disbursement" goes much faster than us, with our procedures, which have made my life almost impossible for almost 4 years. that is the reality, that is where the confusion and misunderstanding lie, and i think that also needs to be said. i agree with your analysis, of course. it is important to see chinese aid gradually aligned with african national priorities, with their national systems, preferably in coordination with other partners on the ground. the fact remains that it is premature, and perhaps even counterproductive, to try to force things along this line today, and above all to try to do so unilaterally. this is a long-term objective, but for the time being i can only see three areas where our cooperation and dialogue could be concentrated. one, the establishment of an open and constructive dialogue between us, long-standing donors, and china on africa. second, we can exchange chinese and african development experiences with our own to advance together the development agenda. a concrete example: china has developed a lot of programs that use photovoltaics, for example. it is clear that on a continent like africa, there is an extraordinary area of trilateral cooperation, here is an example. i spoke to the minister of commerce, who i met at a meeting in tonga, and he's very open about it. we shall discuss this again in beijing, because when i leave you i shall go to beijing, so we shall discuss these questions again. thirdly, to respect our commitments to the paris agenda. it is all about harmonizing our rules, and it is all about the division of labour and i call on parliament to help us, to push our member states to make their actions match their speeches. all the development ministers, all the european prime ministers, all of them, without exception, agree that it is better to divide up the work, that more coordination is needed to be consistent, and so on. yeah, but when you offer them real division of labor strategies, they're absent-minded followers. i hear that too, because it's a reality. as you will have understood, my objective is not just to bring china into the cap, i go far beyond that by first seeking to find common ground with the multiple chinese actors in africa, including the government, in order to promote the development of africa. we europeans and chinese must, of course, start from the african agenda and contribute our respective contributions and advantages, without having to derogate from the values and principles which have ensured stability, peace and prosperity in europe for the last 50 years. this does not make me a dreamer or a naïve, but on the contrary, it is important to recognize, as you do, that china's engagement raises questions and sometimes concerns in africa itself. i too am sometimes concerned, of course, about the way in which cooperation with china is developing, but that is precisely why i am advocating dialogue. it is clear that china pursues its economic, diplomatic and strategic interests in africa. europe too often makes the grave mistake of not admitting that it is tempted by the same thing and, rather than hiding and camouflaging itself behind discourses, sometimes very hypocritical, it would be much better to affirm that africa is a continent with potential, that it is a privileged continent in its relations with europe and that we have common interests to discover, generate and forge. and i think that would be the win-win strategy, because that's the only way that we're going to get out of the relationship, which to me is very negative, of the recipient to the donor, because it's always marked by humiliation, by contempt, and it's always marked by a sense of dependence on the other, that is, the weaker versus the stronger. i have already said that i will be in beijing the day after tomorrow and i will try, mrs gomez, to give a tentative start to a positive interpretation of your very, very good report.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on foreign affairs. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's external relations are not affected by the crisis. the chinese authorities should respect the principles of democracy, good governance and human rights in their relations with africa. the foreign affairs committee therefore regrets china's purely pragmatic cooperation with the repressive regimes in africa. the people's republic of china's commercial presence on african soil has environmental effects which china must deal with responsibly. the reports in the western press are sending out alarming signals about the exploitation of nature, particularly in the congo delta. and these signals range from oil exploitation and overfishing to deforestation, notably in an official nature reserve. the committee on foreign affairs also regrets that beijing is excluding a number of african states from the china-africa strategic partnership simply because of their diplomatic contacts with taiwan. this chinese attitude is not only contrary to its own principle of non-interference in the internal affairs of third countries, but also in conflict with the current rapprochement between taiwan and beijing.
