rapporteur. − mr president, the oral amendment by mr landsbergis obliges me to explain the complicated situation concerning a name. ‘fyrom’ has never been the name of any country but can only be an abbreviation of ‘former yugoslav republic of macedonia’. in my previous report, adopted by the plenary meeting of this parliament on 12 july 2007, it was decided not to introduce the use of this abbreviation as a name but only to use those five words. the conclusion was to replace all other terms included in amendments by ‘former yugoslav republic of macedonia’. i propose to maintain this line. the oral amendment proposed by mr landsbergis is not in line with this decision. i expect that during 2008 an agreement with greece will solve the different views on the names of this state and that then the term fyrom will be forgotten forever.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, let us be frank and open: as frontex is a coordinating agency, we cannot expect it to stop the influx of irregular migrants in itself and of itself, nor to put an end to the trafficking in human beings. frontex is not, and never will be, a panacea to all the problems caused by irregular migration. at the very end it is simple: eu border security is the ultimate responsibility of all member states, and border controls must be operated in a spirit of sharing responsibilities and solidarity between member states. over the past years, we have been facing a genuine paradox. one could hear member states inviting frontex to stop the flow of irregular migrants immediately and to deploy as much equipment as possible. however, the centralised record of available technical equipment, as you know, is just a virtual record. the assets on this list belong to the member states and they are subject to their will to deploy them. clearly, we could debate the will of a number of member states. as for the financial perspective, if frontex were to deploy all the equipment at once, its budget would evaporate immediately. therefore, the main question still to be answered – and i am addressing the council – is whether the council is ready to extend the principle of compulsory solidarity, obliging all member states to deliver, i.e. a mandatory solidarity mechanism. over the past years, member states have repeatedly reaffirmed the importance of ensuring full respect for international human rights obligations in managing migration. member states underlined the importance of european solidarity in burden-sharing as the founding principles. yet, too often, effective decision-making is hamstrung by the inability of these member states to work together in their mutual interest. would the president-in-office agree that the time for words is passed and the time for action has come? would you not agree that in a world marked by increasing regional conflicts and a growing gap between rich and poor, mobility will increase rather than diminish? is it not high time that the member states put themselves in a position to respond to that challenge with a radical, yet responsible, revision of their views on what is supposed to be a comprehensive package on a common – and i repeat, common – migration policy? unfortunately, i have to leave the chamber now, as i am taking part in the ongoing high-level trialogue on the return directive. however, i look forward to receiving your detailed reply at your earliest convenience.
president, mr president-in-office, commissioner, two weeks ago i represented the committee on the environment and the committee on climate change at the informal environment council in slovenia in the autumn. two issues were central: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council should be consulted on the future of the union's external action. most of the emphasis was on biodiversity, biomass and biofuels in relation to forest biodiversity. the possibilities of forest biomass for energy supply, in particular for second generation biofuels, and sustainability criteria for the use of forest biomass were discussed. it was a successful conference, thanks to the slovenian chairman, minister podobnic. we see that deforestation and illegal logging threaten many species with extinction. they are experiencing a profound change in their environment and as a result cannot survive. forests provide a very varied variety of both plants and animals. it is therefore essential, in the context of the protection of biodiversity, to halt deforestation wherever possible. this applies not only to countries outside the european union, but also to the european member states. good forest management should be promoted, especially since forests are also very valuable in other areas. deforestation also leads to serious soil erosion, especially in mountainous areas, and to a disruption of water management, which further seriously affects biodiversity. global warming can also affect the diversity of species. large shifts are taking place in species ranges, which are putting some species at serious risk, particularly in the northern regions. forestry in tropical regions appears to be only exacerbating warming, partly by greatly reducing the capacity to store co2. however, studies published in recent years in nature and science, among others, show that increasing forest cover also leads to higher emissions of methane, a greenhouse gas 23 times more potent than co2. as the temperature rises and the sun shines more, more methane is emitted. the emissions of methane are much higher in tropical areas. additional forest planting leads to additional co2 capture capacity, but some of that co2 capture is thus offset by an increase in methane emissions. nevertheless, the balance remains positive, especially in the non-tropical regions. finally, the negative effects on biodiversity of the factors mentioned are unfortunately exacerbated by the current production of biofuels, which is destroying mainly tropical rainforests. the species-rich forests are being replaced by plantations with low species cover, which also have negative effects on greenhouse gas emissions, absorption capacity and food prices. i am glad that this is being brought up in this resolution. we must also be vigilant about second-generation biofuels.
i would like to say a great deal, mr president, about my ideas on the reform of the wto, but instead i would like to put a number of specific questions to the commission which i would like to have answered. these questions illustrate the points that we need to take into account in the debate on wto reform alongside the procedural reforms already mentioned, such as transparency. in the context of climate change, there is an increasing emphasis on the use of biofuels. i do not want to go into the debate whether this is good or bad, but i do want to raise the following questions with regard to the european union's obligations under the wto. the eu's climate change package includes a number of environmental criteria that biofuels should meet. however, i would like to see, and many of my colleagues would like to see, that social criteria are also set out, such as minimum wages and the prevention of child labour. is this compatible with the wto requirements? if not, are we not forced into a position of taking child labor and underpayment for granted? another question about the trade in gmo food. i believe that the cartagena protocol gives the member states a free choice about the authorization of gmos. is this compatible with the wto and, if not, how are you going to solve it? parliament has indicated that it is in the majority against the seal fur trade. canada is now threatening to challenge this through the wto. how do you think you can maintain the wish of the majority of the eu population in this area? i think these questions are very important and should be taken into account in the reform.
the european parliament has already expressed its concern about the situation in the developing countries, and the european union has also expressed its concern about the situation in the developing countries. now we're back here tonight, and we're talking about genetically modified animal feed. it might have been a good idea to combine the two debates. this rather technical subject of animal feed is of course part of the wider debate on food security and rising food prices. how justified is it to destroy or refuse shipments of animal feed because of the unintentional presence of traces, very small quantities, of genetically modified product? i do not think that is very reasonable, either in view of the food security i mentioned or in view of rising prices. because feed prices have been rising a lot lately, and they will do so even more if we continue to maintain this attitude. many producers are only seeing their costs rise as a result, and i think it is worth stressing once again that the price increases we keep talking about do not automatically mean that farmers will also receive a better income. as has already been said, for example in the pig sector the cost of feed is increasing and the margins are actually only getting smaller. how can we get out of this impasse? not by suddenly being careless about admission procedures. no, i'm sure it wasn't. but i think there are two points which are not quite right, and we must find a solution to them. first of all, as struan stevenson has already said, it takes considerably longer in the european union before a gmo can be authorized for animal feed: two and a half years with us, over a year in the us. i have heard the commission talk about making the efsa more efficient, but that is not the whole problem, of course. the problem is also the ping-pong in the council committees, which can neither find a qualified majority for approval nor a qualified majority against approval, and then finally, after much deliberation, the decision is taken by the commission. so that's part of the problem, and that procedure needs to be speeded up. this is certainly not an argument for automatic authorization of products, but i think people have a right to know sooner whether a product can be authorized for the european market. secondly, we will have to find a solution to the unintentional presence of genetically modified traces in animal feed, for example through a threshold value, especially in the case of gmos which have already received a positive assessment from the efsa. and especially when you consider that the carbonates of the pigs that have eaten that feed outside the european union are just sitting in the store with us. speaking of unfair competition! i regret that i have not heard the commission on thresholds. she's referring to existing legislation. but let's face it, we do use a threshold in labeling, so i think it's possible. in short, do not let producers and consumers suffer because of the indecision of the council committees. now is the time to provide clarity and speed.
i would like to thank the committee on agriculture and the house for following my initiative to discuss this matter here tonight. one of the strange things is that the cultivation of genetically modified crops is increasing all over the world, but only we in europe have a tremendous fear of the cold water on that point. the big question is how. the commissioner says with great conviction: the legislation is the law, and we must abide by it. but if the changed circumstances require that the legislation be changed, then the commission will have to do it anyway. i think the time is right. i asked about it recently, but one genetically modified crop is not the other. there's a big difference between transgenic and cisgenic: one is a species and the other is not. both are covered by the same legislation and the commission should take the necessary steps to change the legislation in this respect. the essence of the whole story, i think, is zero tolerance. there are few situations in the world where you can demand absolute zero tolerance. there must always be a way to leave a certain margin of difference. if i'm speeding for a distance of 30 miles, in most countries there's a tolerance of about two miles before i get a ticket. why is this not possible for the import of goods? why is a tolerance of, say, 0.8 or 0.9% not possible? can the commissioner give me a clear answer to that? i too welcome the fact that there is a discussion going on with the efsa to shorten the procedures without compromising quality. but have the efsa indicated whether or not this is possible? the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's environmental policy is not distorted by the use of genetically modified organisms. we are not going to allow such gmos - i call them artificial food - simply because it is in the economic interest. not as far as i'm concerned. i really put public health, the environment and biodiversity first. brussels is already obliging countries to allow certain gmos, even if they are not willing to do so. this is contrary to the cartagena protocol, which allows countries to refuse new gmo products if they have doubts about their safety. let the member states decide which risk products they allow. i'm not alone in my protest. gmos are causing a stir among large groups of citizens. we see this in poland, in romania, in austria and in cyprus. in the netherlands, for example, there is growing opposition to monsanto's test fields in places like raalte and gemert-bakel. the risk is real that monsanto's engineered seeds will spread to nearby fields of conventional or organic farmers, and that's when these farmers never want the weeds. i am also concerned about monsanto's plans to take over the dutch seed company de ruiter, one of the world's top 10 seed companies. it is clear that monsanto wants to increase its infiltration in europe. either in anticipation of more flexible rules, or to put pressure on politicians to relax those rules very quickly. we must oppose that vigorously. we are more concerned with public health, the environment and biodiversity than with corporate profits.
madam president, when the commissioner answers, could she say something about the possibilities of increasing the tolerance level from zero to a higher percentage, say 0.7%, 0.9% or whatever it is? from listening to the debate, i think that is the essence of what has been said. zero tolerance in the present circumstances is a bit too stringent.
i would like to thank mrs anne ferreira for her report on market-compliant instruments for environmental objectives. one of the pillars of the present proposal is the polluter pays principle. this is an important starting point because it allows for a fair distribution of the burden. that is one reason why i am in favour of a full accounting of all external costs, including environmental costs, in manufactured and consumer goods. this creates a realistic picture and allows for fair competition. last november, i attended the beyond gdp conference. it has spoken at length about the possibilities of using indicators other than gross domestic product alone to measure the prosperity of society. in practice, social and environmental factors play a role alongside economic ones. you may be financially strapped, but when you live in a crowded city with an excess of air pollution, the level of prosperity is still limited. i am glad that this is also mentioned as a point of concern in mrs ferreira's report. finally, i would like to express my support for two specific articles in mrs ferreira's report, articles 58 and 59. the commission has also proposed that the community should be able to provide the community with a financial contribution to the development of the water sector.
i would like to congratulate mr radwan on the completion of this thorough report. it was negotiated until the last minute. it has become a clear, but sometimes critical, report. the rapporteur is convinced that democratic control of the iasb needs to be improved and i am glad that the iasb is heeding these criticisms. the chairman of the board of trustees, mr gerrit zalm, recently expressed his openness to this in the european parliament and indicated that he would prepare proposals for the adjustment of the structure. the iasb is working on ifrs standards for small and medium-sized enterprises. i agree with the rapporteur that the accounting standards under ifrs are too complicated and too costly for smes. i also believe that encouraging the voluntary use of ifrs by smes would risk a disguised european introduction. i think that a distinction should be made between the size of the enterprise and the size of the undertaking. for large multinationals and banks and insurance companies operating worldwide, it is good to have one standard for annual accounts. it is better for small and mediumsized enterprises in europe to maintain their own standards. the iasb has achieved a great deal with regard to international listed companies and the accounting standard for them. if smes now indicate that costs and effort can be achieved equally well with several standards, then this should not be imposed from above with one standard. the interests of investors and the need for transparency must be taken into account. politics is there to facilitate good, transparent and cost-effective information on the performance of business. i think the benefits that ifrs has had for european equity markets are globally applicable, especially when the united states switches from their system of us-gaap to ifrs. the global investor and shareholder have a strong interest in this in terms of comparability. the idea behind the ifrs therefore deserves the support of the european parliament.
i would like to join in congratulating mr radwan, mr president, if only because his report has enabled us to have a very interesting discussion on the tension between legislation and self-regulation, especially in a global context. i realize the gap we're in with our desires. on the one hand we want these global standards to be applied worldwide, but on the other hand we claim our responsibility as co-legislators to be able to assess the content of these standards and to play our role as co-legislators seriously. i think we have shown in recent times that we do that as a european parliament. with the warm plea that i also made there for the xbrl standard. you know that the scc in america is also going to make a decision one of these days to make it compulsory. i would like to ask you how you are going to deal with this in europe and encourage you to draw up a road map for europe in this area as well.
president, zimbabwe's president mugabe was once the hero of liberation. his actions led to the creation of a new state, zimbabwe, in the former southern rhodesia, after british colonial rule and a white apartheid regime that rebelled against that colonial authority, where the black majority of the population was democratically given the upper hand. as ruler, mugabe behaved in an unlikely moderate manner for years. he neglected the urgently needed redistribution of the landed estates. the only radical thing about him was his dislike of homosexuals. only when he was in danger of losing power did he remember his earlier promises of land reform. since then, he has completely disrupted the economy of his country through ill-considered measures. the redistribution of agricultural land did not take place in a planned and gradual manner, but became a matter of complete arbitrariness, with its most aggressive supporters appropriating the land. worse is his dealings with the opposition. this opposition has been constantly intimidated and attacked, but has managed to hold out well so far. in previous elections, there were suspicions that the results had been falsified. this time the opposition won the parliamentary elections, but the announced recount seems to be designed to deprive them of that victory. the results of the presidential election have been kept secret for a month, so that it remains unknown how big the defeat for mugabe was. in recent days he has declared with enthusiasm that nothing will ever change in his country. he can only achieve that purpose by violence and oppression. the import of a shipload of chinese weapons seems to be the way to do that. under these circumstances, it is reasonable for the outside world to interfere in this internal conflict. support for refugees, support for the opposition and pressure on the ruling party are indispensable tools to achieve what mugabe himself once wanted, namely political control by the majority of the population.
the european parliament has already expressed its views on the situation in iran and the situation in the middle east. freedom, democracy and human rights do not apply in that country. elections are held, but the candidates must be approved by the conservative clergy, so that opposers are excluded in advance. many iranians have fled the reign of terror in their country and without that flight, a large part of them would not be alive today. in europe, we must give these refugees space instead of sending them back or treating them like terrorists. at the same time, it is undeniable that a part of the iranian population supports the killing of homosexuals, discrimination against women and the oppression of ethnic minorities. that part of the horror of western decadence in their country at the time of the late 1970s drove out the pahlavi dynasty, which had turned iran into an extension of america and europe. democracy was not yet established, and opponents were repressed or exiled. the mistakes made by the west during that period have contributed greatly to the rise to power of the present reign of terror. if the domestic modernization of iran under prime minister mossadegh had been given a fair chance in the early 1950s, there would have been no breeding ground for the current rulers. in iran, there are movements for change, but they are mostly hindered. ms. khadijeh moghaddam was arrested on 8 april and charged with anti-state propaganda, actions against national security and misleading public opinion. she has since been released, but the severe restrictions on women continue. our solidarity must be on the side of the oppressed.
the former colonies of french west africa and french central africa were decolonised in the years 1958-60. this was not the result of a long struggle and a collective national consciousness that arose from it, but rather a means of preventing the emergence of liberation movements. it happened from the top instead of from the bottom. from being french overseas departments, they became first self-governing territories and then independent states. nevertheless, in these states, with the exception of guinea-conakry, french influence has always remained much greater than in other former colonies. in the great desert state of chad, france has maintained a military presence, and that has certainly influenced who is allowed to govern that country and who is not. this great influence from europe has not led to the development of european standards of democracy and human rights in these countries. the national borders and the states are not the result of internal movements which have shaped their own state, but only of a colonial past. this means that ethnic differences can lead to ethnic conflicts. in such countries, this leads to a constant struggle for power, for the question of who should be the first-class citizens and who the second-class citizens. the ethnic and geographical diversity is often insufficient or not even reflected in the composition of government. the problems which arise from this cannot, in the opinion of my group, be solved simply by stationing a new european military force there. such military power has always existed and has contributed to the present situation rather than preventing it. the solution does not begin with a stabilisation of the present situation, which is only in the interests of the present rulers, but with the creation of a space for change from below.

madam president, although i am aware of the risk of overburdening the agenda of the transatlantic economic council, i would still call for the insertion of the issue of data protection in the agenda, because data protection – contrary to what many people think – is very much an economic issue. it actually used to be the responsibility of dg internal market in the commission, but that has since changed. personal data has become big business, and it is a growing business. we are currently witnessing, for example, some multibillion mergers in the sector. think of google/double click, which we discussed back in january; the merger between microsoft and yahoo!, which has now been called off; and, shortly, reed elsevier and choicepoint. that indicates the importance of personal data. the business is also global in nature. businesses are increasingly confronted with different legal regimes across the world, or even within europe or within the united states – for example: rules on breach notification, data protection, profiling and behavioural advertising. businesses and citizens need global rules for more legal certainty and transparency. therefore we should start elaborating global standards. i think that the transatlantic economic council would be the appropriate platform for doing this and, therefore, i would like to hear whether commissioner verheugen agrees with me that this issue should be included in the agenda for the tec.
after tonight, it is unlikely that a woman will be elected president in the united states, but whoever becomes president, trade will continue to play an important role in the relationship with the united states. we are each other's largest trading partners and therefore good economic cooperation is of great importance. i therefore congratulate commissioner verheugen on his approach. we have high hopes for this transatlantic economic council, although we must realize that it will be a long-term investment. but this council must play an important role in harmonizing regulations, removing bureaucracy and barriers to trade. because it will make it easier for our companies to do business in the united states and our markets will be more closely connected. and, commissioner, please give priority to what erika mann so beautifully called 'those crazy measures', for which we do not have such a nice word in english. the "crazy measures" include the 100% container scan. it should be withdrawn, because it is a nonsensical measure which will have a very bad effect and will cost far too much. president. - we must also act together with the united states when it comes to our interests in the rest of the world. i would also like to mention the fight against unsafe toys from china and the production of particulate matter in asia. because when it comes to those particles, it's like moping with the faucet open. we're not really able to reduce the current. i would also like to call for special attention to be paid to the need to act together in the current financial crisis. the transatlantic economic council must play a positive role in this area too. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. if we go up together, we can strengthen each other; we will also be working towards that.

i should like to begin by thanking mr mavrommatis for his excellent report and for the good cooperation. i have just spoken as rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on employment and social affairs, and i am now speaking as shadow rapporteur for the press. i would like to stress three points, namely the importance of clear guidelines, the importance of lotteries and home grown players. first of all, i would like to look at the specificity of sport and sporting organisations which call for clear guidelines where european legislation applies. i think we should give an answer to that, rather than the case-by-case approach that has been used so far. second, the home-grown players. we must ensure that professional players from a young age are well protected and trained in a stable environment. that is why we support the so-called home-grown players rule, so that young people can train at the club and get their first contract at the same club. naturally, this will ensure that many national players can play in a club without us discriminating on the basis of nationality, which is not the intention. we must respect freedom of movement, freedom of players, and that is an important principle for us. that is why we do not agree with the fifa rule of the so-called six plus five rule. lastly, the lotteries. state lotteries have an important function. you could say that it is a service of general interest because it supports a lot of social organizations, including the sports structure. the liberalization of the market has major implications, so we must first look at the consequences and what good alternatives are available before taking any action.

in writing. - the problem of lobbyists will not be solved by half-measures. only full transparency and mandatory registration can limit the growing influence of industry lobbyists. that is very necessary, because all too often we see colleagues table amendments which are taken literally from a lobbyist. but politicians should be influenced first and foremost by their electorate, their constituency and their common sense, not by an army of professional lobbyists. i therefore call on the european commission to come up with a package of far more far-reaching measures.
in writing. − eu policy is strongly shaped by the interests of large companies, which seek to strengthen their international competitiveness by cutting costs in the areas of environment, labour, safety and consumer protection. it is not surprising that these companies do this, but if they do it should be obvious to everyone. this gives the trade union movement, the environmental movement and the consumer organizations the opportunity to do something about it. for years, i have been calling for the strictest possible transparency regime for lobbyists, including in questions to the european commission. i note that the hard version has not been adopted today. the ten amendments tabled by the united left and the green group to this effect did not obtain a majority. these included a single common register of all lobbyists granted access to eu institutions, openness about lobbyists' purpose and funding, early introduction in 2009, disclosure of unethical lobbying behaviour, clarification of special advisers to the european commission and the prevention of paid lobbying by civil servants during their leave of absence. despite the rejection of this better option, today's decision is a step forward. on this basis, the two representatives of the socialist party in the netherlands voted in favour.
in writing. − we recognise that sport has a special role in society as an instrument of social inclusion and integration, and as a promoter of local democracy. we acknowledge the considerable importance of sport in health terms as a preventive measure for obesity and chronic conditions. we fully support the notion of strengthening the role of women in sport; the importance of protecting athletes of all ages during and after their sports career; the promotion of sports in society, including physical activities in the school curriculum; the need for redistributive measures in sports financing; and the citizens right to information and wide access to broadcasts of sports events. however, we do not support the enhanced role of eu-level policy in sport. we therefore cannot support the steps taken to further increase eu policy in this area. specifically, we cannot support the idea of setting up a european police force for sport.
on behalf of the pse group how long will it be before at least one in ten assistants to members of parliament is working here without any social protection in a jungle of contracts of all kinds that are totally unclear and that arouse suspicion of fraud? every time the socialist group tries to give members' assistants a decent contract with all the normal guarantees of social security, the ppe-de group tries to prevent the coming into being of such a statute for assistants. the word 'statutes' is a real taboo in the ppe-de group. this time the word 'statutes' should be replaced by 'regulations'. and that's not a simple pun. this parliament should set an example in terms of working conditions, but it is falling hopelessly short of what has long been the standard within the eu. without a coherent statute, our employees will never get the protection to which they are simply entitled. this one point, the statute for assistants, is therefore important and the amendment to abolish it would be a stain on the otherwise excellent report by mr lewandowski. i agree with his comments and with those of reimer böge. the budget remains within its limits despite the approach of greater responsibilities for this parliament with the new treaty. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. clearness in this respect must be the explicit condition for releasing the amounts we intend to set aside for this purpose.
i am of the opinion, as are many others, that there should be a statute for members' assistants as soon as possible. it could save us a lot of trouble in the future. something about the buildings. when we bought some buildings in strasbourg a few years ago, the presence or absence of asbestos played a major role. they did an expert assessment and said, there is asbestos in the buildings - that's the case with all the buildings that were built at that time - but it's absolutely not dangerous. we bought the buildings on that basis. there was no suggestion that we should remove the asbestos soon. i therefore think that the administration is on the wrong track in asking for this now, because if it is to be done, it must be done at the expense of the strasbourg municipality. until we have that certainty, i do not think we should start removing asbestos from these buildings at the expense of this parliament. we must first get clarity from the strasbourg municipality, because it must pay.
tobacco kills about half a million european citizens every year. even among non-smokers, 19,000 deaths each year are caused by secondhand smoke. how do i know that? that is the message of the television spots that the european union itself has broadcast in all 27 member states as part of a major anti-smoking campaign. and we have all put 18 million euros into this. but that is a pittance compared with the subsidies the european union gives to tobacco growers every year. this year alone, brussels is spending 320 million euros on this. three years ago, it was almost 1 billion euros. is it not very strange that we are trying to combat smoking and at the same time financing tobacco production with european taxpayers' money? furthermore, the continuation of the financing runs counter to the whole idea behind the reforms of our agricultural policy, namely that we must get rid of production subsidies. president, i fully support the european commission's proposal to continue supporting the tobacco fund in 2008 and 2009, but this fund should not be used as an excuse to continue tobacco subsidies in 2013. although i have great respect for the arguments of my colleagues, i believe that public health must come first in this case.
it is difficult to say which is more absurd, that the european union subsidizes tobacco cultivation or that europe uses a small part of those subsidies for a fund which is supposed to discourage tobacco smoking. these kinds of totally hypocritical measures are precisely the reason why the eu has so little credibility among its citizens. the argument that the eu produces relatively little tobacco is not relevant. in fact, each pack still contains one eu-subsidised cigarette. the cigarette is of such inferior quality that we in europe do not even want to smoke it and we dump it elsewhere. the tobacco subsidy should not be extended, as this parliament wants. the commission has also proposed that part of the grants should not be used for shameful anti-smoking campaigns, as the commission wants. i have tobacco from those subsidies, they just have to be abolished immediately.
european farmers who grow tobacco should not receive a penny from the eu for it. of course, tobacco subsidies have been a steady source of income for farmers for years, a kind of social policy that did not deserve the name. but taxpayers' money should not be spent on products that cause illness. there is therefore only one choice for these subsidies: to be abolished. what is proposed in the berlato report is an absurd attempt to continue tobacco subsidies at least until 2012. that must not happen, and there is another reason for that. it is a petty injustice from the point of view of the developing countries to subsidize european tobacco. this is unfair competition with poor farmers and it goes against the development policy of the european union, a prime example of how one policy area can intersect with another. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. but be in solidarity and let the subsidies for tobacco go up in smoke, in the name of public health and in the name of the coherence of european policy for the development of the poorest countries in the world. that is solidarity.

the fight against discrimination, whether on the grounds of religion, sex or disability, is one of the pillars of the european union. that policy is laid down in all our treaties and also in legislation. the european union has shown great success in combating direct and indirect discrimination on grounds of sex. we can now look at the situation in terms of directives and conventions and find that, despite the most advanced legislative process in the world, the policy is not working properly. you can choose two lines. the opposition line is: we are coming up with a very broad new trajectory and can solve the problems with it. i don't think that's possible this way. the evidence was provided by the 2000 legislation which has led to infringement proceedings against ten member states, where member states, including the netherlands, have been targeted for the unclear functioning of certain provisions in the legislation on equal treatment. so new broad legislation will not help either. it's all about the nice words. we should be doing much more and much more to develop attitudes and action programmes and to ensure that there is no longer any discrimination, even indirect discrimination. we must ensure that implementation is better and then we will also have to find a solution for those problems which can be clearly stated, namely those of people with disabilities. i therefore wholeheartedly support the arguments of mrs bauer and of some of the members of our group, and i hope that we will now put into practice the excellent legislative framework we have. thank you very much.

on behalf of the gue/ngl group. – mr president, within the gue/ngl group there exists great tolerance for minority positions. there are cases where those minorities like to show to the plenary of this parliament that their viewpoint differs from the large majority inside our group. such was recently the case in the voting on my report concerning the relationship between macedonia and the european union. we gave the limited speaking time for our group to one of our greek members, who declared that the european union cannot admit any new member, as it would be better to abolish the union itself. possibly this remark created the misunderstanding that my group did not follow my proposals to speed up the negotiations with this candidate member state. however, this position was only the viewpoint of the communist party of greece, not of our group as a whole, which supported my proposals, as did the vast majority of this parliament. in the last debate this morning we again had such a situation. on the piecyk report on an integrated maritime policy, the two speakers from our group were the greek member pafilis and the portuguese member guerreiro. getting the opportunity to speak on this issue was very important for the parties they represent here. in their national debate, those two parties prefer to use the piecyk report as a symbol for everything that is wrong about the working conditions of seamen and port workers and also for everything that can be linked with nato intentions for the military use of the sea. the majority of our group, including the german and netherlands delegations, take the view that the piecyk report has nothing to do with those issues. we twice supported the defeat of the ports directive but we are not against maritime policy in general. we support mr piecyk’s proposals, which are focused on useful elements like the environment, the protection of coastal regions, education and the quality of labour.
in writing. − the european parliament resolution on the tobacco fund proposes to extend agricultural subsidies on tobacco. we vote against in protest at the whole system of tobacco subsidies. for the eu to financially support the growth of tobacco is ridiculous, to use part of this money to support anti-smoking campaigns hypocritical. all agricultural subsidies for tobacco should be abolished immediately. anti-smoking campaigns are useful but can easily be financed in other ways.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's citizens are not excluded from the european union's internal market. this choice is even more important in crossborder long-distance transport by bus and lorry than in many other industries. in bus transport, rules have been in place since april 2007 that allow the bus to stop for one day every six days during a group holiday to protect the driver from fatigue. we strongly agree with the objective of this measure. we are aware, however, that the way in which they are implemented is not only irritating to businessmen and customers but also to the drivers themselves. i have previously urged that this be improved by agreements with the trade union movement, the european transport workers' federation. it is better that drivers are guaranteed to rest at home, rather than being forced to do so on the road, amidst surprised and dissatisfied customers. the agreement on this is good, but we reject all attempts to unilaterally abolish this protection. the road transport of goods is a problem because the rules governing the taking of goods on return journeys from abroad vary from country to country. this means that the return journey is often unnecessarily long and largely empty. it is too early to adopt the same system for the whole of europe as is now reasonably functioning between the netherlands, belgium and luxembourg. this is only possible between countries with the same level of income and social conditions. we have nothing against crossborder solutions such as those which ensure that all drivers are paid a fair wage, adequate reimbursement of expenses and adequate safety measures. on the other hand, we object to the fact that in such a system the current large wage differentials between eu member states are being abused to reduce labour costs. then everyone who was paid well will lose their job and only people will be working there based on the current working conditions of, say, poland or romania. my group has therefore tabled amendments nos 45 and 46 to the report on freight transport, to make the opening of the cabotage market conditional on growth on a level playing field and an evaluation in 2012.
for the ind/dem group, by mr de gucht and others i would like to thank mr grosch and mrs ţicău for their work and make three further comments. my first comment concerns the amendment of the list of infringements in mrs ţicău's proposal which may lead to the withdrawal of a carrier's licence. this list of infringements is an essential part of the package and constitutes a possible restriction of the internal market. it is therefore preferable to amend the list of infringements by the co-decision procedure rather than the comitology procedure. i have therefore tabled an amendment which proposes that amendment and which i hope you will all support. secondly, i would like to stress the importance of a proper control of the list of infringements. the proposals we are discussing this afternoon are aimed at improving the internal market. it is important, however, that a genuine level playing field be created. the member states will also have to enforce the rules laid down in this regulation, and i therefore ask the european commission to ensure that the member states apply this regulation correctly. and my third point is that there are indeed member states which are trying to reduce cabotage to a minimum. this is totally contrary to the principles of the internal market and indirectly pollutes the environment. i will therefore certainly support the amendment which seeks to lift the restrictions on cabotage from 2012.
for years, there has been a lot of excitement about the cabotage phenomenon. the existing rules would be too vague temporarily because of the term used. it is a good excuse for several member states to further isolate their own market. to make this clear once and for all, the commission launched a new regulation a year ago. and as my colleague mr sterckx has just said, i expected a lot from this. however, the commission has proposed, remarkably, that the cabotage possibilities should be strictly limited. instead of more freedom, the present commission proposal would place more restrictions on the carriers. this is remarkable, because the existing 1993 rules were considered at the time, including by the european commission, as an intermediate step towards full freedom. of course we need a european approach. the sector should not have to face any more national pressure to minimise the cabotage possibilities. however, the commission proposal as such is not in line with the principles and objectives of the internal market, nor with those of the lisbon strategy, or, frankly, not at all. the arguments in favour of road safety, the environment and the reduction of administrative burdens mentioned in the european commission's argument are not relevant here. the free market is simply necessary for the most efficient planning possible. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's transport policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. as dirk sterckx has just made very clear, the alde group is therefore committed to a completely free market, preferably as from 1 january 2012. member states should also be free to agree on previously unlimited cabotage arrangements, if they consider it desirable. it should also be clear, above all, that authorisations granted before the entry into force of the proposed regulation remain valid in any event. we reject the 'one more study and then we will see' approach. the setting up of committees, the running of study no 250 in a row, leads to almost nothing, at least apart from a lot of hassle, associated costs and other messes, and we all know that all too well. the sector and, yes, the consumer, too, benefit from a free market as soon as possible. and finally, i would like to say one more thing: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. there is no place for that in this regulation.

the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's fishing fleet is not disrupted by the introduction of new fishing vessels. there are no social norms here. in bangladesh alone, 200 people have died in recent years as a result of work on the wrecking of seagoing vessels. many children are used in the demolition, since they can easily get into the small spaces. they must remove the toxic substances without protection. the substances then end up in the environment, with disastrous consequences for fisheries and tourism. unfortunately, many european ships also end up on the shores of india, pakistan and bangladesh in this way. the high steel prices, low wages, poor safety standards and lack of environmental measures are apparently financially attractive. the committee on the environment makes it clear that these practices are ethically unacceptable and that the dehumanizing and environmentally devastating situations surrounding the dismantling of ships must no longer continue. the efforts of an individual member state or the european union alone will not be sufficient. whatever we all do in the years ahead, a global solution to the problem of the wrecking yard remains urgently needed. unfortunately, we have to admit that not much has been achieved at the global level. this is precisely why the european commission has published a green paper. the main objective we want to achieve is the protection of the environment and public health without shifting the burden of the problems to other countries. we cannot wait any longer to take action, as many seagoing vessels now flying the european flag will have to be decommissioned in the coming years. the committee on the environment has put forward the following specific proposals. in order to avoid circumventing the regulation on shipments of waste, there should be guidelines for determining when a ship is a ship and when it is waste. the waste shipment regulation should be better implemented and enforced through stricter controls and supervision by port authorities in the member states, with the understanding that port states should have the power to declare a ship to be derelict. a list should be drawn up of the seagoing vessels which are likely to be scrapped within a few years. the european union needs to develop a competitive and clean ship-breaking industry, for example in shipyards that are no longer in use. the development aid should transfer know-how and technology to south asian scrapyards to meet safety and environmental standards. moreover, the demolition of seagoing vessels in bangladesh is a highly lucrative business, generating enough money to quickly bring about acceptable demolition conditions. i would like to stress that it remains necessary to continue working on two levels. first of all, we must take responsibility for our own european fleet and work towards a global agreement. we cannot concentrate on just one of these two at the moment. i have received a number of responses from the shipping industry that this green paper would impede or delay the process of reaching an imo convention. i do not agree with that, but i think that we as the european union are anticipating this imo convention and are better prepared to implement it in due course. i am also convinced that we as the european union want to get rid of the serious situation in south asia as soon as possible. i am glad to see that there is a great deal of agreement on this subject in the committee on the environment and the two joint committees. well, that's a good start. i assume that the house can support this line. it has also been shown that member states can already take action. last thursday, the otopan left the netherlands for turkey after authorities had the ship cleaned, removing all the hazardous waste. this is possible under the waste shipment regulation. an export licence has previously been refused because those conditions were not met. on the same thursday, i was visited by a delegation from the ngo for the responsible shipbreaking in south-east asia. it should be clear that these people have been fighting for years for responsible ship-breaking in their country and we cannot leave them in the cold. a lawyer from bangladesh, people from india, they have been working for this for years, and i think we should support them in this and adopt this resolution. i hope to hear from commissioner dimas what he intends to do in the near future to bring about responsible ship-breaking in those countries and to provide the means for that too.
i would like to thank mr blokland for his excellent report. i think he has very well outlined the enormous problems caused by the demolition of european ships in poor countries. the consequences, both in terms of working conditions and the environment, are often dramatic and the eu must not shirk its responsibility in this regard. mr blokland has chosen, among other things, to take the path of stricter international legislation. i am certainly in favour of such legislation, but i fear that it will take far too long before it produces concrete results. europe must act now and cannot wait for the rest of the world. for shipwrecks, proper burial arrangements should be made. the commission has already proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of the environment. and any vessels containing asbestos or other dangerous substances should be scrapped on european territory anyway. we can't allow our mess to cause great misery anywhere in the world. the moment a ship is no longer fit for use, we must stop thinking of it as a ship. it is then waste and should be treated as such. we simply cannot allow other hazardous waste to leave the eu without guarantees as to how it will be treated, and there is no reason not to do the same with scrap vessels. in addition, as in the whole of european waste policy, the emphasis should be on processing within the union itself. finally, a policy of naming and shaming shipowners and member states who have their ships demolished in an environmentally and humanly friendly way would be a good first step which we could actually start tomorrow. the responsibility for the proper demolition of a ship should always lie with the owner and it is then up to the member states to check that this is done in a proper manner.

the chairman of the council. - mr president, mr karl-heinz florenz, rapporteur, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. today, there is clear evidence that major changes in the climate system are man-made and that these changes are already having a negative impact on nature and human society. it is also certain that unless we act quickly to reduce greenhouse gas emissions substantially, global temperatures will continue to rise during this century, leading to widespread damage and disruption. the fourth assessment report of the intergovernmental forum on climate change, adopted in spain in november 2007, is the most comprehensive and credible scientific assessment of climate change to date. the report says that the warming of the climate system is undeniable, that the increase in anthropogenic greenhouse gas concentrations is very likely the main contributor to the increase in global average temperature over the past 50 years. the ipcc assessment reports published since 1990 show that the science of climate change and its consequences has advanced considerably in recent years. this can be attributed to a number of factors, including the growing evidence of perceived climate change, the hard work of scientists and better communication of scientific knowledge. as stated in the florence interim report, which we believe provides some very important fresh formulations on the problems described, what we know today is sufficient to start immediately designing and implementing policies that will contribute to reducing greenhouse gas emissions. this is why, as we all know, european heads of state or government decided last march to send a strong message to the international community by adopting commitments to reduce emissions. the european union is determined to deliver on these commitments through a comprehensive climate and energy policy. it will then pay particular attention to energy efficiency, renewable energy, biofuels, carbon capture and storage and the transition to a low-carbon economy in general. i would also like to remind you, ladies and gentlemen, of the recent decisions of the european council. as mentioned, the european union made a strong and ambitious commitment to climate and energy policy last year. now, in 2008, it is time to act. the bali climate change conference last december was a major breakthrough in the international negotiation process involving both developed and developing countries. this process is outlined in the bali action plan. the european union is determined to continue to maintain its leading international role in the field of climate change and energy and to maintain the necessary momentum in the negotiations under the united nations framework convention, in particular at the next session, to be held in poznań this year. the aim is to ensure that an ambitious, global and comprehensive agreement on climate change for the period after 2012 is reached in copenhagen in 2009, in line with the european union's objective of limiting global temperature rise to a maximum of two degrees. the european union will also make a major contribution to this by delivering on the objectives set at the 2007 spring european council. and the key challenge is to make the transition to a safe and sustainable low-carbon economy in a way that is compatible with the european union's sustainable development, competitiveness, security of energy supply, security of supply and sound and resilient public finances.
member of the commission. - the mr president, ladies and gentlemen of the european parliament, i would like to thank you for the opportunity to discuss today the interim report of the interim committee on climate change, which was presented by mr florenz, whom i congratulate on his excellent work. the report confirms the european parliament's support for an ambitious european community policy to combat climate change and i take this opportunity, mr president, to thank all the members of the european parliament for their continued support and such a significant contribution to promoting our climate policy and to raising awareness among the general public and to informing members from other countries. i also want to underline, mr president, the important role you have played in promoting the european union's climate change policy, and i am sure that you and the members of the european parliament will continue to do so with the same zeal so that we can use these two years - because there are less than two years left - to achieve an agreement in copenhagen at the end of 2009 to deal effectively with this great threat to the planet. both within the european union, where the debate on the climate and energy package is intensifying, and in the international negotiations, we need to mobilise all our forces and work together as best we can to build on the lead the european union has gained in the run-up to the copenhagen conference in december 2009. the important decisions taken in bali were driven by a combination of two factors: europe's global leadership in the fight against climate change; and the fact that the eu is a global leader in the fight against climate change. on the other hand, the scientific findings of the ipcc, the intergovernmental panel on climate change, have led many of the world's leaders to admit that there is indeed an urgent need for action. thanks to this series of scientific reports, citizens and policy-makers, including members of parliament in various countries, are now better aware of the scale of the challenge and the serious risks we face if we do not act to stop climate change. i think we can almost all agree that there is indeed scientific evidence of the need for urgent and bold action to tackle climate change, which the interim report before us summarises. this report reflects the scientific message delivered by the ipcc and other sources in 2007 more clearly than ever. the scientific debate over whether climate change is caused by human activities has dragged on for decades because of skeptics and has prevented decisive action. but it's closed now. it does not mean that all questions have been answered or that we have understood every detail. but we now know enough to conclude that taking ambitious action quickly is in our interest in terms of energy security and in terms of economic, environmental and social interests. not only can we not afford to wait, but even worse, the time we have available is very limited. if we limit the increase in global temperature to two degrees celsius - a limit beyond which we lose control of limiting or reversing environmental impacts - greenhouse gas emissions could peak within the next ten to fifteen years at the most. in the long run, to have a good chance of not exceeding two degrees celsius, global emissions in 2050 would have to be reduced by 50% compared to the 1990 level. to do this requires a radical change in the way we produce and use energy. what is needed is a global transition to a low-carbon economy, small but significant changes in many aspects of our daily lives. we need a green revolution, no more, no less! it has become clear from the ipcc reports that even with ambitious measures to reduce emissions, some serious impacts of climate change will be rather unavoidable. the international community must therefore be prepared to deal with them and, for this, it needs support, particularly in the most vulnerable of the developing countries, which will face the greatest problems. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. we know that we have a short time, given that the objective and the top priority is to reach an agreement in copenhagen in 2009. the signing of a substantive and comprehensive agreement, meeting the ambitious objectives imposed by scientific findings, is a major challenge. it will take a lot of effort to convince our international partners to sign the ambitious agreement we have set ourselves as our goal. the european union must therefore continue to stick to its strategy, which has proved successful so far. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area, including the establishment of a european centre for the development of vocational training, the establishment of a european centre for vocational training and the establishment of a european centre for vocational training. one of the challenges in this direction will be to ensure that developed countries participate in the effort to reduce emissions to levels corresponding to the two degrees celsius target. this is a reduction of their emissions by 25%-40% by 2020 compared to 1990. i would like to be absolutely clear on this point. we call on the united states to rise to the occasion and to become a factor of progress, not an obstacle to progress. as you have noticed during your recent visit to the united states, the discussions there have started to move in the right direction, but it is obvious that we still expect a lot from them. beyond developed countries, it is also necessary to work towards ambitious emission reduction commitments from developing countries, especially the most advanced developing countries. there are many opportunities for reducing emissions, opportunities that have significant added benefits, both for energy security and human health and for development in general. measures to this end should be provided for and supported in the expected 2009 agreement. i think that things are moving in the right direction here too. there is a growing awareness of the need to take action to tackle climate change. at the same time, the additional benefits are beginning to be realised, whether in terms of energy security, human health or economic development, which will not only not be jeopardised but rather enhanced. the recent visit of commission representatives to china confirmed the following: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. they have already begun to take action and intend to continue their efforts. we must support them, both bilaterally and multilaterally. we will have many opportunities in the coming months and in the coming year to promote our message. one of these will be the g8 and g8 sim5 summits, where the japanese presidency has turned its attention to climate change. another will be the un-led initiative for the world's major economies, as well as the various bilateral partnerships of the european union on climate change. we will use all these opportunities to convince our partners that urgent action is needed and that sound and sustainable policies on energy and climate change must be put in place. in this endeavour we should systematically highlight the scientific findings on which we rely and remind people of the consequences of inaction or inadequate measures. as we know, the common vision is to be negotiated in the context of the bali road map. it is very important that this be done on the basis of the valid scientific advice available to us. we must insist that the negotiations be conducted in the light of scientific findings. i am convinced that, together with the commission, you have an equally important role to play in conveying this crucial message to our partners, our citizens and their parliamentary representatives.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr karl-heinz florenz, for the thorough interim report before us today. it is good that all the relevant scientific information on climate change has been gathered in this report. that can then serve as a good stepping stone to the final report of the interim committee on climate change. i have nothing more to add to that in substance and that is why i have not tabled any further amendments. much is already known about climate change, but much more research is needed because many parameters are still unknown. this report makes active use of these additional studies. i cannot support the amendments tabled by mr březina and others, in so far as they are admissible. i shall follow the rapporteur's position on the amendments by mr doyle and others. finally, i would like to wish mr florenz every success in the preparation of the final report.
i would like to thank my colleague, karl-heinz florenz. he delivered an excellent report. the debate on climate change is very emotional, and rightly so. but today we must take a sober approach. we will be working intensively on climate change in the coming months and there are very ambitious measures on the agenda. it is of the utmost importance that europe shows its credibility before copenhagen and reaches agreement on the climate package. the only way to do that is to have a common basis, and that basis is science. it is to karl-heinz florenz's credit that he has expressed the scientific consensus. our policy is based on the findings of the ipcc. the ipcc is a group of thousands of scientists working together. the fact that the earth is warming is a fact, and that this warming is caused in part by human action is also a fact. to limit climate change, we need to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 60% to 80% by 2050. colleagues, that is not an easy task. the stakes are high. of course, there is a lot of profit and employment to be made from clean production and it is also good that we are ending our dependence on oil and entering the world of renewable energy in this way. but the transition to a low-carbon economy is not easy. so there are two things that are important. first of all: science must be the basis for policy. and that does not mean, mr pieper, that the consensus within the ipcc is a stone-cold truth. we can expect the ipcc to be open to criticism and to the well-founded arguments of the sceptics, because that helps science to progress and that is why our group fully supports paragraph 10. secondly, we need continued public support. in order to enable public support for firm action, we are asking for a translation of the scientific principles into a publicly accessible brochure so that everyone can learn about the challenges we face. this is how we can face these challenges together. i hope that mr florenz's report will provide us with a basis on which to move forward together and to put in place a sound policy by 2009.
the rapporteur. - mr president, since the last report on turkey, there have been a number of positive developments in that country. i am thinking of the adoption of the law on foundations or, more recently, the first amendment to article 301, which should lead to further reforms necessary to guarantee full freedom of expression. but it is also clear that turkey presents a mixed picture and that much more needs to be done to fulfil the agreements with europe, but also to fulfil the promises made to its own people. the report now before us is balanced. i hope that some of the horses being ridden will not make it, because that will keep the report balanced. i have mentioned all the problems in the report, mr president, and there are three important messages. firstly, we are concerned about the consequences of the case before the court against the akp. we expect the constitutional court to respect the principles of the rule of law, european standards and the guidelines of the venice commission on the prohibition of parties. we are pleased that democracy triumphed in 2007 over attempts by the military to disrupt the political process. but we are also concerned that there are still forces at work trying to destabilize the country. it is clear that the modernization of this country and the reforms are now needed. prime minister erdoğan has promised that 2008 will be the year of reforms and we will be happy to keep him. the government must now use the strong parliamentary majority to resolutely pursue reforms, reforms that are crucial to turkey's transformation into a modern and prosperous democracy based on a secular state and a pluralistic society. the reform is first and foremost in the best interests of the turkish citizen himself. a third point. the constitutional process is the opportunity to draw up a new civil constitution, the heart of which is the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms. only in this way can a system of checks and balances be established to guarantee democracy, the rule of law, social cohesion and the separation of religion and state. looking at recent figures, it is clear that the maintenance of the separation of church and state is causing tensions in turkish society. as many as 72% of highly educated turks are concerned about the secular nature of turkey, 60% of citizens in all major cities and almost 50% of other turkish citizens. and, mr president, that unrest has been seized upon by the judiciary, the prosecutors who take the space to sideline a parliamentary majority and work with a high degree of independence. the judiciary should be independent but also impartial in a state governed by the rule of law. the new constitution is the only way for the turkish government to reform the country, to enshrine the separation of church and state, and the rule of law, and to win new confidence from the citizens. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the new regulation to include the areas of the environment, health and the environment. i believe that this means that all political parties, ethnic and religious minorities and the social partners must reach a consensus on this modernisation. the modernisation must ensure that the individual rights of citizens, the civil liberties, are brought into line with the european convention for the protection of human rights. we must continue the negotiations between the eu and turkey with respect, but also without hypocrisy, so that we dare to be open and honest with each other. i very much regret that my colleague - i am referring to joost lagendijk - is being attacked in this way when he is openly and honestly pointing out where mistakes are being made and also asking for the cooperation of all political parties for the sake of the reform process. president. - i have the floor. there is still a lot to be done in terms of the position of the various religious minorities in turkey, the position of the kurdish population and other minorities, the socioeconomic development of the regions, the strengthening of the position of women, the dialogue between the turkish government and the social partners, with particular attention to the trade union movement, which is often under pressure, constructive cooperation in solving the cyprus problem and good neighbourliness in the region. in short, please honor the agreements that have been made. i would like to stress once again, mr president, that i believe that only a society governed by respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms, based on democracy, the rule of law and a social market economy, can develop into a peaceful, stable and prosperous society.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on women's rights and gender equality. - turkey has recently adopted important social and labour legislation. one important goal: the commission has already taken a number of measures to promote the integration of women into the labour market. because economic participation is badly needed to empower women. needless to say, women's rights are human rights. it is important for women to be able to claim fundamental rights such as sexual and reproductive rights and not to be subject to vague criteria such as "against public morals". this is also essential for gay organisations. in turkey, instruments are badly needed to monitor this and to introduce gender mainstreaming. i therefore call for a committee on women's rights in the turkish parliament with full legislative powers. next year, turkey will hold local elections. nationally, there was a doubling of the number of female parliamentarians. but more is needed, because at the local level, participation is still below 1%, a huge challenge to make a fair representation of women in politics a reality.

the republic of turkey disqualifies itself from membership of the european union. the ill-treatment of its tiny christian minority, perhaps 100,000 citizens or just one hundredth of one percent of the total population, leaves me no other conclusion. and, by the way, the copenhagen criteria leave no other choice for the council, the commission and parliament. the outright persecution of syrian orthodox and other christians in the southeast of turkey, tur abdin, for years and days now, is a direct indictment of the turkish state. does a country whose citizens attend christian services and are regularly questioned by the police or the secret service belong in the european union? interrogations which are also accompanied by threats in the personal or professional sphere or even, in some cases, by torture. turkish conditions, turkish self-disqualification. the point is, however, how fair is the european union to itself in this matter?
