on behalf of the pse group i would like to begin by congratulating the commissioner, also on behalf of my group, who has clearly indicated today that he is doing what he can under very difficult circumstances. he himself spoke of a dialogue between deaf people. we must note that the situation in burma is taking a bizarre turn. almost three weeks after the cyclone swept through the country, the regime still barely allows outside help. the authorities themselves seem to be rather passive. the country has been closed to aid workers, experts and media for weeks. the fact that the junta allowed a referendum to take place a week and a half ago, which was supposed to strengthen its own position of power, is almost kafkaesque. the official death toll is estimated by some sources to be as high as 80,000. tens of thousands are still missing and the number of displaced persons is over two million. it's getting to be pol pot-like proportions. at least we think we do. because there's just as little information coming out of the country as there is aid coming in. refusing to allow aid is murder, as former un coordinator jan egeland said earlier this week. the burmese government is making a mockery of its responsibility to protect, the responsibility to protect. the contrast with china, which was tragically hit by a massive earthquake last week, is striking. the enormous devastation was not hidden and was seen on tv everywhere, including in china itself, and the chinese government issued an international appeal for help. the burmese government can't afford much more damage to its image. that's pretty much where the bottom is. china could do much more to persuade the burmese government to accept international aid. the same applies to russia and india, which should increase pressure on the junta either in the un or in asean. i agree with mr nassauer's comments on this. last monday, some agreement was reached in the asean context on international aid to burma. all aid will be coordinated by asean. one step forward. better late than never. but direct aid from western countries remains out of the question. there's a donor conference being held next week. i call on europe to contribute generously and generously, but only if we can also guarantee that our contribution will be well spent and that journalists will also be allowed access to the country, so that we too can really get a picture of the situation there.
how many times have we been here to talk about burma? yet several times a year we gather here and it is becoming a hopeless affair. but conditions in burma have again worsened than on any previous occasion when we have discussed it. 1.4 million victims have not yet received assistance. thirty thousand children suffer from acute malnutrition. hundreds of thousands of homeless people are at risk of cholera, pneumonia and other infectious diseases. i understand that this morning as many as eight foreign doctors from doctors without borders were allowed into the disaster area. it's too little, too late. i would like to support mr nassauer's appeal to asean. they must now finally show their backbone, because it is clear that the junta cares more for the survival of its own regime through the referendum which does not deserve that name - mr wiersma calls it 'kafkaesque' - than for the survival of its own people. the arrival of foreigners could undermine the survival of the military regime, the generals believe. but it 's clear: doctors without borders, oxfam, the british, french and american ships are bringing relief supplies, not regime change, as much as i might like to. but we can't convince the junta of that. so we have to look for alternatives. i believe that the best alternative is to be found in the security council. europe and the united states must push for a change of agenda in the situation in burma. all the member states of the united nations have subscribed to the following two principles: the assumption of responsibility to protect nationals and, if a country is no longer able or willing to do so, the right to international intervention in disaster situations. the united nations must act. i understand the problems and i am proud of commissioner michel, who, instead of sitting down and shaking hands and convening official working parties, simply went there under the motto 'no words, but deeds'. i like that. i also think that the support promised by the european union is exemplary. i also think that french minister kouchner is right when he says that the junta is guilty of a crime against humanity. i wonder why we do not hear the other 26 european member states as loudly. mr president, aid must come quickly to the burmese people, with or without the junta's approval. denying necessary support is a crime against humanity. i would like to see the european union take the initiative to have the united nations and the security council refer this case to the public prosecutor at the international criminal court in the hague. enough is enough, i've had it. i would say: drag the junta to the international criminal court. that's what we're for. as things stand, listen to those behind bars and not the dissidents in burma!
two weeks after the disaster, some of the burmese population has finally received some rice, beans and medicine, but this aid has not yet reached a quarter of all the victims and is criminally late because of the junta. the un world food program says it has been able to provide rice and beans to 212,000 of the 750,000 people who are most in need. the european parliament has already made a number of proposals to the council. i thank the commission for all the positive efforts in burma and also from brussels. but millions of people are left to starve, to thirst and to sickness. that is a form of torture, that is murder and a total breach of the duty of protection. it's a practice that comes close to genocide. the military is therefore responsible for crimes against humanity. that is a matter for our group and tomorrow for this parliament before the international criminal court. the security council must launch an investigation into the crimes of this regime. what is the commission's view on this? as rapporteur on burma, i ask myself: when is the limit of respect for the sovereignty of a country reached? the border was closer to iraq. when will respect for fundamental human rights become as inviolable for the same international community? the credibility of human rights is now being undermined worldwide by the refusal, especially by neighbouring countries, to address the burmese regime and by the unwillingness to come to the aid of the population if necessary without the permission of the colonels. sovereignty does not give you the right to strangle your own people. hence the request to the council of the european union and in particular to the united kingdom as the current president of the security council to put the situation in burma back on the agenda of the security council. china and russia must realize that the situation there is even more criminal, even more serious than it was shortly after the cyclone.
with unprecedented openness, the chinese mass media have reported on the terrible consequences of the massive earthquake that struck sichuan province over a week ago. at home, this official openness has led to an unprecedented solidarity with the victims. the harmonious society came into view. tens of thousands of volunteers have signed up rescue teams are pouring into the disaster area from all provinces. material aid in china is at an all-time high. in short, official openness pays off. the european institutions must therefore continue to encourage the chinese authorities to adopt this unprecedented approach. the critical question already raised in china as to why so many school buildings collapsed certainly fits into this context. china's openness to the outside world is also on the rise. beijing accepted the japanese offer to send a rescue team. taiwan has now pledged 42 million euros to help the victims of the earthquake. i sincerely hope that this compassion from across the street will lead to the necessary normalization of china-taiwan relations. i can warmly join with the colleagues who have expressed their deep sympathy with the unspeakable suffering of the chinese people and i am particularly grateful to mr sterckx for signing the register of mourning on behalf of us all at the chinese representation in brussels. i can relate to that.

author. − madam president, in december 2007 the un general assembly accepted by an overwhelming majority a resolution that urged investigations into the health effects of uranium weapons on civilians and military personnel. we congratulate germany, ireland and italy as the only nato countries that supported the un resolution. the reason for that support may be the fact that many of their soldiers returned with fatal diseases and/or later had children with serious malformations. therefore we appeal to other eu countries to follow their example and submit health reports in line with the un request. du (depleted uranium) was used in the balkans, iraq and afghanistan. du is waste, and is an extremely cheap material for making arms. the estimated global stocks are 1.3 million tonnes. far less than 1 microgram inside the body can be fatal. apart from the radiation, du is a chemical toxic compound. nobody informs the troops, or the populations of the countries where du weapons are used. the latest findings of the world health organisation’s experts concerning the harmful effects of du have been censored. we urge the eu to inform its citizens and the populations of targeted countries. we call on the commission and council to make sure that an international treaty will be established as soon as possible. in order to gain a detailed insight into the matter, it is high time for the commission and council to provide access to all existing reports and initiate the further investigations which parliament has been urging since 2001. meanwhile we reiterate our call to ban du on the basis of the precautionary principle.
europe is rightly investing heavily in maintaining and strengthening competitiveness and we are maintaining our market share in essential sectors, but, as the commissioner has just said, we are only in the first wave of globalisation. it's really important to be on the edge of your seat. then it's about that new combination of research and development and making and selling innovative products in a way that is actually profitable. this report by romana jordan cizelj does indeed put the right emphasis. we must do away with bureaucracy, we must indeed talk about greater efficiency in legislation and regulation. the action programme provides the impetus for this. but still, one cri de coeur from me. it is still difficult for industrial enterprises to find a way through the european instruments themselves. the cip and the seventh framework programme are central, top-down instruments to be decided in brussels. the structural funds are decentralised. each with its own front door, its own criteria, you name it. my message is: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. then i can see the effect in the regions. i see this clustering in successful sectors happening at all levels. in my region, we have set up a one-stop shop for these various instruments last month, and that is clear. i'm going round. there is no need for fragmentation and red tape. we must know what we want and this also increases the visibility for businesses and citizens that is so necessary. they should also say: europe has made the right choice and has done the right thing. that's a long way from being a good point.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe-de group. i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer to this question. i would like to thank the commission and the rapporteur, the commission for its communication and the rapporteur for his report. the great challenge will be to translate the ambitions set out in the communication and in our report into concrete measures. i would like to mention a few points which we think should be dealt with. in the fight against animal diseases we naturally share the commission's view: better to fight than to heal. but if an animal disease does occur, vaccination - and you have said this - must indeed be central to its control. we want to avoid situations like those in my member state, where 285 000 animals had to be slaughtered in 2001 for 26 cases of scd. since there is no distinction between products from vaccinated and non-vaccinated animals, the marketing of such products should be guaranteed, both inside and outside the european union. i would urge the european commission to take care of this, because it has gone wrong in the past. of course, supermarkets, processors and consumers must also take responsibility for this. we also call on the commission to clarify the role of the european union, the member states and the sector itself in financing the fight against animal diseases, especially as this subject is indeed part of the debate on the health check of the common agricultural policy. the fact remains that member states deal with this financing in very different ways, and this could have consequences, including for competitiveness. so in this we ask for more clarity. of course, exporting countries must meet the same requirements as our european producers, for example in the area of the identification and control of animal diseases. finally, i would like to hear from the european commission when it intends to present its analysis of the current transport regulations and how we can limit as much as possible the possible spread of animal diseases in this area.
i would also like to thank the rapporteur for the enthusiasm with which he has carried out his work. i think that with increasing globalisation we must take more account of the outbreaks of infectious animal diseases in the future. people are traveling more, taking products they're not supposed to, and that can cause outbreaks. everyone has been talking about it: we need better border security, because prevention is better than cure. i always notice, when i come to the united states or australia for example, that the border controls are considerably stricter there than in europe. i wonder why the european union does not have a similar system to that of the united states, for example, where everyone is asked to fill in whether or not they are taking animal products with them. then we can control it properly. what should happen in the event of outbreaks of contagious animal diseases? i think everyone is convinced by now: the mass slaughter that used to happen is no longer acceptable. we must vaccinate all animals and then the products of the animals that have been vaccinated must be able to be sold on the european market and beyond. that's the problem. i am not impressed by the efforts of the commission to convince supermarkets, consumer organizations and so on that products from vaccinated animals are as good as products from unvaccinated animals. it is still necessary that, if vaccinated against foot-and-mouth disease, the products of those animals should be labelled so that a consumer knows that. this seems to me to be completely unnecessary. so the commission is taking an active stance. we are still used to the fact that, again this year, there is still a huge surplus in the agricultural budget every year. so far we have been able to pay for all outbreaks of contagious animal diseases from that agricultural budget. in the future, that might well be different. the new countries are being phased in and the margin in the agricultural budget is getting smaller and smaller. still, there's gonna be a price to pay in the event of an outbreak. european law says that it must be done. i therefore think that as soon as possible - i already received a pilot project in the budget for this in 2004 - there should be a scheme of european animal insurance, animal health funds by country or at european level, and so on. all the plans are in place and the commission must get down to work as soon as possible. the last point is the transport of animals. i am not one of those who say 'if you transport the animal for so many hours, it is by definition good'. it may be that two hours of transport is worse than, say, nine hours of transport. it is the conditions under which the animal is transported that the commission will have to take into account, not only the length of time but also the circumstances.
i would almost say how strange it is that the commission has a strategy for animal health with an emphasis on prevention. that's really great. but what a disappointment that animal welfare is only a by-product of the commission's strategy. a well-known song in the netherlands begins with the words "an animal is more than a piece of meat and a human being is more than a consumer". unfortunately, the commission does not really sing along to this song. it still sees too many animals primarily as producers of meat, skin, hair, eggs, milk and manure. the economic view is still far too dominant and therefore, in my view, too one-sided. fortunately, and i thank the rapporteur for that, his report is already much better, if only because, unlike the commission's proposal, it does contain genuine welfare points. i'll name two that i think are very important. recognition that intensive livestock farming seriously increases the risks of outbreaks and epidemics. the enormous size of the european bio-industry is not the question of whether or not a new animal disease will occur, but when. it is rightly pointed out that animal transport poses a high risk of spreading animal diseases. we must therefore avoid, reduce and improve transport of animals as much as possible, also because of the enormous suffering they cause. this report is not as strong on animal welfare as i would like, but any improvement is an improvement and i will therefore support it wholeheartedly.
'prevention is better than cure' is the motto of the european commission. i couldn't agree more. that is why i support her strategy. the principles of cooperation and communication appeal to me. i also think it is good to work on simplifying and clarifying legislation. i would like to draw particular attention to vaccination. i'm totally behind that. the mass preventive killing of healthy animals should be avoided as far as possible. dutch farmers have experienced in the past how such measures can intervene. i would stress the call in the wojciechowski report to take measures in this connection to avoid the creation of trade barriers. uncertainty about the marketing possibilities of products from vaccinated animals must be avoided. good public communication on the safety of these products is therefore of great importance. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the vaccination programmes are properly implemented. animal health and welfare are important issues for european citizens, and rightly so. i would therefore urge that this be raised in the context of the wto. a good strategy cannot be undermined by imports from countries which do not have the same animal health and welfare obligations as the european union. this is damaging consumer confidence and is bad for the support of farmers and other direct stakeholders. these remarks enable me to support the strategy proposed and i thank the rapporteur very much for his report.
development money can and should be better spent. that, mr president, is what we can conclude from the rapporteur's extremely balanced report. my compliments. the report comes at the right time. europe seems to be getting tired of spending on development. this trend is extremely worrying and we should not underestimate it. even countries that have always been known for their international solidarity, such as the netherlands, are voting for a reduction in development spending. the only answer is to provide transparent, efficient and results-oriented work. the european union can still make a major contribution to this, particularly through the three cs: the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the programme. the commissioner has also made many references to this and is working on it. we can also do more to increase the number of projects and programmes. when something goes wrong, an extra program isn't always the best solution. we'd better do a few things right than half of everything. president. - the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the resulting problems are much more difficult to solve in burundi than in other african countries. it is difficult to draw a geographical boundary between areas which are typically the home of different groups of people. it's more like the caste system as it was traditionally in india. even before the german and belgian colonization, there was a hutu majority and a tutsi minority in burundi and rwanda. the tutsi, who also differed in outward appearance from the hutu, were the rulers. the hutus were their subordinates. in the days of european colonization, there were attempts to change this, but they were not aimed at the equality and equal rights of the hutu. the main issue was the balance between the two groups of the population in favour of power for the german or belgian administration. even after independence, these old contradictions have never been resolved in a lasting way. a massive campaign by the hutu majority to exterminate the minority tutsi who are a nuisance to them, as in the northern neighbour rwanda, has spared burundi. but that can also be explained by the less far-reaching emancipation of the hutu. when we speak in the resolution about 14 years of civil war, peace negotiations, the fnl opposition movement, the disarmament of the rebels and the new fighting that broke out on 17 april, we must be aware of that history and the unresolved problems. the main thrust of the resolution is that the violence must stop and that agreement must be reached. i agree with that, but i see little reason for optimism on that point. the resolution rightly calls for european support for democratic governance, education and health care. we can do something about it.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, negotiations with the council have been long, intensive, complicated and tough. the starting point for most member states was: ‘how do we get rid of this directive, as we do not want any european standards on the return of illegally staying third-country nationals?’. clearly council and parliament had a very different understanding of what should be a serious return directive with sufficient safeguards. parliament had to fight for each and every single word and comma. to everybody, and to the pse group in particular, it should by now be crystal clear that the compromise package puts in rules where none exist at present. member states with more favourable conditions in place should maintain these, or introduce them if they wish to do so. national parliaments have a role to play to ensure this in the implementation of the directive. moreover, we also secured a political declaration from the council that this directive will not and cannot be used as an excuse to lower existing standards. experiences with 10 other asylum and migration directives do show that the fear that there is a tendency among member states to use the transposition of such directives as a pretext for making their national law more repressive does not materialise. at present, no eu legislation exists as regards the return of illegally staying third-country nationals. with the adoption of the package, community control mechanisms will become available. infringement procedures, competence of the european court of justice, commission reporting, ep monitoring: all these will be available. three years of debate have shown that there is no more room for manoeuvre and it is obvious that at second reading no further improvements will be made. yes, it is unfortunate, i admit, but it is true. council will start tabling a list with many ‘no-go’ proposals. pandora’s box will indeed be opened. clearly the current political climate in most member states will not be of any help. so how to put in place common minimum rules where none exist at present? how to make sure that community control mechanisms will be available? how to make sure that coe guidelines are made legally binding for all member states? at this stage, the fundamental question is: do we want a directive or not? do we want a directive which is not perfect but undoubtedly a first step in the right direction, or do we not want a directive at all as we consider the current situation to be satisfactory? even now, many member states would be relieved if the directive died out due to parliament’s fault. so, what an irony that those members of parliament trying to break up the compromise package are effectively supporting those member states not wanting to have any european safeguards on return in the first place! the compromise package should be seen as a very modest but important first step. the return policy cannot be looked upon in an isolated way, but should be seen as a necessary part of a total package on migration, including legal, as well as asylum. indeed, in my view, after almost three years of debate and negotiations, it is high time to take up our responsibility. mr president, we have heard a great deal in this debate and i do not want to repeat everything, but i think there are two fundamental issues at stake here. first of all, fair competition or a level playing field between european producers and those from third countries. the european poultry sector has invested millions in advanced control of salmonella and other contaminants throughout the production chain, based on our european rules. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's trade policy is not distorted by the introduction of new products. same monks, same caps, as we say in the netherlands. we have recently demanded that for meat from brazil, and the same should apply to chicken meat from the united states. secondly, the precautionary principle. the reports on the health and environmental effects of this technique still raise the necessary questions. the commission's proposal to decide on a possible two-year authorization followed by an analysis based on new data is of course completely contrary to this precautionary principle. first we need clarity on security and then a debate, not the other way around. the idea of labelling american chlorinated chickens is also unacceptable, because the inequality between farmers here and there remains and, moreover, processed products are likely to escape this labelling. and when i speak of processed products, i mean, for example, the considerable quantities of chicken nuggets and drumsticks which will be consumed on television tonight by dutch, romanian, french and italian football fans. i therefore want to call on the commission to adhere to these two basic european principles, the level playing field and the precautionary principle, to respect the wishes of a large majority in the management committee and in this house and thus to abandon this unfortunate proposal.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 4064/89 on the common organization of the market in fruit and vegetables (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). in the cities, areas formerly available for pedestrians, for children playing, for trees, for parks and plantations or for free trams were converted into car spaces between the 1950s and the 1980s. outlying roads were also widened or supplemented by wide through-track highways. public transport, on the other hand, became more expensive, while the network of lines and frequencies were reduced. even the construction of metro networks was not always aimed at speeding up public transport, with more space for large numbers of passengers, but rather at making more room for the car on the roads above the metro tunnels. many people are now condemned to use the car because of the location of their work and the lack of other means of transport. that takes up a lot of space, a lot of energy, affects air quality and causes a lot of unnecessary noise. but it is especially unsafe for pedestrians and cyclists, who have to share a scarce public space with the car. when we talk in this parliament about measures to protect pedestrians and other vulnerable road users, unfortunately we are not usually talking about alternatives to the car, but about how we can shape the car in such a way that the damage in a collision is reduced. for years, we have been talking here mainly about car lights, about braking systems and about the front edges of cars that can injure pedestrians. the european commission is now proposing to replace the 2003 and 2005 directives which required member states to incorporate better security measures into national legislation with a regulation which directly imposes eu legislation. the choice of regulation quickly provides the greatest degree of certainty and uniformity for companies involved in automotive technology, but it also has disadvantages. we are thus pushing democratic considerations within the member states further into the background. moreover, that regulation would not enter into force until 2014 and would be more moderate for phase 2 than the directive already provides. i support the rapporteur, mr ferrari, if he wants to introduce safety measures as soon as possible. my group can agree to this modest improvement, because all the little things help, but for real security more is needed. the best contribution to safety is to prevent further growth in the number of cars and to reduce their number where possible.
the report by mr ferrari deals with the protection of pedestrians and other vulnerable road users. i am pleased that mr ferrari has succeeded in reaching agreement with the council at first reading. i would like to thank him for his work and congratulate him on his excellent report. i'd like to make a few comments. let me begin by saying that the protection of pedestrians and other vulnerable road users is of the utmost importance to me. you know that in my country, the netherlands, the weaker road users make up a large proportion of the total number of road users; the proportion of young people in the labour force is relatively high, especially in the transport between home and work and to and from school. recreational walking and cycling are also becoming increasingly important. these walkers and cyclists are of great value to our society. walking and cycling are extremely environmentally friendly modes of transport and, in addition, cycling and walking are very healthy for citizens, provided the air quality is good. a large proportion of car journeys in european cities are less than 5 kilometres. it is therefore important to encourage citizens to opt for clean and healthy forms of transport, while ensuring that they are adequately protected as vulnerable road users. because although i just praised the netherlands, because so many citizens there cycle and walk, there are also more and more citizens who no longer dare to let their children cycle or walk a short distance to school, for fear of accidents. however, the measures included in the compromise will help to reduce the frequency of accidents and, if they do occur, their harmful consequences. that is why i am satisfied with the compromise reached. i hope that citizens, at least in their own urban agglomeration, will increasingly cycle and walk instead of driving. on the other hand, we must be careful not to get into a situation where we are trying to reduce all the risks on the road to zero, because it is not possible to eliminate all the risks. it is equally important that the directors be made aware of their own responsibility. all measures taken in this area of policy should emphasise that road users themselves remain principally responsible for their own safety and for the safety of their fellow road users. finally, i would like to briefly touch on the actual legislation proposed. i welcome the choice of a regulation rather than a directive. this will ensure that the application of this important legislation is clear and will prevent any further divergences between member states as regards the protection of pedestrians and other vulnerable road users. i am also pleased that this new proposal focuses more on active safety systems than the 2003 and 2005 directives. active solutions, such as brake assist systems, have the advantage over passive safety systems that they also prevent a lot of negative side effects of accidents, such as delay, traffic jams and immaterial damage that are not limited by the application of passive safety systems. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. i support the idea of setting deadlines for increasing the requirements for type approval. the safety of pedestrians and other vulnerable road users is not a subject to be neglected. i also want to mention the fact that amendments to article 11 must be made by the codecision procedure. it is not desirable that essential changes be made through the comitology procedure.
in writing. - war, dictatorship, discrimination, natural disasters and poverty are driving people from africa and asia to flee to europe. for many refugees, this is not a choice for improvement, but a choice for survival. at the tampere summit in 1999, an attempt was made to reduce the flow of refugees to a number of eu member states. this was not achieved by appealing to mutual solidarity to spread refugees more widely across the territory of the eu member states, but only by creating even greater barriers to entry into europe at the external borders. in recent years, more and more distressing situations have arisen. many people drown at sea, and when they reach land they are imprisoned for a time, forced into illegality or forcibly returned to a country where they cannot survive. the weber report and the attempt to reach agreement with the council at first reading are leading to a further deterioration. there is a growing opposition to this in many quarters, and rightly so. if this is nevertheless adopted, people can be detained without trial for a year and a half, children can be sent back more easily and returnees can be banned from entering for 5 years regardless of the deteriorated situation in their country of residence.
in writing. − the provision of electricity is not a trade but a utility. it must be delivered to all interested parties without interruption and with the lowest possible environmental impact. this requirement is contrary to the risks of international trade by competing companies. electricity will continue to be a scarce and fragile commodity, especially as fossil fuels are depleted and global warming continues. it is therefore good that in many eu member states it is the state or local authorities that have set up power stations and electricity networks. privatization of these utilities is undesirable and dangerous. the sale gives new owners a monopoly on the transportation network. this network is an unprofitable tool between production and sale, but its ownership can be misused to impose a high toll on users and any competitors. the setting of electricity prices through the stock exchange leads to consumer prices that are much higher than the production costs. the eu member states are rightly looking at how to combat these dangers. the situation varies considerably from one member state to another. considerations can best be made there. i am in favour of the greatest possible freedom for the member states, the third option as envisaged in the glante amendment, but then against this european directive as a whole.
mr president, the fisheries sector is in a difficult situation because of the high fuel prices. in 2008 alone, the price of gas oil increased by more than 38%. there are no signs of an end to the price increase for the time being. in my country, the netherlands, some 15 vessels of the cod fleet have already been ordered to stop fishing because every fishing trip results in a loss. but the dramatic rise in prices is not the only factor responsible for the dramatic situation. the import of large quantities of fish at low prices from outside the european union is also partly responsible for this situation. large quantities of yellowtail flounder and pacific flounder are imported into the netherlands and sometimes sold as sole and flounder. this is unfair competition with the sole and plaice caught by the dutch fishermen. the use of the names of these fish species needs to be more closely monitored. the dutch fleet must also be transitioned. the ships we have today are too big and too dependent on fossil fuels. in fact, all vessels should be replaced by smaller multi-purpose vessels with sustainable fishing methods. unfortunately, the european commission has only granted a temporary licence for five electric pulse-fishing vessels for sole. we'd like more. the sector needs financial resources to survive and this needs to be addressed in the short term. i welcome the proposals of the european commissioner. perhaps there are also opportunities through the co2 savings of the fisheries sector. it's good to look at that again. the dutch fishing fleet is responsible for about 1% of total co2 emissions. we want to reduce it by 20%. if you look at the price of co2 of 25 euros per ton, there are possibilities here. especially when you consider that with a cleanup that fleet disappears and there's a complete reduction in co2 emissions. a seven-year depreciation could be expected. i hope the commission will go ahead with the proposals. parliament will be happy to support them, but perhaps you could take this with you.

i would like to thank the rapporteur, mrs gurmai, for her report. she took the right approach. mr kusstatscher is of course right in his philosophical criticism of the use of the term 'intelligent car'. and of course, this is about artificial, artificial intelligence, and that's how we should understand that word. there are two issues which should be clearly highlighted in the resolution on the intelligent car. firstly, the development of the intelligent car must serve the interests of a clean environment. secondly, this development should have a positive effect on road safety. i am glad that these issues have been clearly included in mrs gurmai's report. i would like to make the following comments. firstly, i believe it is important that the development of the intelligent car should be in line with the environmental objectives of the european union. the report mentions the european commission's objective of limiting the co2 emissions of new passenger cars and light commercial vehicles to an average of 120 grams per kilometre. i would add that the target for 2020 is 80 grams per kilometre. i think that the development of intelligent cars can also help to achieve this goal. certainly, in the sustainable development of energy sources for the car there are still many intelligent possibilities to achieve the co2 emission targets. secondly, i note that road safety has also been served by the further development of intelligent cars. the report already says a lot about this, so i will not go into it at length. with regard to recital j, it should be clear that, regardless of whether technological development makes drivers feel safer and therefore less attentive, each driver is responsible for his or her own prudent driving behaviour. this principle must remain at the forefront and it is good that this is brought to the attention of the directors. my final remarks will be directed to this report as a whole. it is of the utmost importance that the development of intelligent cars is not seen in isolation from the development of an intelligent car environment. it is precisely in this cooperation that there are many opportunities to reduce environmental pollution and to make traffic safer. a good transport system is of vital social importance. we must, however, accept that transport is detrimental to the environment and will continue to be associated with accidents. however, the development of intelligent transport systems can certainly contribute to reducing these negative effects of transport. i would therefore like to draw attention once again to recital 13. i expect the member states and the european commission to give strong support to the introduction of environmentally friendly and vehicle-safe features. i also stress the importance of sharing knowledge about intelligent transport systems in a good way. it is likely that the knowledge will be available in member states which are relatively less affected by the negative effects of transport mentioned above. in that case, it would be good to have a system which ensures that this important knowledge is also available in member states which have a strong interest in it and which respects the intellectual property rights of the developer of the intelligent transport systems. i think that the member states should reach clear agreements on this.
in writing. − whereas we fully support cooperation between the member states to prevent the import of dangerous toys, drugs and other illegal or dangerous products, and whereas we fully support cooperation with trading partners to this end, we cannot vote in favour of this resolution due to the severe impact of the customs union on developing countries’ trade. the eu uses the customs union as a tool to negotiate favourable trade deals with developing countries while at the same time imposing high tariffs on products that are seen as unwanted competition for eu products. this policy is hampering the potential for economic growth in developing countries.

four features stand out. first of all: privileges for a military caste; a closed armed group controls the government and the economy. that group can enrich themselves while the others remain poor. secondly: the oppression of national minorities: the groups in the interior with a different language and a different culture who want self-government. the british colonial past has added their territories to burma, but in that country they are condemned to the position of third-class citizens. thirdly: the acquisition of foreign income through export on the basis of competition through extremely low wages or forced labour. fourthly: the systematic exclusion of groups who want to change this situation from any participation, even though they have won the only free elections that this regime has ever dared to organize. in the relief efforts after the flood disaster, we saw how much this regime is afraid of the possibility that foreigners on the ground might notice how bad it is or that they might influence the population. internally displaced persons must return quickly to their devastated villages without reconstruction with the sole aim of making contact between discontented masses impossible. immediately after the disaster, a referendum was held as a matter of priority, the main aim of which was to permanently exclude the democratic opposition from the government by means of a false result. the imprisonment of people for political reasons is a survival tool for such a regime. the outside world must stand up for these prisoners and other opponents. china and india should do the same, along with europe.
the author. - mr president, the violence in somalia has everything to do with the fact that there has been no generally recognised government since 1991. the south and centre of the country have been the scene of years of fighting between the armies of various warlords, and in the north the administration of the territory of the former british colony of somaliland is not internationally recognised. the union of islamic courts and the army of neighbouring ethiopia have since clashed. agreements on reunification of the country were never generally supported, and those who supported them often ended up withdrawing. this prolonged chaos soon turns support from the outside world for any transitional government into interference in intractable armed internal conflicts. in recent years i have warned on several occasions that the european union is being tied to one of the warring parties by co-financing failing structures without any prospect of a lasting and comprehensive solution. europe must certainly help if a solution is possible, but premature intervention leads to accidents. on 15 november 2007, during an earlier urgent debate on somalia, this parliament adopted a resolution calling for an end to foreign military interventions and for dialogue and reconciliation at home. it is good that the resolution before us today also speaks out against the misuse of european union support for warlords who want to restore their power, against the use of child soldiers and against new arms supplies. yesterday, an assassination attempt on the interim president abdullahi yusuf failed. armed forces are still at odds. we must now see whether the agreement reached last week to end the violence within 30 days and replace the ethiopian intervention force with a united nations peacekeeping force within three months will have a serious chance. if this leads to real solutions, european support will be useful.

- a writer. - we too are appalled by the latest measures announced by the italian government to solve the so-called roma problem in italy. i am very sorry that we have to have such a debate here today. i myself was rapporteur for slovakia's accession for many years and have had a lot to do with the roma problem. i used to say: i am not saying that we should not discriminate, it is simply forbidden, those are the rules and agreements of the european union. the registration of roma is very much like discrimination and that did not happen in slovakia. it is a pity that i now have to debate the action of the government of an existing member state. the latest package issued by interior minister maroni to address, as the government now calls it, the roma emergency has a bad side. the creation of a database of fingerprints of roma children is contrary to the fundamental principles of the european union: non-discrimination, equality before the law, protection of minorities. the announcement of this measure comes at a time when the european commission has not yet completed its assessment of an earlier italian package of measures. in may of this year, the prefects of the major cities were given extraordinary powers to act against illegal immigrants and roma, powers that were also frowned upon at the time. i would therefore call on the italian government to exercise great restraint and not to go beyond the limits of what is acceptable in europe. abolish the measure! this has no place in the european union. the european commission is in a situation which, to my knowledge, has never been seen before. we therefore ask the commission to examine the measure carefully. the commission must take an objective position, look at the letter of the treaty and not be guided by political considerations. this approach and italy's approach are at odds with the eu treaty and the commission must make it crystal clear where the line is drawn. but it is not just italy, let us be clear. what this latest episode has made clear once again is that much more active policies are needed to break the social and economic isolation of the roma and to combat discrimination against them. last wednesday the commission presented an overview of what is already possible with european instruments. i thank the commission for that. as the commission says, member states can make more and better use of the existing instruments to promote the integration of roma. but i also expect the commission to come up with concrete plans which were not there last week. several resolutions of this parliament, the last of which was adopted in january of this year, also call for this. if one thing has become clear, it is that we need not wait any longer to take the roma and the position of the roma in europe seriously. it is a very special minority which cannot be counted among the traditional minorities. it is a problem that affects europe as a whole, especially since the recent enlargements which have made many roma eu citizens. a policy of repression will not ultimately solve the problems of the roma, nor the problems and tensions which they sometimes cause in our society. it is a matter of an integrated approach, and the way in which the italian government is trying to tackle the problem is unacceptable in my view.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, digital fingerprints are a modern version of nazi methods to distinguish gypsies from other citizens. the digital fingerprints database is the most brutal revival of racism since 1940-1945 – for the fingerprints are a state-authorised first step to facilitate mass evictions of an ethnic group. most of the roma who are legally crossing the borders of an increasingly borderless europe are poor and badly educated. what they basically need is a place to stay. poverty should be solved on the spot, not by evictions, and let us keep in mind that roma are the largest minority in europe – not less than 19 member states have a population smaller than the european roma community. during communism, roma had jobs and free access to health care and education; after communism their living standards dropped. but the poverty was a fertile soil for fund-raising. the gypsy industry emerged; ngos like oxfam and care made big money as executors of projects. but roma hardly benefited, and moved to the west as soon as they were allowed to. pushed by the council of ministers to examine policies to improve roma inclusion, the commission admits the urgency of this in a document of 2 july. it refers to the european parliament resolution for a european framework strategy and it recognises its role as a coordinator – finally! in the document, under the heading ‘lessons learned’, i read ‘for effective roma inclusion, the full involvement of civil society and in particular roma ngos is a factor for success.’ roma have become partners! i hope we will soon see how this will be feasible within a structure of the commission itself. meanwhile i strongly advise the commission to study the osce action plan. why reinvent the wheel? finally, i call upon the commission to make clear to the italian government that fascist rule is in contradiction with eu law, and that roma victims who lost their property should be compensated. once italy exported fashion; nowadays it exports racism.
i would like to thank the rapporteurs and the shadow rapporteurs for their cooperation in this hellish task we are facing. although the final result is not quite what i would like, i think that we have achieved the maximum that can be achieved in these political relations. in all these cases, i think there are two things that come first: maximum consumer protection and full transparency. i believe that economic interests are entirely subordinate to these two basic concepts. maximum consumer protection means systematic application of the precautionary principle. if you don't know 100% that something is safe, don't put it on the market. it is also essential to avoid misleading consumers when they make a purchase. maximum transparency means that we do not give in to pressure from multinational food companies to keep their formulas and production methods secret. consumers have the right to know exactly what is in their food and under what conditions it was produced. in the light of these two principles, i must say that the four reports, although not always ideal, are a clear improvement on the original proposals. they are in favour of consumer protection and transparency and i will therefore support the compromise. it is a pity that we have not been able to arrange the labelling of gmos directly. finally, i would like to add one more thing. i find it very remarkable that in new proposals, such as the new foodstuffs, the commission is already launching the uniform procedure before parliament has adopted it. i think that shows, to put it mildly, a lack of respect for european democracy.
mr president, we are holding this debate today because citizens and governments are concerned about the construction of this pipeline. they are concerned about the environmental situation in the sensitive baltic sea region. but this is a huge politicization of the debate. everyone agrees that all international and environmental conventions must be respected, especially when it comes to the sensitive baltic sea area. but, mr president, the politicization that is taking place here has nothing to do with that. we must recognize that we need energy, that domestic production, for example in the netherlands, is falling more and more and that we therefore need the gas of others. that gas is from russia. it would have been much better to transport that gas overland. but that has been made impossible. now we have to go through that sensitive area. so my first point is respect for all international and environmental conventions. the second point is - and i hope not everyone has been deaf - that if there is sufficient environmental impact assessment, then permits must be issued. such licences should be issued by germany, denmark, sweden and also finland. we shall have to make a further assessment of this later, at the end of the year. another point. we also need a certain reciprocity. we depend on russian gas and the russians depend on our willingness to buy. i hope that this reciprocity will be properly regulated in the partnership agreement in the future, without all the political emotions associated with this project. that way we will be able to solve many problems in the world.
the peace process in afghanistan is a matter of life and death for citizens of the european union and beyond. they all deserve the utmost respect and the full support of the european parliament, not to mention our prayers for divine protection, because their mission remains very dangerous. the murderous attack in kabul yesterday morning is further proof of this. i would like to draw the attention of the council and the commission to two specific problems of the complex situation in afghanistan. without an effective strategy against the talibanization of northern pakistan, the stabilization of afghanistan will remain primarily a western wish. as a result, nato forces are operating with their hands tied. the second afghan problem is the urgent need to modernise agriculture. its greatest handicap is the continuing insecurity in large parts of the country. un statistics are clear: the more insecure the region, the more abundant the poppy fields are in annual bloom. i wish the council and the commission all the wisdom and perseverance in the real stabilisation of afghanistan.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that the community is not able to meet its own energy needs. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr peter liese, for the way he conducted the negotiations with the council and for the efforts he has made to achieve this result. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. the emission ceiling, for example, is higher than parliament proposed, whereas the auction rates are lower. however, this agreement is more ambitious than the original commission proposal and i think that is a good thing. the aviation sector also needs to become a cleaner sector and reduce its greenhouse gas emissions. in our fight against environmental pollution and climate change, this agreement is an important step in the right direction, especially in the light of the progress made in the climate package which we as parliament are now actively studying.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection. - i too find one minute very short, but i thank the commissioner, the minister, the members and also mr la russa for the preparation. i'll make this short. the energy market must be able to operate at european level, in particular to ensure that final consumers are supplied with energy at market prices. that's our goal. the disconnection is not an end in itself but we think it may be the way to a functioning market. if the so-called third or fourth way, as proposed by the council, can ensure a functioning market, then that is of course fine. however, in order to be ready for a possible alternative solution if that third or fourth way does not work, we have already put forward a proposal, which i hope will come to fruition tomorrow, for a so-called european network operator, which will be directed and controlled by the european union. i hope that this can be supported by both the commission and the council so that in a few years we will at least have an alternative. in october, mr president, we want to organise a round table meeting with all the interested parties and we hope that this will be a widely accepted idea.
for more than 50 years, sovereign wealth funds have been active on the financial market. in recent years, the resources of sovereign wealth funds have increased enormously in, for example, china, russia and the arab countries. the state investment funds aim to invest the state's surplus reserves in a profitable way. i think that these countries may be entitled to make the best use of their foreign exchange reserves. i am therefore in favour of the contributions from these funds. the funds improve the liquidity of financial markets and create growth and jobs. they also contribute to longer-term investment. they provide stability for the companies in which they invest. we must therefore continue to give them room to invest. however, these funds may also entail risks. we will have to look at the type of investment they make and whether they meet the requirements of transparency. the political involvement in these sovereign wealth funds is not yet fully clear. the disadvantage is also the lack of transparency and the growing concern we may have about these public investment funds. the european commission must therefore take action, and that is a good thing. otherwise, these sovereign wealth funds are dealt with nationally. i am against having too much national policy. we in europe must respond to this together. it is important to have a coordinated position at european level. i therefore welcome the response of the council and the commission to work closely together at the level of the imf and the oecd. i look forward to the guidelines that the imf, the world bank and the oecd are developing and i hope that transparency, good governance and reciprocity are incorporated into them. i think that we should limit ourselves to the public investment funds when it comes to reciprocity. we are always in favour of private investment, but we can demand reciprocity from public investment funds.
madam president, i share the concerns expressed here by many speakers about state-owned funds, but i would like to stress as well that private commercial funds, such as hedge funds and private equity, are of even more concern. this is because they often do not have a long-term-oriented focus, whereas sovereign wealth funds, together with pension funds, often have this long-term orientation and can strengthen the role of minority shareholders, as well as having a positive role in providing the market with liquidity. the point is that they are often not transparent enough in their investment strategy and intentions, and i hope that the commission will focus on this. the committee on legal affairs recently adopted a report in this parliament on the transparency of institutional investors, and there we asked the commission to come up with a directive guaranteeing common standards of transparency. i hope the commission can react to this request. my last point is about the code of conduct and the ‘comply-or-explain’ procedure. is this also an element which the commission has considered?
madam president, thank you for giving me the floor again. it is because mr purvis is saying that we might be demonising sovereign wealth funds and private equity and hedge funds, and i do not think this is the case. we also chose a very balanced approach in the oral question as it was tabled and in the resolution and i think it is wise not to ignore or deny that it is important for us as a parliament to take account of what is happening in this field. i think it is our duty, it is our right to do this, and i would not like to see this set aside as mere demonising.
president. - before the european union was established, we had a well-organized railway system in most of europe. since the end of the 19th century, this has been one of the core tasks of the state, along with the postal services. each state was responsible for its own railroads. in most cases the state had become the owner of the main or only domestic railway company. the need for this was created by the fact that the original private enterprises were mainly focused on making quick profits. they had failed to ensure the quality and continuity of the service on a sustainable basis. after they were taken over by the state, the infrastructure and the frequencies improved. the infrastructure belonged to the national railway company and thus to the state. the execution of passenger and freight transport was in the same hands. the commission has also taken the view that the community's transport policy should be based on a balanced approach to the problems of the environment and the environment. it was not necessary to have separate bureaucratic state bodies to deal with this. almost everything except the sleeping carriages and the dining carriages on international long-distance trains was nationally regulated. there was also good international cooperation. international rail travellers saw the railways as one big overarching european institution. at station counters, handwritten tickets to all possible destinations in europe and good information on foreign offers could be provided to everyone. regional cross-border and very long distance connections between non-contiguous states were available to a much greater extent than today. the system, which we can envy today, had four weak points, especially after electrification. different national grid voltages and different forms of electronic security were chosen. freight transport was hampered by checks, disconnection and transshipment at border stations. as public transport became increasingly unprofitable due to the rise of the car, border lanes were reduced and passengers had to pay an additional fee for crossing the border. after the advent of the airplane, information and ticketing for long-distance international travel were increasingly neglected. it is only in these areas that the european union's complementary action could bring about improvement. for years my group has been very irritated by the fact that the european union's interventions are not primarily aimed at these crossborder improvements but above all at greater liberalisation. to limit the damage of these changes, more accompanying bureaucracy is needed. a series of previous decisions on railways since 2001 have created established facts. in view of this new reality, my group is seeking the best possible result for the future. the results presented at second reading are relatively good. a number of considerations of subsidiarity, safety and the desire not to leave everything to agreements between the railway companies which have now become independent lead us to agree tomorrow, despite our criticism of the foregoing.
i would like to thank mr rack for his efforts. urban mobility is a complex subject for policy making at european level. a clear delimitation of responsibilities is not easy to achieve and this is reflected in the report we are debating here. on the one hand, the report states in several places that the european union has a role in the development of urban mobility plans, and in other places that urban mobility is a responsibility of european cities and municipalities, and that the principle of subsidiarity requires the european union to take only limited regulatory action. in short, weighing responsibility in this matter is difficult. i think, however, that the rapporteur, mr rack, has dealt with this matter well and has found a good wording with regard to the principle of subsidiarity. i have a few critical remarks to make. firstly, i think the report has become too detailed. this suggests that it is possible to design a european framework that can solve all the problems in all european cities. the detailed list of subjects for harmonization and a list of areas of action are very nice and well-intentioned, but i wonder if we are going much further with this. i think a general approach would have been more practical. this could have focused more on the exchange of best practices and technological developments in urban mobility. i realize that this approach is less ambitious, but i think that the priority must remain that urban mobility is a matter for european cities and municipalities. the list of subjects for harmonisation also includes some social standards. i don't think this is a good idea. social standards, such as the mobility of vulnerable road users, are highly national and therefore cannot be harmonised or standardised. despite these criticisms, i shall vote in favour of the report. the report by mrs rack has succeeded in making it clear that a safe, sustainable and well-functioning urban mobility structure is a matter for european cities and municipalities, and i think this is the great merit of the report. the european union can lend a helping hand by providing financial support and exchanging relevant information, but a regulatory european union is neither necessary nor desirable in this context. i would like to conclude, however, by saying that in all this we must not lose sight of the fact that the responsibility for the development of urban transport systems remains with the european cities. they will have to develop systems and work well with all the authorities concerned. finally, i would point out that the success of these transport systems depends on the participation of the citizens. they also have a responsibility to contribute to the objective of urban mobility plans: sustainable, safe and well-functioning urban transport.

- i'm the reporter. - ladies and gentlemen, today we are voting on clarifying and simplifying european social security coordination and thus also on clarifying the annex. the european parliament, the council and the commission have worked together to simplify the proposal so that european citizens can better understand the complex rules governing social security coordination. i would like to begin is true, ladies and gentlemen, that the process has been long, but it has certainly been a successful one. we now have a good compromise, which all the member states and all the european institutions, including the european parliament, can follow. the guiding principle in assessing the amendments has always been the conviction that changes to the current coordination system should in no way lead to a reduction in the rights of citizens. one very important example of this is the deletion of annex iii, since annex iii allows member states to restrict the rights of their citizens. with this report we have committed ourselves to a europe which gives more rights to its citizens in as many areas as possible. the beauty of european cooperation is that it enables european countries to work together to care for their citizens. social justice is an important part of that, and it doesn't stop at the border. citizens must be able to rely on the guarantee of their social rights, even across national borders. the single market allows citizens to move freely within the european union. we want to encourage that. the citizens must be able to count on their social security, their pensions, wherever they live or work, and good care, not only for themselves but also for their families, throughout europe. that is the true european cooperation.

in writing. − today the proposal of mr. lambsdorff for a european parliament recommendation to the council on the eu priorities for the 63rd session of the un general assembly was adopted without a vote in plenary. not only is this practice – that is enabled by ‘rule 90’ – very dubious, it also gives the false impression that the whole ep agrees with the content of the report, which is certainly not the case. we strongly reject the recommendation that the current status of the lisbon treaty would call for a ‘reorganisation and expansion of the offices of the council and the commission in new york and geneva in view of the increased powers and responsibilities that the eu’s representatives will be expected to exercise with a view to ratification of the lisbon treaty.’ not only is this an insult to the irish voters that rejected the lisbon treaty by a wide majority during the referendum, it is also an attempt to interpret the lisbon treaty in such a way that it would ‘confer legal personality upon the eu’, thus making it a superstate.
in writing. the two largest groups prefer a two-party system. the main feature of such a system is that both parties have one common interest, namely that a second, third and fourth party do not have access to political decision-making, and that they remain completely irrelevant to the voters. only the largest fractions count; the protest and the alternative must be excluded. in the event that others nevertheless make their way into parliament by way of exception, they are preferred to be allocated the least attractive place possible, as individuals with restricted rights. some members of this parliament do not belong to a political group. this is often the result of coercion by others. the same pressure forces other members to join a group with which they partly disagree. the group of the european people's party has a right to vote in the european parliament. the reason is that you cannot form a group of less than 20 thousand or more of your own kind. for democratic input from all existing opinions in society, it is better that this threshold should disappear, rather than raising it to 25 or 30 and introducing rigid rules against dissidents. i absolutely reject that.
in writing. − cities are densely populated, have little outdoor space and have large transport flows over relatively short distances. the scarcity of space does not allow for heavy traffic, and noise and air pollution are reasons for striving to reduce the number of cars in the city. of course, the city must also be accessible to firefighters, police, ambulances, movers and transport for people with reduced mobility, but the scarce outdoor space must be available especially to pedestrians, cyclists, the tram, children playing and green areas. only then will the city be livable. the text on which we are voting today does not make that clear choice, but merely attempts to reconcile conflicting interests and ideas. fortunately, the eu has no jurisdiction in this matter. the only thing the eu can do is to contribute to the transfer of best practices, good experiences gained in the meantime with improvements. these improvements are not only important for the city where they have already been applied but also as an example for others. examples of this are the selective access of cars to central london, the new tram networks in strasbourg and bordeaux or the long-established car-free inner city of groningen. the eu will unfortunately have little to contribute to this on the basis of this report.
on wednesday afternoon, 18 june, i was to meet three honest, peaceful chinese citizens in a hotel in beijing. just an hour before we met, i learned that two of them had been arrested by the security services and the third had been ordered by the authorities to cancel our meeting. more than 31 hours later, both detainees were released. the official version is that they were not arrested and therefore not released, but only checked. in any event, the chinese authorities apparently wished to prevent any personal contact between a member of the european parliament and these three nationals. i can understand her disgusting attitude, though. beijing could hardly expect an olympic propaganda story from three prominent representatives of the thriving protestant house churches. in the run-up to the big sporting spectacle, members of unregistered protestant churches in particular are facing increasing religious persecution. the chinese progressive leadership has chosen to remain silent on the harrowing details of this repression. - it makes sense. after all, what glory is to be gained from the forced labor of a simple house church minister in beijing? for three years he had to work for ten to twelve hours a day manufacturing soccer balls for the upcoming olympic games. speaking of the chinese form of forced labor! furthermore, what should we think of chinese authorities who have had members of house churches arrested because they volunteered to help the victims of the terrible earthquake in sichuan province out of inner conviction? i didn't get to see him. mr president, in short, i believe that beijing has long since extinguished the olympic flame by its flagrant violation of fundamental rights, well before the olympic games began in its own country!

president, after the large influx of accessions in 2004 and 2007, enlargement is stagnating. croatia has to wait until 2011, macedonia enters at the earliest in 2014 and the other five western balkan countries follow even later. negotiations are under way with turkey, but it is uncertain whether accession will be possible in the coming decades. with all the states that were formerly part of the soviet union's sphere of influence now in the european union, it seems to be tired of enlargement. the debate on enlargement and neighbourhood policy is based on two different views. in one view, the european union is a world power and a superstate which is gradually attracting more and more decisions from the member states. this superstate wants to make its immediate neighbours dependent on it, but does not give those countries influence as equal partners within the union. countries which are not sufficiently adapted or whose economies are weak are kept outside the union, but are nevertheless drawn into its sphere of influence without any participation. my group does not want to go that way. the other view is that it is a cooperation in diversity and equality. the union is open to any european state that wishes to join and that meets criteria such as democracy and human rights. such a union does not seek to impose decisions that have no impact on public opinion in the member states, but seeks to solve the transnational problems of its citizens by working together. such a union is the most useful and has the best chance of survival in the long term.
on behalf of the ppe-de group the fisheries agreement with mauritania, which was concluded on 1 august 2006, had to be renewed this year. on 14 december last year the commission suddenly proposed not to renew the fisheries agreement because the fishing opportunities it offered had not been fully exploited by the european fleet, especially for smaller pelagic species. it was a big surprise for us. at that time, partly at the instigation of parliament itself, the negotiations were resumed. the new protocol which the commission is now presenting to us is a very strict restriction on the old one. not only are the quantities of fish which have not been fully fished in previous years reduced, but all fish species show a sharp reduction: 25% for squid, between 10 and 50% for demersal species and 43% for pelagic species, while the compensation to mauritania remains the same or is reduced only to a limited extent in subsequent years. we wonder whether this restriction was really necessary and on what basis it is based, especially as the fisheries sector is currently in great difficulty due to the fuel price crisis and the reduced fishing opportunities. the commission has therefore decided to suspend the fishing authorizations for the period from 1 january 1999 to 31 december 2000. the fishing industry must be able to exploit every fishing opportunity that exists at this moment. for example, it would be possible to create a bypass to mauritania for tuna fishing in the mediterranean. the dutch pelagic fleet is also affected by the restrictions now proposed, particularly for cod, herring and mackerel. this is about mauritania. i should like to make a very brief comment on the proposals made by the commission. unlike the proposals on mauritania, i am very pleased with them. i think there are many points of reference in the adjustment of the fleet to the fuel crisis and i hope that these new measures will help to avoid a cold reorganization of the sector.
in writing. the pvda delegation voted in favour of the agreement with mauritania, not because it is a good agreement, but because it is a small improvement on the existing situation (less catches). in general, we are against these agreements because they deprive developing countries of their sources of food and income. in the case of mauritania, it is even more distressing that development money is being used to support fisheries interests. what a disgrace!

madam president, this is just to react to the british sceptics because i agree with them that we should not make legislation on the basis of the lowest common denominator. the thing is, however, that you then have to think about how you want to make legislation, because if we also agree that we want to have a common approach in catching criminals, then you cannot make legislation through unanimity. that is why everything is now stuck in council. but i also expect their help in coming up with a decision-making process with qualified majority voting (qmv), because otherwise we are stuck. either you isolate yourself and do not want to cooperate in the field of justice, or we go over to qmv, because that is the only way we can make really substantial, meaningful legislation.
