the rapporteur believes that the european sanctions policy should be evaluated first and reassessed second (see paragraph 11). european action through the imposition of sanctions can only have constructive value if the human rights situation in the partner country concerned is actually improving. a thorough evaluation is therefore certainly important to examine the effectiveness of sanctions policy. the council and the commission must, however, enter into a vigorous debate on the use of sanctions policy. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. i wonder whether unanimous sanctions are even possible, given the diversity of member states within the union, and i am thinking here of the case of cuba, but also of zimbabwe. in view of the possibility of reassessing the sanctions policy, which mr flautre also advocates, it is doubtful whether the sanctions policy is such an appropriate instrument. in short, to improve the situation in the union's partner countries in the field of security and human rights, it is important to think about the right instrument. for example, can serious violations of religious freedom in china and india give rise to real formal action by the european union? the question of reviewing european action in cases of this kind is more important at the moment than any premature reassessment of sanctions policy. i hope, moreover, that if a number of amendments are not adopted, i shall support the flautre report.
mr president, sanctions were a suitable means of gradually putting an end to the worst forms of colonial domination and to the apartheid regimes in south africa and southern rhodesia. in the past it has always been the opponents of change who have rejected sanctions and have mainly called attention to the negative side effects. on the other hand, the representatives of the poor and the oppressed in those countries pointed out that they accepted the disadvantages of the boycott as the price to be paid for their liberation. since the long economic boycott against the atrocious regime of saddam hussein in iraq, we know that such a boycott can sometimes also lead to the inhabitants of such a country becoming isolated, afraid of the outside world and supporting their government. a boycott certainly does not help to impose the wishes of the outside world against the wishes of domestic public opinion. it is and remains a suitable instrument to support the suppressed public opinion and the struggle for improvement in a country. if we make that distinction clearly, sanctions will remain a useful tool for achieving greater equality and democracy.

president, commissioner, rapporteur, ladies and gentlemen, i think that there is now a proposal which is better than what was presented last time. there is much more room for decentralizing responsibilities. the role of the member states is also increasing, and i would like to discuss this again as a member of the committee on regional policy. i am surprised that the position of the ports is clearly defined, but that at the same time the necessary connection with the hinterland is not provided for in certain situations. i would like to give the example of antwerp here in belgium and the ruhr area. for years there has been talk of the activation of the iron rhine, the rail link that would finally put an end to the endless line of containers that you have on the motorway in front of you. this is ridiculous! it can be done in other ways, but this is primarily the responsibility of the national states. the same applies in the netherlands. in the netherlands we have laid the betweel line from antwerp to the ruhr area, a multi-billion investment. what do you think of my surprise? the german border is lacking the optimum connection! that's something we should talk about. on the one hand, if we see the growth of transport through the ports, these arteries, in global connectivity, we must take a more rigorous integrated approach. i know that the european commission is working on a document on territorial cohesion which must be achieved not only in western europe, the old member states, but also in the new member states and beyond. there should be more control in there. not hard legislation and regulations but a way to test each other on those responsibilities. no money from the structural funds. billions are going into port development and clusters, without proper alignment with the hinterland. this is my cue. i would like to ask the commissioner whether this is the main point of the green paper on territorial cohesion which will be presented in october.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. often, that transport is done in the most environmentally unfriendly way: the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the information it receives. the enormous growth in freight transport, which is still continuing, is due to the fact that its costs have been falling. it is not good for the environment, for safety, for working conditions and for animal welfare. my group therefore favours limiting transport growth and shifting as much as possible of the necessary remaining transport to rail and inland waterways. we find this wish expressed in a moderate form in the proposals of the european commission and in the supplementary proposals of the rapporteur, mr cramer. we do object to the possible displacement of passenger trains in favour of freight. in order not to interfere with each other, the growth of freight transport requires complementary infrastructure where necessary. i would also like to point out the problems encountered in the launch of the european single security system ertms. investments in these are useful for the future, but cause problems in the short term.
i would like to thank mr cramer for his work as rapporteur. the report looks good. i would like to stress the following points in the report. the transport of goods must be free, fair and clean, and we are working hard on this in this parliament. earlier this year, the end of the cabotage restriction was in sight. that was a good start. this report continues on that basis, and that is excellent. the rapporteur rightly points out that urban goods logistics needs a specific approach. it is of the utmost importance that european cities become cleaner. i therefore think that automated speed adjustment in cities is an excellent means and i hope that the european commission will support measures and make proposals to ensure that freight transport in cities does not have to constantly slow down and speed up. this is how we make freight transport faster and cleaner.
president. - one of the points made in the president-in-office's speech on this subject was that it is a very sensitive matter. i'm all over that. in fact, i believe i am the first speaker to say that i do not see the need for such a directive. i do not see why europe should have an additional directive. why do i think that? firstly, in the field of soil, we already have a lot of directives that affect soil health and the environment. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's water resources are not used for the production of waste water. all of them affect soil health. in europe - and this is the case in france and elsewhere - we are suffering from too many administrative regulations. the average farmer takes more time to fill out forms on all sorts of things than just doing his farm work. if another directive is added to it, that is too much of a good thing. we must first see what the result so far of the current directives is, whether they are not sufficient, and whether they do not contribute sufficiently to a healthy soil. the groundwater directive will not enter into force until 2009 and it is completely unnecessary to introduce a new directive before then. the commission has made a proposal and has calculated what the benefits would be. what i've missed in that whole calculation is what it costs in administrative burden for the stakeholders to do it all. again, too much time is wasted on paperwork, filling out forms, attending meetings and i don't know what else. what could possibly happen right now? the commission can play a very important role in the exchange of experience. there are certain countries which are already very far along in the process of making the soil healthy and there are other countries where this is not the case. the countries that have done so so far have done so without any help from europe. why not use these good examples for the countries which are still having problems at the moment? i think that we are facing too much administrative work at the moment and that europe and the european union in general are not making themselves more popular with the citizens by stacking one regulation on top of another and saying, 'you can do it yourself'. no, let us limit it as much as possible and take as an example the results in the member states which could serve as an example for the other countries.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i speak on behalf of the alde group, with regard to the kirkhope report. pursuant to rule 168, the alde group would like to make a request for referral back to committee. to be clear, there is no intention of breaking up the compromise package with the council as such, but my group does believe that a more extensive debate on the definition of a parent carrier should take place. the commission is working on a formal notice, which is most welcome. however, that does not give us the 100% clarity we need at this very moment. a proper debate should be allowed for, as well as decent consultation of our legal services. there is no rush to put this matter to the vote at this very moment.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. discrimination and violence are a relic of a time before the judeo-christian-islamic civilization, when the equality of all people was not yet assumed. tanzania has 150,000 inhabitants who, due to a lack of the color pigment melanin, have red eyes, pale skin, and light hair. they are seen as a product of witchcraft. many of these people are killed, after which their skins and other body parts are stripped off and used in magical rituals. because of the large number of albinos, tanzania is the main supplier of human body parts for all of africa. these terrible practices cannot be ended without active education among the common people in tanzania and across africa, better medical care and better access for albinos to leadership positions. the tanzanian government is taking measures, including the registration of all albinos, to protect them. without a change in the perception of the position of albinos, that registration could also be misused in the future to track down and eradicate these people. in europe we had a bad experience in the 1940s with the registration of endangered populations.
on behalf of the pse group. – mr president, i would say to mr lauk that he may support the report that mr dăianu and i drafted on european supervision, but there was a lack of support for it from the natuurlijk typisch een voorbeeld van de afgelopen tijd waarin je ziet dat de commissie achter de feiten aanloopt. wij hadden veel eerder al aan de orde gesteld dat er op het punt van stock lending, het lenen en uitlenen van aandelen, iets zou moeten gebeuren omdat dat misbruikt werd in campagnes van hedge funds tegen bedrijven. nu eindelijk hebben de financiële markttoezichthouders, een aantal afzonderlijke toezichthouders in de afzonderlijke landen, dat gedaan. het gaat hier niet om een gecoördineerde europese actie. dergelijke initiatieven moeten dan gewoon weer uit de nationale kanalen komen. wij hadden dit kunnen vóór zijn en hadden wellicht ook een aantal defaults, een aantal banken of verzekeringsmaatschappijen die omgevallen zijn, kunnen voorkomen als wij hiervoor adequate maatregelen gehad hadden. het illustreert ook nog eens dat dit soort zaken over de sectoren heen gaan en dat sectortoezicht alleen voor de banken, alleen voor de verzekeringsmaatschappen, alleen voor de effectenhandel, niet volstaat. dergelijk toezicht moet je echt bundelen en ervoor zorgen dat op die effecten over en weer geanticipeerd wordt en dat voorkomen wordt dat dit soort zaken plaatsvinden. het andere punt zijn de beloningssystemen. in het verslag hebben wij aangegeven dat het van groot belang is dat die aan banden gelegd worden en dat in de aandeelhoudersvergaderingen moeten kunnen gestemd worden over het beloningsbeleid. ik heb met vreugde gezien dat mijn democratische collega's in het congres bij de discussie deze week over het noodfonds dat er in vs komt, willen dat een van de voorwaarden voor het lenen uit dat fonds is dat de exorbitante salarissen en bonussen van managers aan banden gelegd moeten worden. het zou toch te gek zijn dat goldman sachs en morgan stanley, die zich nu onder dat toezicht stellen en ook gebruik willen maken van die publieke middelen, doorgaan met die miljoenenbonussen. het is gepast is dat wij in europa ook verder gaan dan de aanbeveling van een paar jaar geleden. ik wil de commissaris vragen wat hij op dit punt gaat ondernemen, want het is echt tijd voor actie.
the rapporteur. - mr president, the un is currently meeting in new york on the millennium development goals. if nothing changes, we won't make it in 2015. if we do that, we will not have reduced poverty by half, we will not have dramatically reduced maternal mortality, we will not have all children going to primary school. in fact, there are calls to set aside the mdgs. it's not working. it's called "aid fatigue". it's another word for cynicism and short-sighted self-interest. too little help can't get you anywhere. and yes, aid projects often fail. and that's the problem. too little help is not enough. it's like a football coach giving his team only right-handed shoes. after dozens of games, he says, you're losing to everybody, this is never gonna work. i'm done with this, you can keep the shoes and figure it out on your own. there are plenty of countries where aid is successful. the scale of the aid is important. in rwanda, a lot of aid has been given after the genocide. the country itself has risen admirably. there is much criticism - and i have it for kagame - but still, after the horror of 1994, rwanda is giving a lesson in life to the whole world. however, without the aid, this economic growth would have been less significant. in mozambique too, aid is successful and there are many other examples. since the millennium development goals were set, 29 million children have found their way to primary school. if the world would just stick to its own agreements, there would be no food crisis. if every rich country spent 0.7% of its gross national product on development aid, the number of malnourished children would not rise again after years of decline. the european parliament will therefore use this report to make a strong appeal to the member states to honour their own commitments, especially those member states which are lagging behind, such as france, germany, the united kingdom and italy, to name only the large member states. the aid must be increased until 2015, in a clear timetable, in equal steps. no backloading, because a late catch-up after a series of small steps up, deprives the poor countries of 17 billion euros in aid until 2015. one thing must be clear: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. it takes more than that. the european union must continue to invest in peace and security, good governance and respect for human rights. in kenya, thanks to european pressure, this has been achieved, with raila odinga now prime minister. in eastern chad, the situation is much more difficult for eufor. but it's essential now to start tying private money flows to public goals. access to credit is the key. loans are rarely reserved for the poorest and that needs to change. with development as the goal, equal access for women and men, no usury, investing in opportunities for small entrepreneurs who are strong networkers, connected to local organizations. private banks do not automatically think of these conditions and are less likely to grant loans to women than to men. the european union can have a huge impact here with credit guarantees. the european investment bank will also have to provide much more support to micro-credit institutions than it does now. it is only then that people will really have the opportunity to demonstrate their capacities and build their own existence as autonomous, independent citizens. the poor countries must also be given more influence in the imf. in addition, more money must be made available for the effects of climate change with a share of the auctions of co2 allowances. the polluter pays, the poor countries are not responsible for global warming. the aid will also have to be directed towards renewable energy. development policy is at the heart of the european union. the european union must be a solidary player on the world stage, each member state in its own way, but in a coherent way and with 0.7% of gross national product. that is millennium goal 8, and fulfilling that eighth promise brings those other goals a long way closer. finally, during the reading of this text, 80 people died of starvation and 50 children under five died of easily treatable diseases.
i am convinced that the council is genuinely concerned with improving the safety of citizens and that it also attaches importance to civil rights. you may be very pleased with the reports before you, but if you read them carefully you will see that we reach a very different conclusion from you. we think that the proposals you have adopted are simply not up to par and that there is a risk of infringement of civil rights. how is it that we come to a different judgment? first of all, traditional parliaments have more regard for civil rights than governments and so there is also a problem in decision-making. but parliament particularly wants to look at the effects of these decisions on society in the longer term. if we look at the relationship between government and citizens historically, we see that government has a monopoly on violence and that citizens have fundamental rights that government should not touch, except when necessary, effective and proportionate. but if the citizen is too often confronted with government action where it is not necessary or justified, his confidence and therefore his cooperation with that government will be diminished and then we have a long-term security problem. trust comes on foot, but it goes on horseback. in my view, the proposal on data protection does not provide the protection it is intended to provide and the council is moving towards the extension of the framework decision on a slippery slope. let me begin with mrs roure's report. i would like to thank mrs roure very warmly for all the years she has been guiding this report through parliament. i have a number of questions to put to the council. the proposal concerns european cooperation in the field of justice and police, i.e. services concerned with security. but i understand that it now says - perhaps you can contradict that - that the council does not consider itself bound by these proposals when essential national security interests are at stake. what interests are involved and can you give an example of a case in which you would like to throw this framework decision in the trash? another point is the sensitive data, which mrs roure herself has already mentioned. i can still understand why you'd want some of the data. but can the council explain to me what it can benefit from knowing whether someone is a member of a trade union? just give an example of when it is interesting to know whether someone is a member of the fnv in the netherlands. is that an indication of recalcitrant behavior? or in what case could this possibly play a role? the sex life? is this about active pedophilia? yes, that would be a crime, and then, of course, that would be recorded. but what kind of things do you want to know more about? as regards the transfer of data to third countries, i remember a hilarious moment during the german presidency when a council representative described the need to transfer data to iran very quickly. that was a moment when the whole house was looking at this man in amazement. he couldn't have meant it, passing information to iran! i therefore ask the council member to give us a guarantee that sensitive data will never, ever be sent to iran. and in addition to that guarantee, could you give me the article number you're basing it on? although i think the level of protection is insufficient, i think the objective is good: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council should be consulted on the future of the european union. a key word to improve this cooperation is trust, and here too it is about trust. i blame the council for not doing enough to increase that trust and therefore cooperation. trust must be based on something, such as substantial data protection and rights of suspects worthy of our rule of law. but then you don't care. you've been working on the procedural rights of suspects for my entire term and we still have nothing. and where you can seriously help cooperation, you leave it behind. instead you have come up with the wild framework decision on the fight against terrorism. mrs lefrançois has written a very good report on this and i would like to thank her very much for it. my question remains: what problem does the council want to solve? the incitement to violence is prohibited in all member states, and rightly so. but now, incitement or provocation must be punishable as well. and when is it provocation or provocation? when someone writes that the united states is a rogue state, an axis of evil that must be fought? if that person also writes, "whoever is not for us is against us", is that provocation? if a western man makes an anti-islam film with the intention of hurting, does he incite an attack? or am i provoking you? ambiguous legislation is bad legislation. if you want to make incitement to violence a european crime, you have me on your side. but not with this. when you put all the language versions together, it's not even clear whether it's "provoking" or "inciting", provocation or incitement. a woman may dress provocatively, but that does not make her a rape-invited assailant. the first paragraph of article 1 is particularly worrying in this respect, stating that it does not matter whether or not terrorist offences are directly advocated for conviction. this is going way too far for my group.
mr president, to start with, on a point of order, i would like to ask the presidency of this house to write to the french presidency and indicate that we find it unacceptable that the french presidency is not available for the whole duration of such an important debate. voorzitter, terrorisme is niet uitgevonden op 11 september 2001. het bestond altijd al en zoals de vorige spreekster net al heeft opgemerkt, wordt in het trend report van europol inderdaad aangegeven dat de overgrote meerderheid van de aanslagen niet van moslimextremistische achtergrond is, maar dat het gaat om separatistisch geweld, extreem-rechts en extreem-links geweld. wat wél nieuw is sinds 2001 is dat regeringen wereldwijd de strijd tegen terrorisme aangrijpen om rechten en vrijheden van burgers in te perken. nu ben ik helemaal vóór samenwerking in de strijd tegen booswichten. ik sluit mij helemaal aan bij de woorden van manfred weber. heel vaak worden echter maatregelen genomen, zoals het inzamelen van persoonsgegevens, voor doeleinden die helemaal niets te maken hebben met terrorisme. zo worden bijvoorbeeld de pnr-gegevens gebruikt voor controle op immigratie of het bestrijden van gewone misdaad, wat op zich ook heel legitiem is, maar laten wij het beestje dan ook bij de naam noemen. de plechtige verklaringen van de raad over de burgerrechten en de privacy klinken nogal hol als de raad niet eens aanwezig is, maar vooral ook niet bereid is om de aanbevelingen van het europees parlement over te nemen, zoals neergelegd met name in het verslag-roure. misschien moet de raad dan ophouden met krokodillentranen te huilen over het ierse "nee". dan heb ik ten slotte nog twee concrete vragen. ik wilde graag van de commissie -want de raad is er niet - in writing. − it is regrettable that the report by rasmussen on hedge funds and private equity has been watered down following the compromise negotiations between the three largest groups in the parliament. it is also regrettable that the amendments tabled by the greens and the gue/ngl group, which were directly taken from the rasmussen draft report, were not adopted during the plenary vote. in spite of this, we decided to support the report in the final vote. this is due to the urgent need and necessity to counteract harmful financial speculation and instability on the markets. in this respect, the report could be seen as a step in the right direction.
in writing. - the vvd members of the european parliament voted in favour of the ebner report, partly because it gives a good impression of the specific problems of agriculture in mountain areas. the report also calls for the establishment of a european union-wide system of controls on imports of agricultural products from third countries.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's maritime transport policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. businessmen who want to keep their costs as low as possible may be tempted to use old, dangerous ships. they are a danger to the crew and to others and can be a serious threat to the environment. the poor working conditions, which are made possible by the choice of registration under a flag other than that of the country of ownership and the actual base of operations, also lead to disadvantages. operating costs can also be reduced by dumping ship's waste and cargo residues on board. to prevent this, it is necessary to ensure that ships of malicious operators can be definitively denied access to european ports, that anchorages outside ports are also excluded for these ships and that sufficient inspections are carried out to determine what is wrong. the port state's obligations under the international labour organisation's maritime labour convention must be strictly observed, the polluter must pay and all the rules must apply at night. the council's attempts to give such undertakings more flexibility than parliament wanted at first reading have unacceptable consequences. the council rejected the majority of parliament's 23 amendments on the inquiry into shipping accidents, which could jeopardise the independence of the inquiry. the council has also been slowing down on the protection of passengers on board ships since it refused to follow the athens convention in 2003. the common position of june 2008 limits the information obligation and liability. in the case of shipwrecks, the council is not following the proposals of the commission and parliament, which aim to ensure that a ship in distress is always received in time at an airport and that crews are protected from punishment for negligence which is not their fault. all dangerous situations and abuses in shipping must be brought to an end as soon as possible. it is therefore important that parliament, in relation to the council, also in the second reading, should stick to the line which has been set out earlier.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). although it is unfortunate that we are missing a couple of proposals, i am satisfied with the content of the package as it now stands before us and i am grateful to the rapporteurs for the work they have done. there is one part of the package that i am not satisfied with. the costa report on the liability of passenger carriers has two paragraphs which ensure that the same level of liability is imposed on inland waterway passenger carriers as on passenger carriers on the high seas. this is completely undesirable. first, there is no need. there are hardly any known cases of accidents involving passenger transport on inland waterways. and, in addition, these two paragraphs mean the end of a large proportion of the companies which transport passengers on inland waterways, because they cannot afford the associated sky-high insurance premiums, if there is any insurer who wants to offer them insurance at such a level of liability. the commission has not yet received any comments from the french authorities on the compatibility of the aid with the internal market. it seems to me clear and logical that such companies are not subject to the same level of liability as large carriers of thousands of passengers by sea. we must not make a fool of ourselves by imposing on the carriers of passengers on inland waterways a towering and extremely expensive level of liability. i am also still very much dissatisfied with the way in which these paragraphs have been put back into the text. the chairman of the committee on transport should never have declared these amendments admissible, because there was already agreement between the council and parliament on this matter at first reading. for the reasons stated above, i have asked for a majority vote on paragraphs 9 and 20. i hope and expect that many colleagues will support me in rejecting these paragraphs.
mr president, mr clark, you really must look into this proposal and not just talk about other companies, because there is nothing to be gained by what you are saying. you've got it all wrong. this is about maritime security. i do not want to spend any more of my two precious minutes on this, but i will now turn to the actual contents of this package. it's been a wild ride, this maritime security package. we, as the european parliament, are putting our heels in the sand, because we want the whole package. but it seems that the council is also putting its foot down. i am the shadow rapporteur on the independent accident inquiry and the council has been very unhelpful so far. but if we both show some flexibility and add a little water to the french wine, we should be able to reach an agreement before the end of this year. the epp's main point in the independent accident investigation is that the investigation is genuinely independent. the council's position on this point is not adequate at the moment. finally, there must be one final person responsible for an inquiry, because if three member states each conduct their own inquiry, it is very unclear and not transparent for the public. so one person is ultimately responsible. thirdly, we must not only investigate rare accidents which are widely reported in the media. the epp also calls for the commission to be given the power to adopt legislative measures to ensure that the directive is implemented. finally, i agree with what mr blokland has said about the proposal in the costa report to include inland waterway transport in the liability regime. the epp has been and remains against this. so, council, stick to that point and i hope that tomorrow amendments nos 11 and 20 will not receive a qualified majority.
i am very much against the suggestion made by mr costa that there is a link between the deaths on the seine and the fact that the epp does not want to support his proposals. that is a link which mr costa is not allowed to make, so i am strongly opposed to it.
i would also like to thank mrs caroline lucas and mr zaleski for the efforts they have made to produce this resolution. unfortunately, the trade in waste wood is still widespread in europe. the european commission's 2003 flegt action plan, which was designed to prevent illegal logging from entering europe, has had only a limited impact. it is therefore regrettable that the european commission has still not come forward with the new proposals. because bad behaviour by importers still seems to pay off because there is little action against illegal trade and no sanctions. responsible timber importers are therefore literally and figuratively paying a high price for complying with environmental and safety standards, as much cheaper illegal timber is still widely traded. the timber industry itself has already taken good initiatives, such as certification. we must support this with binding legislation, appropriate within the wto framework. i therefore also advocate that existing certification systems, which have been developed by the sector itself and also by ngos, should be recognised. we do not think that what mrs lucas wants, namely the creation of a new european institution which would also involve a lot of bureaucracy, is necessary. of course, measures must be taken on the spot to combat illegal logging in the region. we also expect the european commission to propose sanctions which should also have a preventive effect on importers of wood. because if we do not sanction companies that are acting illegally, we run the risk that this illegality will continue to pay off, and that is what we must get rid of.
president. - commissioner wallström has just spoken about reaching out to the citizen. the most visible part of the european community's action is cohesion policy, with thousands of small-scale programmes in regions and cities. we are also going to be actively reaching out to the citizens in the coming period, i am thinking in particular of the open days of regions and cities in the coming weeks here in brussels, but also of a wide range of activities in the member states. so far so good. i'm not sure. but there are also adjustments that need to be made, and i will mention two of them. first of all, there is the parliament's fervent desire to make the european solidarity fund more flexible. citizens expect quick action in the event of natural disasters, floods. we have a view on this and the european council has been blocking that debate for a few years now. let us at least see that it is unleashed. secondly, the green paper on territorial cohesion, expected in october, should lay the foundations for cohesion policy beyond 2013: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we are seeing a concentration in a number of regions in europe, which is a consequence of globalisation and should be so. at the same time, however, we must have a vision of how to achieve a balanced development in europe in all areas with their own qualities in the broad range of agricultural development, r & d, ecology, etc. the single market has brought many good things and it is almost complete. however, cohesion policy is now entering a new phase. the green paper will lay the foundations for this, followed by legislative steps in the new period.
the joint motion for a resolution rightly addresses the precarious position of the christian minority in india. i would like to stress to the council and the commission that this situation, particularly in the state of orissa, is hopeless unless the causes are really tackled at their political and socioeconomic roots. on august 23 this year, saraswati, a local leader of the radical hindu vhp in orissa, was reportedly killed by maoists. the christian minority, however, had to pay the price. hindu nationalists, armed with clubs, axes and torches, went hunting for their christian fellow citizens and held horrific meetings for weeks, even in the refugee camps. this violence seems purely religious, but it has an obvious political component. the point is that in orissa, but also elsewhere in india, the hindu nationalist bjp is gaining strong support today. in its ideology there is no place for religious minorities, let alone a christian minority. council and commission, i have just heard that you are meeting the indian prime minister, mr singh, in marseille on 29 september. prime minister singh has called the outrages in orissa a national disgrace. you must speak to him about it in marseille. you must set an agenda and discuss this point in detail, namely what happens to religious intolerance and to the persecution of christians, because it is unacceptable to maintain a strategic partnership in this way.
mr president, like europe, india is a subcontinent of many peoples and languages, where efforts are being made to put an end to a past of war and oppression. yet india still has torture and death sentences and violence against the people of kashmir, who have never had the referendum that was needed after partition in 1947 to decide which state they want to belong to. in europe, the strategic partnership with india, agreed in 2004, must not be a pretext for turning a blind eye to the discrimination and the lack of rights of 170 million untouchable people who do not belong to any caste. the prime minister of india in 2007 rightly compared the plight of these dalits to apartheid in south africa. many dalits have converted to christianity or islam. the extremist opposition party bjp wants to use the upcoming elections to mobilize hindus against christians and muslims and to force dalits to resign themselves to the fact that they will be forever poor and without rights. how do we help the indian government to stop the violence and how do we ensure that eu aid reaches the dalits after the floods?

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 2052/91 on the common organization of the market in fruit and vegetables (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). these trans fatty acids exist in natural and industrial forms and are found in many foods. although several scientific studies have produced different results, the majority of studies indicate that there is no difference in risk between naturally occurring trans fatty acids in food and artificially added industrial trans fatty acids. both types of trans fatty acids are equally harmful when consumed in excess. i therefore think it is not appropriate to maintain this distinction in the present proposal, hence my amendment. it is also very difficult to eliminate all trans fats without other risks to public health. a ban on trans fats leads to an increase in the concentration of saturated fats, according to the uk food standard agency, among others. those saturated fatty acids are at least as harmful as trans fats. on average, humans ingest far more saturated fatty acids than the world health organization recommends as a standard. i therefore tabled amendments to limit the amount of saturated fatty acids as well, and at the same time i propose not to call for a ban on trans fatty acids, as is now stated in article 32 of the report. what would be possible, for example, is that we set a limit of, say, 2% of trans fatty acids in total energy intake. this is technically feasible and to some extent already in practice. we must avoid a trade-off between trans fats and saturated fats which does not improve the health of the consumer.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, madam commissioner, madam minister, last week i attended a presentation by my danish colleague, karen riis-jørgensen, of a new website with 'patient rights' in all the languages of the union. there was also a danish lady who had not been there for a while. she had breast cancer and didn't make it through the danish system because she had the wrong amount of tumors. she would have been treated if she had five tumors, but she had seven, and she didn't meet the criteria. so somehow she had to have two tumors removed before she could be helped. in short, an endless magic trick. eventually she was able to get help in germany. she did, she borrowed money from family, friends, and went to germany where she was helped. now the seeds are gone. she's cured, insofar as you can say you're cured of cancer. the battle that someone like that, that one person, has to fight with a system at a time when you're already sick, at a time when you're as weak as you can be, is inhuman. and then we say, we put the system above the patient. i find that totally unacceptable! in the end, the danes did pay for a good part of the treatment, so everything has turned out positively. but what happened to this lady is not an exception, it happens all too often. the european commission's proposal is therefore a major step forward in helping these patients and my group would like to support it. we must also ensure that this debate does not become an ideological debate. this is not another health services directive. this is not about how we are going to reform health in the european union. this is not a question of whether or not to let the free market run wild on health. it is not a question of subsidiarity for me either. it is not a question of whether the member states or the union win. no, it's about whether or not the patient wins here. otherwise, we'll just leave it at that. we must certainly talk about all these other things, perhaps have a good debate about them during the election campaign, but that is not the point, not for me and not for my own group. we do not want to harmonize health, and this is not the time for that, and perhaps it is not possible at all. we must, however, learn to exploit the opportunities offered by the european union, the economies of scale we have, so that we can finally have truly specialised help for rare diseases. we have had this opportunity for many years, of course, but we can now really make use of it and we must seize it. in conclusion, last week there was also a meeting in this house hosted by mr dagmar berend, where the european patient forum presented its manifesto. i'm glad that the patients are also fisting at the door, because we need the patients' input. we are now going to decide democratically where the lawyers have already made a headway. but now we are taking the decision where it really belongs, namely with the people's representatives.

i am here because i am concerned. we are talking today about the european schools and i am grateful to mrs hennicot for that. i would like to draw commissioner kallas' attention to a specific aspect of european schools, namely religious education. i have heard several disturbing reports in the past year. as you all know, the governing body of the european schools decided last year that there must be at least seven pupils with the same language in order to teach a subject in the mother tongue. for general subjects such as geography and mathematics, this rule is not a problem, but for religious education it is quite different. this increase in the border has led to the fact that there are now european schools where pupils are unable to receive any religious instruction in their own language. i am very concerned about this, especially when it comes to primary education. i think it is very important to talk about religion, to talk about and to learn about the values and norms that we all in europe so emphasize. religion is all about feeling, and as we all know, it's best for children to express their feelings and emotions in their mother tongue. i find it unacceptable that, depending on language and nationality, some pupils have the opportunity to receive religious instruction in their own language and others do not. i therefore ask the commissioner to pay attention and to draw a clear line here. every pupil, regardless of whether he or she is a native speaker of english, german or dutch, should have the same opportunities and the same quality of education. i would also like to draw attention to the position of the teachers, in addition to the pupils. the 'minimum of seven' rule raises the question of whether there are enough pupils each year and makes it uncertain for teachers whether religious instruction will be given the following school year. this uncertainty of occupation has consequences for finding good teachers for religious education in all languages. i ask the commission to support the importance of religious education for parents and pupils in european schools and to support the continuation of religious education in the mother tongue in european mr president, the citizens are looking to europe for protection, stability and unity. it is precisely at this moment that a strong europe is more important than ever. everyone agrees that action was needed and it is good that it was taken quickly. it was inevitable. but there are a number of developments which i find worrying and i also detect a certain ideology behind some of the actions. a number of people have already been celebrating the death of capitalism here. but to be honest, politicians are not bankers. emergency measures, yes, but i also see a number of operations which amount to a mere nationalization of banks. frankly, there are some bankers who are clearly no longer to be trusted and to whom we cannot give our savings. but ask yourself, would you trust your savings to politicians as bankers? mr schulz, for example? now, not me. the crisis must not be used to evade, weaken or even abolish the rules. i find the call for flexible application of competition policy or the stability and growth pact extremely worrying. it is these very rules that have made europe so robust. i have a specific question to the commission - and i note with regret that mr barroso does not seem to find the debate interesting enough to stay until the end. the dutch minister for finance stated this afternoon in the parliamentary debate in the lower house that the purchase of fortis and abn-amro - not only the bank but also the non-systemically important components such as insurance - had not been notified as a state aid operation. i would therefore like to know how the commission intends to deal with cases of this kind in the future. the commissioner kroes said last monday - and i agree with him - that the competition rules and the stability pact apply without reservation. how do we deal with these kinds of cases? what will happen if it is found that the state aid rules have been breached?
the question is, mr president, whether this financial crisis will lead to further european integration or whether it will be the opposite. in my view, a crisis of this magnitude can only be tackled at european level. this should lead to further european integration. but that requires leadership. i would like to thank the commissioner for his report. so far, the response to the financial crisis has been largely in the hands of the member states, and i welcome that, for example in the case of the response to the crisis at fortis. the commission, however, remained silent. in my view, the financial crisis is caused by the rapid innovation in the financial sector in recent years. banking products are packaged in a way that could lead to quick profits, but also to great risks. it is only that these risks were not and sometimes still are not apparent. it is terribly difficult for experts to make a good estimate and thus to assess the value of these new financial products. the commission must therefore now come up with measures to increase the transparency of financial products and to improve the governance of banks. the commission should also put forward measures to improve supervision and make proposals to strengthen cooperation between central banks and other supervisors. this is the position which parliament will express in the report on the follow-up to lamfalussy and the future supervisory structure for the financial sector, which we shall be discussing shortly. i am also very sorry that the socialists have refrained from voting on such an important report in the committee. i wonder if they will abstain again tomorrow. the crisis shows the importance of europe. the fact that european governments operate separately in this area can lead to state aid and to the disadvantage of foreign savers, customers and investors. only a european response can be a full-fledged response to the crisis and that is why the commission must take the lead today.
rapporteur. − mr president, since 2002 i have been dealing with the issue of stability and supervision on the financial markets. already at that time, in a report for parliament on prudential supervision, i pleaded for a strong system of european supervision comparable to the european system of central banks. thank god we have the euro and the ecb in these turbulent times, but it is all the more embarrassing that we do not have this strong supervisory architecture. at the time i could not get a majority of parliament behind a revolutionary reform; everyone was speaking about evolution. nevertheless, when i have spoken over the years with a number of supervisors and other high-level representatives from the industry and supervision, people told me: ‘we cannot say this publicly, but you are right that something more has to happen; a real crisis situation will show that this is not sufficient’. so we needed a crisis to create the sense of urgency. needless to say, i would have preferred to avoid such a crisis and i wish that we had been better prepared. but the crisis is there now and the tsunami that mr mccreevy still did not want to look at some weeks ago, when we dealt with the rasmussen and lehne reports, is there and it is unprecedentedly heavy, i would say. i would now expect this momentum to be there, but i am disappointed and embarrassed that what we are doing in the european union is too little, too late. the whole world is watching but we are not managing to really get a common approach. even economists and commentators that were the cheerleaders of this wonderful innovative financial engineering that should not be hindered by regulation are almost unanimous in their disappointment at what europe’s leaders have done so far. i could refer to the editorial in yesterday’s financial times for instance, where they say: ‘thus far, europe’s leaders have displayed a purely declaratory brand of unity, jointly proclaiming that they will each take care of the problems in their own back yards.’ so the financial times also concluded that we need a conductor and not only solo singers. tomorrow i think there is an opportunity for parliament to show that we not only sing our own arias of regret and anger and play the blame game, but we can make a real contribution constructively to establish leadership and pick up on very concrete proposals that the european commission could immediately initiate. it may look quite technical for many other members of parliament but i can assure you that these have been well thought through and well prepared by us in the committee on economic and monetary affairs. it only needs leadership and courage from you, commissioner, to give the green light and to start up the process. you should not listen too much to the city lobbyists keeping you from doing things, and of course you should listen to experts – but the political responsibility is with you to take these initiatives. mr barroso has already mentioned some short-term measures. i am happy with what he said about a wise persons’ group but, here again, this was something that we in parliament proposed two years ago, and we did not get your support for this. in relation to the crd proposals, the measures for the credit-rating agencies: it is not quite clear to me what measures you are going to present on the recommendation on executive pay. here, i hope, you are not listening only to the weak proposals of the ecofin council but that you are prepared to move a bit further. finally, on the supervision: it is not only long-term measures for the wise persons’ group; we should already start with something now. concerning my amendments 5, 6 and 7: i hope there will be support for a proposal which immediately leads to an independent chair and vice-chair for the european supervisory structure.
rapporteur. − madam president, there is a lot of material for debate. if i may quote the commissioner on some of the things that he said, i should like to start with his statement that, in times of crisis, politicians should do something. i think they should do it much earlier. before a tsunami comes over us, we should take the measures and be proactive and prevent this type of thing from happening. so i agree with what others have said: if we are starting to act now, it is too little, too late. my second point is this issue of the supervisory steps to be taken. you mentioned the solvency ii example where you also accuse us, parliament, of weakening these proposals on group supervision. i do not think it is a weakening of the structure that has been proposed, as i think my colleague mr sánchez presedo has mentioned already. what is proposed in fact by the commission is a system of group supervision where lead supervisors, which often are the big member states, take the lead in the process of a college. i support the idea is that we should create colleges and that we should take some of these supervisory issues at a common, joint, level, because these cross-border groups act at the joint level. but the point is that there may be conflict between the interests of these lead supervisors of one national member state and the supervisors of a host member state, a big part of whose markets may dominated by this other group. so in these cases of conflict, it is not fair to just say that you can have a sort of voluntary mediation by the level 3 committees followed simply by advice to the lead supervisor, and then in the end the lead supervisor can either comply or explain that he is deviating from this advice. this is what is worrying the host member states, and that is why you cannot stick solely to national supervisors dealing with this. there you need a neutral, impartial, independent arbiter at european level, and this is what i have already proposed in this report: not a bureaucratic supervision structure, just something to build on the present structure simply by adding to the other chairs of these three level 3 committees an independent chair and an independent vice-chair to act together with these level 3 committees and to deal in a binding way with the resolution of conflicts that may remain between supervisors. this is what i think should be the guarantee to enable host member states, smaller member states in particular, to give their assent to this type of group supervision and these mandates to lead supervisors. so this extra level, the extra add-on that we need to solve this problem, as well as for the short term, is in amendment 7 that i have tabled for the vote tomorrow. another issue is the role of these supervisory committees as compared to the commission. i also have the impression, like mrs kauppi, that in the commission you would only prefer to have these supervisors as advisors and not really have an independent role for them to play, for instance in the international connection, in connection with the iasb standard-setting board or the fsf. a direct question to you. we have been told that mr barroso was very proud that now the commission is invited to this financial stability forum, but that although you were invited last week you did not go to that meeting. maybe you can directly respond to say whether this true or not? a final thing, on the executive pay recommendation, the only thing you have mentioned is that this is soft law and one member state is following up on this. there is room to come up with stronger proposals, so please come up with your proposals.

i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr seeber, for his report. many of us here think that clean drinking water looks like this. i would point out that in many countries this glass is empty. a lot of people don't have water or the water looks like this. if there is a shortage of oil, the debates are very heated, and i sometimes wonder why this is not the case when it comes to water. water is a matter of life and death. the world's population is steadily increasing, and the amount of clean drinking water available is only decreasing. europe too is facing water shortages. but also hidden water scarcity, for example by cutting off households from drinking water supplies. this is due to a number of factors, including poor sanitation, the gradual liberalization of public services, the use of pesticides, fertilisers and intensive livestock farming. do you know that 100 grams of beef requires 2,400 liters of water? the commission's communication focuses largely on water conservation. this is bringing water to the sea, as far as i am concerned, because the first thing to do is to tackle the source of the problems mentioned above. i think of the fact that first of all, investment must be made in sound sewage and water treatment systems throughout europe and beyond. the use of pesticides must also be reduced, the impact of intensive livestock farming on the water supply must be considered and measures against climate change must be taken. access to clean drinking water is a civil right, a right of everyone. the question is not whether there is enough drinking water, the problem is the distribution of clean drinking water, unnecessary pollution and the urge to privatize. in england alone, privatisation led to a sudden sharp increase in prices and in the number of households cut off from water. this is a disgrace! i'm advocating that the drinking water supply be kept in public hands.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's water resources are not depleted. water scarcity and drought have both climatic and human causes. tourism also takes a great deal of water resources, especially in those countries where the problems are already great. the ipcc's research results have shown that the risks will only increase in the coming years. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the situation in the water sector does not deteriorate further. i therefore believe that this report sends a good signal. efficient water use is literally vital in the fight against water scarcity and drought. i would also like to stress the principle of water pricing, which is also included in the report. there are good experiences in several member states. i also welcome the report's link with the water framework directive. i think that's the right place to address the issues of water scarcity and drought. finally, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, richard seeber, on his excellent work.

co-rapporteur. − mr president, it is the second one. it was indeed about the deposit guarantee schemes. we tried to strengthen the present text by asking that these rules in the european union be ‘urgently revised to avoid arbitrage between guarantee levels in member states that may further increase volatility and undermine financial stability instead of increasing security and depositors’ confidence’. then it says they should also guarantee a level playing field for financial institutions. sorry for this.
in writing. - i abstained on the report by mr panzeri on the intensification of the fight against undeclared work. although there are some good points in the report, i believe that the report is trying to introduce elements of flexicurity back into the european labour market by a backdoor. flexicurity will lead to more flexibility for employers and less security for employees. i can't agree to this.
in writing. - more than 20% of jobs in europe are undeclared. it's affecting our social security system. the proposal acknowledges that the market is partly corrupt and that competition is distorted as a result. we support the fight against undeclared work. we shall abstain, however, because this proposal is seriously flawed. unfortunately, it is based on a neoliberal mindset that pits workers against each other. the fight against undeclared work is used as a pretext to promote the so-called flexibilization of labour. this is worsening the labour market position of europeans as they are forced to compete with cheap labour from elsewhere. the eu's enlargements in 2004 and 2007 have had a profound impact. the wealth gap within the eu has widened considerably as a number of new member states have seen their once thriving industries collapse. these are now countries that mainly import goods and export labour. people from poland or romania come to work elsewhere for wages that are not lawfully paid and under unacceptable conditions. their low pay is being abused to reduce the pay of others. europe cannot be further integrated if we have very different income levels within the eu and if those differences are abused in the competition.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' to cover all transport services. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are not undermined. on 23 may 2007, balkenende defended in strasbourg that the eu symbols should not be included in a constitutional treaty. his main reason for doing so was that otherwise the dutch council of state would find a new dutch referendum necessary. the commission is proposing to include the eu flag, the anthem and the motto in the european parliament's rules of procedure. he argues that he is thereby sending a political signal that these symbols represent european values. he hoped that the eu would be a benchmark for freedom, development and solidarity in the world. the eu is also characterised by its uncontrollable size, its intrusive interference, its risky coercion of economic freedom and its display of military power. it is better if 'listening to the voter' becomes more of a european characteristic. this symbolism will not change anything. the blue flag is already on every banknote and car number plate. we are still pretending that the treaty of lisbon has vitality, when it has been rejected according to its own rules.
in writing. − this proposal is an attempt to finally take earlier decisions seriously by improving controls, better information, guarded motorway stops and the abolition of the exemptions for the so-called self-employed as of 23 march 2009. the transposition into national law and enforcement of social legislation for road transport is hopelessly behind. the four member states which fail to do so are protected as long as their names are not publicly mentioned. the fact that they do not regulate working, driving and resting times properly is bad for road safety and for the health of professional drivers. my party, the sp, supports the plan to do something about this, even though better proposals were voted down at an earlier stage. we do this because we consider compliance with social legislation to be important and because the directive will also cover self-employed drivers. this will put an end to the abuse of workers by their employers in forcing them to become self-employed without staff. as ostensibly self-employed, they are forced to work under worse working conditions and take on business risks they cannot bear. the competition in this business is killing me. words about following rules should be turned into actions. this is the only way for drivers to maintain a sufficient income and livelihood.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it now appears that the commissioner responsible, mrs hübner, with whom we are so anxious to debate this subject, will not be able to be present. the commission presidency and the coordinators of the various political groups therefore propose that this debate, which is so important for parliament, be held in the november part-session. the request is therefore made to move the debate to the november partsession. that is the request i would like to make.
on behalf of the pse group. – madam president, some things that you have worked hard on never happen, and some things happen only very late. that was the case with this directive. you would not expect any more for it to happen. in 2002, six years ago, i was working very hard as your rapporteur in first reading on this commission proposal after the social dialogue negotiations of the social partners’ fields. we managed to convince the commission and the vast majority of the council to adopt 95% of our amendments and improvements of the text, only a blocking minority of, at the time, 4 of the 15 member states did not want to go along with that majority. several presidencies tried very hard to break the stalemate but, after some years, the proposal was – well, maybe not really dead, but at least in a coma. that was a shame, since the problems that the directive tackles had not disappeared by far – on the contrary, in the period of enlargement of the eu. also, with the directive, those problems of exploitation of migrant workers are not fully solved, so we have to continue to work on this, but a temporary agency directive is a first step. it creates clarity about the status and rights of employment of temporary agency workers by reinforcing the principle of equal treatment as the basic principle of labour law but at the same time – and no less importantly – by emphasising the role of collective bargaining and decent industrial relations in a growing and maturing temporary agency sector. i emphasise this, and this is linked to the negative connotations of exploitation by gang masters from the organised professional temporary agency sector. in the present directive this sector can really prove to be mature and to have a good social dialogue with trade unions, and then help reach very advanced arrangements for flexibility and security in the labour market.
