president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on legal affairs and citizens' rights, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 1408/71 on the application of social security schemes to employed persons and their families moving within the community (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). the first question is: which court has jurisdiction? the second question is: which law should the court apply? the first question is to be dealt with at european level, and i think that is understandable. this guarantees that every european citizen can bring his case to court. the second question concerns a field which is typically a matter for the member states themselves to decide. the existing national legislation is based on many national principles and these must be respected. the commission proposal also aims to harmonize these collision rules. the report by mrs gebhardt largely maintains the commission's report and does not seek to remove chapter iia from the proposal. that is why i shall vote against the report and against the proposal. i therefore call on the council to reject the commission proposal as well.
rapporteur. − mr president, i will start with another remark on the absence of the council, because in my speaking notes i had some remarks about good cooperation between the european parliament and the council, dialogue, spirit of the lisbon treaty etc., but the council is absent for this debate as well. i think that is absolutely disgraceful, because it is the council that is hammering out a pnr policy and is required to reply to questions, and it is not here. the council has publicly made solemn pledges to involve the european parliament, but now we see what the pledges of the council are worth – nothing. i think this is an affront not to the european parliament but to the citizens who are entitled to answers and transparent decision-making. so please, mr president, pass on my discontent to the representatives of the presidency. it is a joint debate on, the one hand, the proposals for an eu pnr and, on the other hand, the agreement on pnr between the eu and australia. they are essentially the same problems, and the problems have already been raised in the context of the agreement with the us and, later on, with canada. one of the main issues is that of purpose limitation, because everything else follows on from that – purpose limitation or, in other words, the justification for the proposal to begin with. now, everything is wrong with the justification, everything is wrong with the purpose limitation. i shall describe this to you. to start with, subsidiarity: the commission and the council state that the aim of the proposal is harmonisation of national schemes. however, only a few member states – i think it is three so far – have either a pnr system in place or plans for such a scheme. therefore, the proposal cannot possibly harmonise national systems, because they are non-existent. it merely creates the obligation for all member states to set up such a system for the collection of pnr data. i would call this ‘policy laundering’, because whatever we cannot achieve at national level we try to achieve via the back door of the eu. i am very pro-european, but i do not like this. moreover, the commission has proposed a decentralised scheme so that the european added value is even less clear, and it creates an unworkable patchwork of rules and systems for the carriers and a very intransparent system for citizens. the stated purpose in the proposal of the commission is to identify persons who are, or may be, involved in a terrorist or organised crime event, as well as their associates, create and update risk indicators, to provide intelligence on travel patterns and other trends relating to terrorist offences to be used in criminal investigations and the prosecution of terrorist offences and organised crime. the commission, in its proposal, claims that the eu has been able to assess the value of pnr data and to realise its potential for law enforcement purposes. however, to date we have not seen any concrete evidence to substantiate this claim. any evidence so far provided by the us is anecdotal and, to be honest, the information that we got from various us government agencies over the last year or so only seems to demonstrate that the mass collection and processing of pnr is not useful at all. there has only been a single evaluation of the us pnr scheme, which did not assess the results. as a matter of fact, a recent dhs-funded report raises substantial doubts over the usefulness of behavioural surveillance as a tool to identify potential terrorists. this is easy to understand, because how are you going to develop risk profiles of potential terrorists on the basis of pnr data? it is blatant nonsense. how are you going to determine if anybody has bad intentions on the basis of his telephone number or his credit card number? in other words, the purpose stated in the commission proposal is demonstrably invalid and unfounded, and yet that is the basis on which the council is working. the commission and council seem to be confused as to what can and cannot be done with pnr data. pnr records tend to contain very summary data and, on average, no more than 10 fields, with very basic information. so it is totally unclear how this data could serve to identify high-risk persons. law enforcement authorities already have the necessary powers to obtain pnr data on a case-by-case basis in the context of an investigation or prosecution into known suspects and possible associates. so the commission proposal would merely remove the obligation of getting a warrant and due cause. so if law enforcement authorities need new powers, it is for them to demonstrate when and how the existing powers were insufficient. so far we have never had an answer to that question. we already have a directive on api data, and they can indeed be used for the identification of persons and they can be used to run persons against a watch list. this is not possible with pnr data. so if we have a directive on api data, then why do we need more? that has not been demonstrated. automated systematic analysis of pnr of all passengers can be useful for other purposes, for example combating drug trafficking or illegal immigration. they may be very legitimate and valid purposes, but let us have it on the table and let us not talk about the prevention of terrorist attacks, because that is something completely different. if the commission and the council intend to expand the scope of the proposal to cover other purposes, as i have just said, they should clarify in detail, for each stated purpose, what use will be made of the pnr data. in other words, you can use pnr data in a targeted manner in the context of a specific ongoing, concrete investigation. you can use pnr data for automated systematic analysis, for example against drug trafficking, but in that case you do not need to store the data. so we need to know exactly what the purpose is. that brings me to the issue of, let us say, the legal base, because if you read the small print of the eu-australia pnr – and this also applies to the eu-us pnr – it is not only about the fight against terrorism and crime but also about immigration, public health risks, administrative purposes, customs, immigration, supervision and accountability of public administration. that has nothing to do with the fight against terrorism. the commission and the council have chosen a third-pillar instrument for the pnr proposal and also for the agreements with other countries, but the third pillar is about police and justice cooperation within the european union. it is not about security in other countries. the commission may argue that, indirectly, if we provide data to the americans, the australians and to south korea, for example, we will benefit from it in terms of security. that may well be true, but then i would like to understand where public health comes in. where does immigration come in? where does the supervision and accountability of public administration come in? it has nothing to do with it. i will not go into all the other details of the implementation, but the question of purpose and justification needs to be answered before anything else, because the claim that it has been so useful in the fight against terrorism has not been substantiated to date: we are still waiting for evidence, and i would very much like to have that. and, if there is no evidence, then we should look at the proposal again.
mr president, i would just briefly like to reply to that because i think it is a matter of courtesy as well as political interest for the council to be here and i am sure they can spare one minister to be present. we have had previous debates in the past two months where the council was present but left the debate half-way through. i think that is unacceptable and it is not for me to report that to my group. it is for the presidency of this house to transmit our discontent to the presidency of the european union.
i would like to make a further comment on what mr bradbourn has said. i am not opposed to the pnr either, because it is a bit of a given. the point is, and i agree with the rapporteur on this point, that we must look very carefully at how best to use this instrument and when the need and the usefulness are really established. i would also like to make a proposal to the commission. commissioner barrot says that he is really very keen to work with the european parliament and i appreciate that, but we are not quite getting past the question of utility and necessity and that is, i think, the real heart of the debate between the commission, the council and parliament on this subject. commissioner, you say you have a lot of evaluations that show that it's all very useful. i know from what i have read that it mainly provides information on migration and has solved a number of other issues, but as far as i know it has not done much to combat terrorism. i'd be happy to investigate that with you at some point. so i propose that there should be an inquiry and that you and i should sit down together to define the question of inquiry, and then we should have an inquiry and then we should have a full discussion. we are not opposed to pnr in principle, but we do want it to be handled with care, in line with the principles of our privacy policy. i hope you will support that. can you respond to this? as for your agreement with australia, it may well be much better than any other agreement, for example with the united states, but my question is: what other chords are on the shelf? you just said that this must not fall into the hands of repressive authorities. so i can assume that we will never negotiate with russia and we will never negotiate with china. can you at least confirm that and perhaps give a list of the countries with which negotiations are already under way and which we will not be informed of until a few months from now?
rapporteur. − mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply, and i will be very happy to sit down with him and talk through all the details. i shall come back briefly to purpose, because there are many misunderstandings about what can and cannot be done with pnr data. pnr data are already available now, today, without an eu pnr scheme, simply with a warrant and due cause. but the need for further, unlimited powers has not been proven. so i do not dispute the usefulness of pnr data in themselves: i dispute the usefulness of this mass collection and automated analysis. i am not the only one who is of this view; i am in good company. the data protection authorities have the same view, but they are being ignored. the air carriers are telling us the same. security experts from airport security tell us the same, and i will give you a quote from a report that was commissioned by the department of homeland security – i will be very happy to give it to you. it says: ‘automated identification of terrorists through data mining or any other known methodology is not feasible as an objective’. i did not make this up. these are security experts commissioned by the department of homeland security. there is evidence for the usefulness, as you said, commissioner, but usefulness in combating drugs trafficking or illegal immigration or other purposes. this may surprise you, but i am not even opposed in principle to using pnr data for those purposes. but we must be very precise in defining the purposes so as to ensure proportionality and to ensure adequate legal safeguards. i would like to conclude on a very personal note. i am very unhappy about the way that we have been arguing about pnr for five years now, and the council and the commission just charge ahead like a runaway train. i would like to say to my fellow irish eu citizens that, if you share my desire to end this kind of undemocratic and untransparent decision-making, then please say ‘yes’ to the new eu treaty.
the commission's proposals for a more rapid recovery of cod stocks in european waters are the subject of the busk report. i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr busk, on his report. cod is an important fish species for the eu. in the past, it was a popular food, and cod was eaten as a substitute for more expensive meat. today, cod is a luxury fish that is available only at high prices. since the late 1980s and early 1990s, cod stocks have been declining. this is due to a variety of natural causes, such as the warming of the north sea and the near disappearance of severe winters, but also to the intensive fishing for cod. it is this particular cause that the commission intends to tackle with the new cod recovery plan. i agree with the commission's wish to simplify the rules for restricting cod fishing. the current system is too complex and leads to many differences of interpretation between fishermen and inspectors. simplification of the system will at least solve this. it is necessary, also for the dutch fisheries, that we maintain the recovery of cod stocks at european level. the proposed measures took time to take effect. for more than a year and a half, cod stocks in the north sea have been making a remarkable recovery. parliament - and i too - wishes to involve the fisheries sector and the regional advisory councils more in the measures to be taken. this will improve the sector's footprint. the new rules are, as far as i am concerned, a step in the right direction. the member states can regulate cod fishing better, while fishermen know much better what they can and cannot do. it will take four to six years to see whether the measures we are taking now will have any effect. i therefore call on the commissioner to take the time to evaluate the measures taken before proposing new measures. otherwise, the fishermen will be in an impossible situation.
in writing. the adoption of the medina report is a strong signal for better regulation. the report contains the main epp positions, such as: independent monitoring of the implementation of impact assessments by the european commission; timely and comprehensive consultation of stakeholders; the 25% reduction in administrative burden should be a net objective. reductions in burdens resulting from existing legislation in certain areas should not be offset by additional administrative burdens resulting from new legislation. unfortunately, there is insufficient support from other groups for the following points: support the commission to consider self-regulation and co-regulation as serious strategic policy options. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the eu's environmental legislation and will continue to monitor the implementation of the eu's environmental legislation. the epp also believes that the commission should take account of the opinions of the high level group on administrative burdens (stoiber group). the commission has also proposed that the directive on the protection of the environment be amended to include the possibility of the member states to impose a minimum level of protection on the environment.
madam president, it is interesting to see that financial market supervision has become a chefsache in the council. i appreciate the setting-up of the high-level group with mr jacques de larosière, which we in the ep have been demanding for several years now. i want to give this clear message: coordination alone is not enough. we really need institutional solutions. voluntary cooperation of national supervisors on the basis of ‘comply or explain’ systems like in the solvency ii and the crd proposals is insufficient, and the financial stability forum, which is pretty unaccountable and which only has representatives from larger countries, with their national interests, is not sufficient. it does not give a clear voice from europe. we need a unified architecture similar to the european system of central banks, which is perfectly placed to be an arbiter internally and a strong voice internationally.

president, commissioner, minister, ladies and gentlemen, relations with russia have undeniably changed in recent times. i would like to say that in my view, isolating russia is not an option. at the same time, it is very difficult to talk about partnership when there is so much mistrust of russia in the member states. i do think, as chairman of the delegation for relations with russia, that we must keep the dialogue going. the council does that, the commission does that and we as parliament must do that too. we have had a very lively discussion on this in our delegation to russia in connection with our visit to moscow at the end of this week. the result of this was that we had to continue the dialogue, but we had to let our partners know that it was not business as usual. we need to report this openly and try to have constructive discussions. we expect much more from russia, mr president. the withdrawal of troops from the buffer zone was only the first step. the only way to reduce tensions is to reduce the number of troops in abkhazia and south ossetia or to withdraw them. there is nothing in the agreements about this, but it is in keeping with the spirit of the agreements and i would also like to hear the minister's opinion on this. the eu, and the presidency in particular, has been incredibly clear and very active in recent times. i think this should continue. i have three more questions. first, as regards the run-up to the summit: what signals and what concrete steps do you think are needed to restart the negotiations? secondly: how do you involve parliament in this? and thirdly, a note: russia is not a party to the oslo convention on cluster bombs. now it turns out that the dutch journalist was killed by a russian cluster bomb. how can we, commissioner, still include russia in the new treaty to be signed in oslo?
president, the member states of the european union have not closed ranks in response to the unilateral territorial division of georgia under russian supervision. there is no question of a joint, let alone firm, european response to the kremlin's power machinations. the crucial question remains: what does the union mean by a strategic partnership with the russian federation? in more poignant terms: is moscow really an indispensable partner for brussels, or am i touching on a political taboo in europe? the fact is that russia has so far blocked an effective international response to the iranian and north korean nuclear programmes. at the same time, moscow is not proving to be an indispensable partner in the bloody fight against islamic terrorism, for example on the afghan front. the cold figures may be the only sign of a strategic, indeed indispensable, partnership between the european union and russia in the energy sector: today, the eu27 depend on russia for some 70% of its oil and gas exports. however, for its own sake, the eu must reduce this strong dependence as quickly as possible. finally, moscow itself indicates that its energy reserves will be exhausted in ten to fifteen years. the council and the commission, where is your strategy for energy diversification? the new turkmen gas find should spur you to action.
president. - in the climate and energy debate currently taking place in this house, we have repeatedly stressed the importance of a sustainable society. one part of this is a sustainable transport sector. in recent months we have tried to impose stricter standards for freight (euro 6) and passenger cars. this is a source approach. but other stages can also be taken to make transport more sustainable, through green procurement, as proposed in the proposal we are discussing. i wholeheartedly support the principle of including energy and environmental impacts in vehicle procurement. this will stimulate sustainable vehicle development by promoting demand for sustainable vehicles and thereby encouraging manufacturers to innovate more. important aspects to be taken into account are fuel consumption and related co2 emissions, but also the emissions of toxic and otherwise harmful particles and substances such as nitrogen oxides and fine dust. for effective policy, it is important to ensure a good exchange of knowledge and information between member states. this will allow the exchange of best practices, which will help to optimise green procurement procedures. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr dan jørgensen, for the compromise he has reached with the council on this subject. the first stage of the procedure was difficult, but in the second reading i think we have reached a reasonable agreement, which i have been able to sign.

- i'm the reporter. - mr president, i would like to begin by thanking, as i have so often done, all those involved: i would like to thank commissioner kuneva, the shadow rapporteurs and also the three council presidents, because we started this report in 2007, under the portuguese presidency. the slovenian presidency was followed by a french presidency, and we finally found a compromise. it is a pity that the secretary of state, mr luc chatel, is not here, because it is of course great to see that we have reached a compromise, and that already at first reading. in 1994 a directive on timeshare was adopted which has considerably cleaned up the sector. the major problem was that member states implemented the directive differently. there was a patchwork of legislation and also compliance was different in different places willingly or unwillingly and that still inspired some distrust among consumers with regard to buying timeshare across the border. we're working on a new policy now. we have broadened the scope of the directive a little, because the 1994 directive did not define a number of products or did not define them sufficiently, such as long-term holidays, the exchange of products, etc. i think we have now succeeded in making a clear improvement on this point and both industry and consumers are very satisfied with the result. i hope that we as politicians are too. we have worked quite intensively with colleagues from the various groups and we have achieved a good result. for example, there is a ban on prepayment, because that was a very annoying problem for many consumers. you paid and could not get back the eur 1,000 or eur 1,500 you had paid in advance. the 14-day cooling-off period is also a very important matter, i think. then, and i am personally very pleased about this, there is a checklist, a standard information form which expressly states what you have bought and also that you have the possibility to terminate your contract within 14 days without giving any reason. then there is the voluntary code of conduct for the sector and the quality mark, a distinctive or quality mark, and also the fact that the commission has undertaken to monitor it. that's very good for me. i think it is good that the commission has committed itself to following these codes of conduct. i am particularly pleased that the commission has undertaken to monitor compliance with the scheme in the member states in the trialogue. in some cases it will be somewhat easier for the consumer to go to the national courts. i regret that jurisdiction, the determination of the competent court, is not explicitly mentioned in this directive. i'm sorry, but in a compromise, you have to add a little water to the wine every now and then. i also regret that, if crucial information has not been provided, a contract is found to exist after a year. all of that can be done in a compromise. overall, there has been enormous progress for both the consumer and the sector, in particular maximum harmonisation. this makes it easier in some cases to go to court in the member state where you live. it will be much easier for the sector to do business across the border. i think, mr president, that we are on the verge of a good result and that we as a parliament, together with the commission and the council, can be proud of it.
- i'm the reporter. - mr president, if we adopt this directive this afternoon, we will adopt a directive on a small part of the internal market, but it will be a very big step for the european consumer. this directive is a precursor to the horizontal consumer protection instrument. as rapporteur, i proposed that this should be laid down in a regulation. in the end it has become maximum harmonization and i think that maximum european harmonization will not only protect the consumer but also the bona fide companies involved in the trade in question. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's tourism sector is not left behind. i am glad that this maximum harmonisation will give all european consumers equal rights. i believe that european consumers, regardless of their country of origin, should have the same rights when they buy goods, services or whatever within the internal market. this directive will do just that. i hope that the member states will also regulate and monitor compliance in the same way, so that consumer confidence is maintained. the internal market, and this is why i believe in europe, can only function well if it is based on trust, both from business and from the public and consumers. if we succeed, i think this will be a precursor and an important signal to harmonize the horizontal consumer protection instrument as much as possible for all consumer purchases. i think, mr president, that this is a great step forward, because we have already optimized many areas of the internal market, but not yet consumer confidence. i think that the directive before us is a very important step in that direction. i want to thank everyone who has contributed positively to this.

in writing. −for many employers, the temptation is to pay as little as possible for the remuneration of their employees' work. other costs associated with the work, such as safety and amenities, are also under pressure. workers can only protect themselves against this by ensuring that their pay is based on a collective agreement binding on all and by adequate legal protection in the country where they live and work. both the original objectives of the services directive and the recent rulings of the european court of justice affect that protection. if they allow a worse foreign cmo or foreign law to apply, more and more employers will use cost-saving options for them and the income of employees will fall sharply. some people have the illusion that the draft eu constitution or the lisbon treaty offer sufficient guarantees against this. to achieve this, the text will have to be amended before it can be adopted. it was also expected that the andersson report would provide the guarantees envisaged. the compromise reached on this text unfortunately makes this even less the case than it was initially. that is why we cannot vote for it.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development and cooperation. - i am speaking as a member of the committee on development and i would like to make three points to that committee. firstly, the food crisis, secondly, the system of evaluating development policy and thirdly, the lack of resources in chapter 4 - foreign expenditure - which has been discussed earlier. first of all, the food crisis. of course, that is a big problem. millions of lives are at stake and a joint european approach is urgently needed. but we need a different approach from the one the commission proposed in the first place. the budgetary agreements must be respected and the powers of the council and the european parliament must not be overridden. i am therefore pleased that the commission is going to review the first proposal to use unused agricultural funds for this purpose. mr president, the stakes must be: the commission has already made a number of proposals. of course, food aid is needed for people whose lives are in immediate danger, but in the end it is a long-term issue and therefore food production must be put back on the european union's development agenda as a priority. we will also have to talk about a re-evaluation of the food security thematic programme and we will have to look at how we can give small farmers better access to crucial production factors. we are therefore also pleased that the proposal for a pilot project on micro-credit has been adopted, as this may make it easier for small farmers to finance food production. secondly: the systematic evaluation of development policy. this one needs to be better. the evaluation is too much about where the money goes and too little about the results. if we are to maintain public support for development cooperation, we must have a better view of the results of our work. thirdly: the lack of resources for external expenditure, chapter 4. this matter has been discussed. this is not just about development cooperation funds, but also for kosovo, the middle east, etc. we have great ambitions. you're right to be. that is why this item also deserves a structural increase.
the budget of the other institutions, mr president, is fairly balanced between the ever-strong, almost chronic, demand for more staff and the need to reduce expenditure. the european parliament, as a prudent budgetary authority, remains within the agreed limits (one fifth of the institutions' budget), but nevertheless invests in knowledge by increasing the number of staff in the political groups and by strengthening the library. it must, because the questions we face are always complex and always linked to globalisation and global issues. the foundations are being laid for the introduction of the members' and assistants' statute, and that is a matter of social justice that has been delayed for too long. the number of staff responsible for liaising between the european parliament and the national parliaments will be increased. that is essential, although it is a small amount. it is about the exchange with the national parliaments and it must be improved. there is often a great lack of knowledge in the capitals about the brussels agendas and about subsidiarity issues, while european citizens are more critical than ever of what should be regulated at national and european level. as regards the other institutions, mr president, one thing: the accessibility of citizens must be a priority. that is why it is important to increase the ombudsman's staff, to mention just this one point. the european union must be open to all citizens, including and especially those who have complaints.

the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the situation in the baltic states is not affected by the crisis. the holodomor is the starvation of 6-7 million people, of whom 3.5 million in ukraine, 2 million in kazakhstan and hundreds of thousands in the north caucasus on the volga and in western siberia, in the years 1932-1933. holodomor represents the brutal deprivation of the ukrainian peasantry of its grain harvest by stalin and his cronies as a trial bolshevik means of educating the countryside in the spirit of forced collectivization of agriculture. holodomor is the deliberate denial by the stalinists of the acute famine in ukraine and elsewhere in the soviet union, so that millions of victims were deprived of all domestic and foreign aid. holodomor stands for the soviet leadership's uninterrupted export of grain during the famine years of 1932-1933, even though it could have fed about a million and a half people for a year. holodomor represents genocide against the ukrainian villagers by a pure policy of starvation in the form of a complete economic blockade of the ukrainian countryside, by the introduction of natural resources, supply stoppages and blacklists based on the decree of 18 november 1932, dictated by molotov, stalin's then envoy in kharkiv. the holodomor represents stalin's paranoia about ukrainian nationalism, which the great leader considered to be the cause of what he saw as the inadequate supply of grain from the european granary. the holodomor represents stalin's intentions to disrupt forever all ukrainian dreams of autonomy, or even independence. mr president, we know today that this demonic plot has failed.

in writing. −my group is not one of the co-signatories of this resolution and votes against it by a majority. the reason for this is the view that the emergence of the soviet union in what was then backward russia was a step forward, giving many poorly educated, poorly paid and disenfranchised people a better life. i share that view, but that cannot justify all the means that were used at the time. some modernists considered the individual right to life of people with a different viewpoint to be of no importance at all, and this was especially true of their leader, stalin. that attitude fit into a long russian tradition of oppression and violence. all the bad tools of the past were used again, this time to break down any resistance to progress. the original ideal of democracy and equality for all people was subordinated to it. the good that people pursued became the justification for the evil they did, on the idea that history always judges in favor of the victors. now, 75 years later, much attention is rightly given to what went wrong and to the many victims. that is why i am voting in favour of this resolution.

president, commissioner, europe is expected to act forcefully. citizens in affected areas do not understand why it should take months for europe, mired in its own procedures, to provide clarity. how's that going? something happens somewhere and a few hours later you see on tv what happened and you think, there needs to be action, there needs to be solidarity. then it turns out to be deafeningly quiet. we are stuck between the national state and the european bureaucracies. the annoying thing is that there is no communication about that at the moment either. it has been stuck in the council for two years, but it cannot be that we do not get the finger behind the blockade and that we do not hear alternatives. that's the big point. in six months' time, parliament will be dissolved. then there will be a new parliament and we will have to hand over this dossier to the appropriate bodies with good proposals in hand. the french presidency is doing everything, but they have not done anything on this point. we have not done anything on this point and we are asking for an explanation. i do have a compliment for the european commission. mrs hübner and her colleagues have indeed drawn on the file and it must be possible to draw it out with united forces. it's naming and shaming. this is where we say: the council and the french presidency have failed.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has adopted a report on the proposal for a council directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. - the environment committee believes it is important to be a reliable public authority. that doesn't mean you can't make big changes, but you have to announce them well in advance and have alternatives for people. last year the european commission presented a communication on the state of health of the common agricultural policy. the agricultural policy should be radically reformed. the aim was to cut direct subsidies sharply and to increase returns in terms of environmental protection and employment. we are ultimately disappointed in the way the proposals have been drawn up, because we believe that they mainly involve a few insignificant savings on direct subsidies. farmers should not be subsidized according to past yield or land ownership. the environment committee wants this to be done for the public services they provide, such as biodiversity enhancement and water management, and for their work on the environment, animal welfare and food safety that goes beyond the legal obligations. we want this to happen by 2020. tonight we're going to talk about the decline of the bee population. to reverse this, less spraying will be required and biodiversity will have to be promoted by buffer zones, and in this context, genetic engineering and also intensive one-sided agriculture are a problem. i hope that both tonight and this morning we will be committed to an agriculture where arable farming and nature are mutually reinforcing.
madam president, it is well known. i'm not so enthusiastic about all the health-check proposals. let's say i was a farmer and i had calculated in 2005 what i could expect to see until 2013. i can now expect - to put it a little more bluntly - 8% less from the modulation, 10% less from the national reserve and potentially 9% less from the capping of the high income supplements. that's a 27% reduction in the total. how can the average farmer in the future expect a european government to keep its promise? mrs buitenweg said the 27% was insignificant. i think it's a big part of what's promised. i am therefore also against this modulation and i would like to see the capping of these total income supplements much more limited. i think that we should take one opportunity with regard to this national reserve, and that is to develop as soon as possible a system of insurance against animal and plant diseases throughout europe. sooner or later, with the increased travel of people, another contagious animal disease will break out in europe and we are not prepared for it from a budgetary point of view. if that were to happen again, i would not know where the commission would get the money today in the future. it has to be through an insurance system. the milk quotas are a matter of concern. we can only see that more and more new markets are developing internationally. i think it is not a good thing for europe that we say to the americans or the brazilians, for example, that this market is for you. we must also take part in this and that is why a soft landing of milk quotas is necessary. we could take three measures: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
mr president, in the 1960s the dutchman sicco mansholt, a leading member of my party, shaped the european agricultural policy and we are proud of that. his vision was to ensure that all europeans had enough and safe food and that farmers had a decent income. and those are the ideals that i will defend here again with all my heart and soul. but a lot has changed in the last 50 years: rapidly growing world population, climate change, globalisation, unfair trade and food price speculation, see the recent food crisis. the debate on the health check is mainly about means, instruments and cleverly devised exceptions. i regret that the larger picture is being overlooked in the run-up to 2013. it is time for an intelligent update of our agricultural policy in which we retain mansholt's ideals but throw away the outdated parts. the commission has already made a number of proposals. it is high time for fair trade and more attention to sustainability, health and innovative solutions to the challenges we really face. we owe that to the rest of the world, to our future generations and to the taxpayers of europe.
mr president, we have heard a lot this morning in this long debate. fortunately, i was able to offer the commissioner a portion of school fruit this morning before we started, so i hope that with that snack we can continue on this important subject. i'm going straight from school fruit to milk. apart from the discussion of all sorts of technical instruments, i do not think it is possible to explain to the european citizen that we have paid 340 million euros in surcharges in the past year, while we are all together almost 1% below the european quota. we shall have to put an end to this lack of logic in the council. i would also like to say that the commission's proposal for an annual increase of 1% in the quota is very meagre in my view and may well be half-filled in the council of ministers. the 1% is not a good indicator of the opportunities that we have within the eu and on the world market. the argument i have heard before in this debate that our quota system would automatically guarantee us a good price is far too short for me. the price developments since 1984 also show this clearly. of course, mega-corporations like the united states are unthinkable in our country. we will have to keep safety nets for very bad years and for unforeseen circumstances, such as animal diseases. i would also like to ask the european commission - and i think this is equally important - to look at the distribution of returns in the food production chain. while supermarkets are currently operating at economic margins of about 20% and intermediaries at margins of almost 10%, many primary producers - the farmers - without whom bread cannot be put on the shelves, are currently operating at negative returns. but back to the main line. i told you, this is about our daily bread. food security must therefore be at the forefront of this debate, but especially in the debate on agricultural policy after 2013. because the last thing i think we need is to find ourselves as dependent on the far-away world for our daily bread as we are for energy.
in writing. − on 23 october 2008, in my explanation of vote on the 2009 budget, i called attention to the usefulness of eu intervention in providing school pupils with fruit. a school fruit program can be helpful in preventing children from becoming overweight and unhealthy. the question is why the eu should be the one to provide this instead of the municipalities organising the education. the eu funds now pay the member states, which are obliged to add an amount to the funds themselves, after which the municipalities are responsible for the implementation. the main result of this approach is a lot of red tape and cumbersome bureaucracy. the amount has already been doubled to eur 182 million in the recent budgetary procedure and will be 500 million in the future as a result of the busk report. the dutch minister for agriculture, who is in favour of the present system, has announced in the press that she does not find this enormous increase useful and will fight it. since it is not parliament but the council that decides on this, it is to be expected that this increase will not take place. meanwhile, in public opinion, the school fruit is becoming a symbol of the eu's wrong priorities.
president. - i have one point to make. it is true that europe must play a greater role on the world stage - this has been said by several people - but it must also speak with one voice and not in the fragmented way it does today. the major member states will have to come to terms with the fact that we must now act together as a european union. the leading role in the regulation, re-regulation, supervision and improvement of financial markets was not conceived by gordon brown or nicolas sarkozy, but by the european institutions. the lines now drawn must be defended from europe as well. apart from the agenda for the financial markets, all attention must now be directed to the economic crisis that is coming. all the lights are on red. here too we must take action internationally, but also within europe, with a stronger european action, with a european rescue plan. that's got to be more than a little bit more of the same, but that's got to be a real turnaround. the economists of bruegel have also really fallen from their faith and now advocate that more should be spent. the restraint must be transformed into a policy of encouragement. i think that what they are proposing, that one percentage point, is far too little and that we will not get there with the 1% vat reduction they are proposing, because the figures for the reduction are much higher. in the netherlands, for example, construction is down by 20%, so i think we should apply the low vat rate there to ensure that there is a boost in the housing market, all over europe.
president, commissioner, as coordinator of the epp for regional policy, i want to be very active in thinking about solutions. europe must now come up with a programme of action. i also support mr barroso's position on this. the investments from the structural and cohesion funds - the european budget is already 60 billion a year, which is doubled to 120 billion in the national member states - also offer an opportunity to be active, to be more flexible and to speed up. this is a matter of targeted investment, both in the lisbon - knowledge - and gothenburg - energy and ecology - lines. it's about jobs, it's always about jobs. it is about re-skilling people who lose their jobs so that they can join this new economy. only with such an attitude and with those well-trained people will we get a new perspective after the crisis. the present programme should be noted for a number of points. for example, the 2007-2013 regulations can provide flexibility. funds may be transferred within operational programmes in the member states themselves. the money that has not been committed - and this is a very large sum - can be spent more quickly. we can now put into action the position adopted by parliament, namely to use the unused funds from the n+1, n+2 and n+3 resources in the current period and possibly the free funds from the previous period. we ask the european commission - and i know that mrs hübner is working on it - to come up with an adjustment package to show the citizens before the elections next year that there is a real power in this. we are happy to wait for those proposals now with the call to respond quickly. i would like to thank the commission for its work so far, but i think it will have to be improved. parliament is prepared to do that.
on behalf of the pse group. – mr president, firstly i shall say something about the procedure. it is a pity that we did not discuss it this morning, commissioner, when we were sitting together, because i was a bit surprised that you are so harsh now in your speech to parliament. on the procedure, i am particularly harsh on the way that the french presidency has dealt with our proposals, in not meeting any requirements to negotiate or try to have a dialogue on a possible compromise. it is the same for the commission. with these types of issues it is normal to try to get together and find a solution that meets the concerns of the different parties. i regret that this has happened, and i hope that in the time between the plenary and the vote in committee there will be an opportunity to do that. on the content, i think we do not differ so much. i am also very much committed to relief of administrative burdens. i am always pushing for the xbrl system in accounting and this type of electronic platform. i think we all agree that it is necessary, precisely because in some member states the electronic society is not yet completely in place. it is necessary to have this temporary transitional solution so member states still have the option of using this type of paper registration. this is really a temporary problem and there could be a pragmatic solution. i heard this week that it is only 20 years since e-mail was first used and since the first internet connection, so in 10 to 20 years’ time it will be completely normal for everything to be processed electronically. it is only a very temporary problem that we are facing and we should not make a big issue of it. we should try to find a reasonable, pragmatic solution in the spirit of what you are always telling us you want also.
on behalf of the pse group. – mr president, there are times when politicians might like to influence statistics – particularly at the moment. if the statistics on the economy are so bad, you might wish to take them away and dream that it is different and that this does not influence the spirit of consumers etc. i fully agree with what everyone says about this report: that decent, true and fair statistics are an important instrument for politics and that this is an important reason to have independent statistical bodies to present these statistics. i would also like to congratulate the rapporteur and the commission on the proposals on this regulation. we had good cooperation with the council to reach compromises on what we have stated in this document. it is important to have these independent bodies to have a clear contact point for the commission in the member states and for this european system of statistics to include the statistics code of practice, which has been elaborated by the experts, into european legislation. i think this is a good achievement and i hope that it will really work and help us to overcome the bad statistics and really try to face the recession that is coming and get clearer measures to counter it.

problems and has been one of the elements that has caused the crisis that has prompted the request for this aid. so when you make critical comments, they should be based on good information and not on totally wrong information and, above all, if you had stayed here in the debate until the end, you might have been aware that your information was totally wrong. since that has not been the case, i deficit has been much more important. in 2008 the deficit will be clearly below these figures, it will be below 3%, and in the commitment that hungary has made as a condition of this aid, the deficit target for next year is 2,6%. so if these deficit reduction targets are met - and i hope they are met - hungary will have other problems, and certainly it has and will continue to have them, unfortunately, but next year it will no longer have an excessive deficit.
on behalf of the green group, i would like to make a point of order on behalf of the green group with regard to the debate and vote tomorrow on regulation (ec) no 1073/1999 on the functioning of the european anti-fraud office (olaf). this is the graßle report. it has come to my attention that the commission is considering stopping the current process of revising the regulation in the light of the recent revelations of irregularities within olaf. the commission would therefore like to withdraw the present proposals during the co-decision process with parliament. i would ask you, on behalf of the green group, to ask the commission whether this is the case and whether it will make a statement on this before the start of the debate tomorrow, so that the european parliament will know whether the debate and the vote still make sense.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the situation in the eastern congo. my interlocutor is familiar with the state of emergency in both north and south kivu. his account, without wishing to hide the misery in the region, offers some hopeful points of light. the light is certainly that the congolese presidential guard, in cooperation with the police, arrested government soldiers who were looting more than a week ago. a hopeful sign of a beginning of law enforcement by the congolese government. this is a development which deserves our full european encouragement and support. it is also a bright spot that rebel leader nkunda has so far failed to mobilize or recruit tutsis in south kivu for his power aspirations. my spokesman sees this as a positive expression of tutsi identification with the congolese state and civilian population. a third point of light is the church's reconciliation initiatives at local and provincial level. these platforms involve not only the traditional authorities, the tribal representatives, but also the elected authorities. these platforms in north and south kivu deserve twofold european support: financial assistance and expertise in conflict resolution. the involvement of local authorities, again both groups of local authorities, traditional tribal representatives and elected authorities, is crucial for the provision of humanitarian aid in both kivu areas. my spokesman emphasizes from personal experience that these structures of authority, despite all the conflicts of war, continue to function even under flight conditions. this is why we are urgently calling for the provision of cash to the masses of refugees in the villages of north and south kivu, as well as to the villagers themselves. because in the words of my insider, "if you have money, you can always get food. miraculously, there's always a market somewhere. in the congo, working with cash handouts, giving money away, is quite possible. it doesn't make the population dependent. she can now decide for herself how to spend her money. you're stimulating the local economy and you're taking advantage of it. to make this happen, we will talk to the elected village committees who will identify those most in need". after such a conversation, mr president, and with such a heartwarming european commitment to reconciliation, there must be a future for north and south kivu, indeed for the whole of the congo. i warmly urge the council, the commission, the european parliament and the european institutions to do the same.
mr president, it is significant that the commissioner for industry is here and not the commissioner for competition. i have serious reservations about supporting the car industry. the problems are undeniably very serious. but why support the car industry and not other sectors? the problems may have been exacerbated, but they were certainly not caused by the credit crisis, and state aid should not be a reward for bad behaviour. the eu treaty does allow for rescue aid, but we cannot use taxpayers' money to support struggling industries. let us also not forget that a lot of money has been put into bailing out car manufacturers in recent years and not always with the same success. yes, we have a short-term responsibility for jobs that are on the way, but we also have a long-term responsibility to leave to future generations a healthy economy, a healthy environment, but also healthy public finances. i think it is a bit bittersweet, mr president, that for years there has been no money for health care, no money for education, no money for childcare and no money for the environment and that we are now being asked to put billions into saving the car industry. so if aid is given, mr president, it is with very strict conditions in terms of the compensation of the car industry in the form of restructuring and only for future-oriented, environmentally friendly and innovative activities. because, finally, mr president, state aid may save some firms, but it can also destroy others by distorting competition.
mr president, with all the justified criticism we can have of the car industry and the way the commission has operated, we cannot avoid paying attention to the car industry, because there is a dramatic decline, just as there is in the housing sector. all the lights are red, so we have to intervene. timely, temporary and targeted. i want to emphasize three things: it should not be just the big car manufacturers, but also the suppliers and the financing companies. the restructuring must be geared to cleaner, more efficient cars and coordinated at european level. no single action by member states which exacerbates the problems of their neighbours. this is particularly important for the subcontractors, because they really do work across borders. they operate within the internal market and must therefore benefit equally from the measures, and not just at national level. the european commission must then ensure that the level playing field is maintained. i therefore agree with the role assigned to the competition dg in this.
i would like to say that i agree with what has already been said by rebecca harms. the support needed for the car industry is really a symbol of a failed industrial policy. for years, there has been talk of the need to prepare the automotive industry for the 21st century, and that has not happened. the automobile industry has repeatedly turned a deaf ear to calls for cleaner and more economical production. in the end, none of that came to pass. the car industry has done nothing to innovate to produce lower co2 and still the lobby continues to weaken the carbon requirements of the car industry. that's too bad. the question is a little bit, what now? i think we can continue to support the car industry, but that we must have very strict requirements and that we must only really support electric cars, a whole programme to enable the electrification of the car industry and, of course, the retraining of workers. because it is very, very important that there is a future for the workers in the automobile industry. we must never forget that.
in writing. the health check of the common agricultural policy is a large and important dossier. whether we like it or not, we are setting the direction for agricultural policy after 2013. i would like to make two points. unbundled aid will make agriculture more market-oriented and therefore more competitive and innovative, but we must not move towards a fully liberalised agricultural market. let's not throw away the umbrella when the sun is shining. we must set up intervention mechanisms, insurance, etc., so that they do not disturb the market but at the same time provide a real safety net. the proposals to transfer substantial sums to the second pillar by modulation do not have my support. the first pillar funds appear to be better spent than rural development funds. i also see all sorts of problems with co-financing in relation to a level playing field.

while now, the eu has been trying to come up with a comprehensive package on migration, including measures to tackle illegal migration as well as measures to push europe’s forward-looking strategy on legal migration. you might remember the very emotional debate on the return directive. at the time, the alde group said that the return policy could not be looked upon in an isolated way: it should be opportunities for legal migration, to address the demands of businesses in urgent need of qualified workers. the fact that we could not expect too much from the epp on legal migration is unfortunate, but could in a way be foreseen. the fact that the pse is, broadly speaking, happily in line with the epp on the blue card amendments makes my hair stand on end. following the vote in the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, the scheme has been watered down significantly. far too many restrictions have been introduced, which do not help in any way when trying to make the eu more attractive to highly qualified workers. it must be clear that the intention of the blue card is to increase the competitiveness of the eu economy. the blue card proposal is not an easy attempt to take the best and leave the rest, thereby creating a brain drain from developing countries. the current trend is that the vast majority of the highly qualified workers emigrate to the us, canada or australia instead of the eu. if we want to reverse this trend, we have to be ambitious. this house is about to adopt a report that would weaken an already very modest commission proposal even further. member states’ protectionist practices instead. we all know that the council has been excellent in making ambitious statements, but we also know that, too often, effective decision-making is debate on legal migration, owing to a lack of focus. the tampere programme, the hague programme, the french immigration pact, the stockholm programme coming up – at the end of the day it all comes the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's citizens are able to access the european union's labour market. the commission proposal does not bring about a simple procedure, and that suggestion is being raised. in addition to the commission proposal, national schemes for skilled migrants will remain in force and additional requirements may be required. what is the added value of the european regulation? this is not an answer to the demands of educational institutions and businesses. they want a single system for workers and students from outside the european union, and that will create additional administrative burdens, whereas less was promised. i would like to advocate leaving immigration policy to the member states, so that each member state can have its own clear procedure. we can then make agreements within the eu on whether people should be allowed to travel freely and settle elsewhere.
mr president, we have finally taken an important step. europe is agreeing on the conditions for a single system for recruiting top talent. that's the added value of this card. this step is necessary, but as far as i am concerned it is only the first. with the experience of the blue card in motion, we must look at the next steps in a new mandate. at the same time, mr president, we are living in turbulent times. in the short term, redundancies may be necessary as a result of the financial crisis. it is therefore important that the system of blue cards, as described in mrs klamt's report, leaves room for national member states' policies. member states should be able to decide how many skilled migrants are needed and to work in their own countries without causing a brain drain in sensitive sectors such as health care in third countries. but we also have to look ahead. europe is in dire need of skilled migrants. the best solution is therefore to make the blue card scheme as simple as possible, so that it can actually attract talent. i think we can find that solution in the present proposal, thanks in part to the amendments tabled by the pse group. for example, it is important that people who are already legally resident in the eu do not have to return to their country of origin to apply for a blue card for an eu member state if they meet the other conditions.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this crisis will increase the pressure on our social systems. the expected growth in unemployment may lead to some relaxation in the labour market in the short term. in the long term, however, this will not solve the problems of ageing outlined above. if the deterioration of the economy increases the pressure on the older worker to leave the labour market early, we fall back on the old mistake. the emphasis now and in the future should be on flexible voluntary retirement combined with the organisation of work so that longer working lives become a real option. the financial crisis has once again demonstrated why we need to be responsible with our pension funds. the sustainability of the pension system, adapted to demographic and economic developments and based on risk-averse long-term investment strategies, should be given high priority. the european commission should also work on the regulation and supervision of pan-european pension products.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0179/97). as a large proportion of expenditure is related to the common agricultural policy and the regional funds, the union is being screened. the responsibility is thereby shared with others, who consider the promised money as their own. the collection of funds at central level and their subsequent distribution to stakeholder groups or to projects in municipalities or provinces makes control more difficult. this week we invited the council to agree to more money for school fruit. such a scheme is useful for children's health, but it is best organised at a small, local level rather than at the most large-scale level we know of in europe. we may be able to reduce the risk of fraud considerably by concentrating the cash flow on budget support or equalisation contributions for the poorer regions, with the sole criterion that the inhabitants of those areas should be given as much opportunity as possible to continue living and working in writing. − most people want to continue living and working in the area where they grew up and speak the language. there are two main reasons why people leave their homeland. the first is that they risk being imprisoned or killed. to escape that fate, they become refugees. the second is poverty. people move to areas where wages are higher, even if they do not receive full pay, if their jobs are insecure, if they are poorly housed and do not have good prospects. changing expectations about demographic trends and the shortage of people for certain occupations are leading to a sudden return to the usefulness of immigration. the refugees who come to eu countries spontaneously out of dire necessity are increasingly unwelcome, but those with a high level of education and a chance to do so are encouraged to come. with this sort of selection, we extract these knowledge workers to the countries where they were trained. those countries need these people the most. without these people, they cannot make good the shortfalls that are the cause of their poverty. if a blue card causes brain drain, that's bad for europe and the whole world.

president, ladies and gentlemen, we have unfortunately had to note that, at the 10th anniversary of the eu code of conduct on arms exports, no progress has been made in converting this voluntary code into a legally binding instrument. the european parliament has for years called for the code of conduct to be transposed into legislation binding on the member states, taking into account the increasing internationalisation of the arms industry. the european parliament is currently examining a proposal for a directive aimed at creating an internal market for defence products. the pse group calls for greater transparency and effective sanctions in the event of non-compliance. our group has once again made reference to the code of conduct, but the lack of a legal framework means that we can only call on the member states to act in the spirit of the code of conduct. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. last year alone, western countries sold an estimated $42 billion worth of arms to developing countries; half of which comes from european soil. this has to be different. the european union must set an example in this area.

mr. president, emotions are running high as the climate package nears completion. some argue that the climate can wait, at least until the crisis is over. but that's not a good idea. first, it is absolutely essential that we act quickly against global warming. the reports are that the warming is happening faster than expected. second, the oil is running out. we really need to move to a sustainable energy supply. and thirdly, effective climate policy is a good weapon against recession. investments in renewable energy, green energy infrastructure and energy efficiency in buildings are creating millions of jobs, and we cannot afford to let that go. of course we must be careful that jobs in the steel, paper or automobile sectors do not disappear abroad. as it stands, the package is going in the right direction, but we have to be careful not to leave it empty-handed. the agreement on the fuel directive is at least satisfactory. 10% reduction in co2 based on a well-to-wheel analysis. that is a good and powerful signal, especially at a time when oil companies are increasingly using energy to produce gasoline and diesel. the fuel directive thus provides a major impetus for global reductions in emissions from flares, accelerated deployment of electricity in road transport and the shift towards more efficient biofuels that also meet stringent sustainability requirements. europe is leading the way. similar initiatives have been taken in the usa, and the post-petrol era is in sight.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on economic and monetary affairs. - president, we are pleased with the outcome of the climate package. we clearly see the cda/epp position in this. it is important that europe also sets itself a target of -20% by 2020, which could rise to -30% in the case of an international agreement. what we must be careful about is that it is introduced proportionately in the member states. that all member states comply with it in the same way. the reduction commitments are set for each member state separately. this is basically a fair distribution and it remains important to maintain it. otherwise there are too many freedoms for the member states. if one member state complies with the agreement and the other does not, there is a risk of distortion of competition. so it is not just the climate that is suffering, but also the conditions of competition for firms and industries between the different member states. i have asked for this to be given full attention in the econ report; the commission should strictly monitor compliance and, where necessary, impose additional requirements. we will be following this closely from parliament.
