- i'm the reporter. - the president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, tomorrow will mark the end of a long process. i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs, all the staff, the commission and the french presidency for their commitment, their work and their willingness to cooperate. tomorrow we can agree to an agreement at first reading and give the green light for a special directive. for the first time, co2 requirements are linked to a product and a production process. parliament has worked to improve the directive even further. the directive will be a huge stimulus to the use of good and safe biofuels, to the use of electricity in road transport, which can bring about enormous efficiency gains, and to the reduction of methane flaring. these are wonderful results, and they set europe on a sustainable path. but let's go back to the beginning. the fuel directive has two objectives: air quality and reduction of co2 emissions. in the field of air quality, there are three improvements over the original proposal. firstly, cleaner fuels for shipping will be available more quickly. secondly, the exception for ethanol. the commission proposed to increase the maximum vapour pressure when ethanol is added. there's been a lot of debate about that. the southern countries in particular want exceptions to allow the blending of ethanol. but it is in the southern countries that the problem of harmful ozone is also at play. the compromise reached is that an exception is only allowed if the air quality requirements are met. a third point concerns mmt, a fuel additive which is harmful both to health and to cars. so, you could say, ban. but unfortunately, because of wto rules, that's not easy. that is why a limit value has now been set, and that is a clear benefit for health and for the reduction of neurotoxic substances. now i come to the second main objective: the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions. i have just said that this is the great innovation of this legislation. for the first time, the concrete co2 requirement is linked to the production process. the oil industry will have to report in the coming years how much greenhouse gas emissions are caused by oil extraction, transportation, distribution, refining and the use of diesel or petrol. the well to wheel analysis is then used to establish a standard value. then the whole chain must reduce emissions by 10% by 2020. we have, of course, spoken at length about this 10% target. 6% of this is binding and part of this can be achieved by improving efficiency throughout the chain, reducing flaring, more efficient refineries and repairing leaks. another part can be achieved by using biofuels, but then they must be the most efficient. we are not getting anywhere with growing biofuels, which are only slightly better on balance. we're taking a huge step backwards when we first cut down tropical forests, and then we start growing biofuels. so there are strict sustainability requirements. we have now incorporated these requirements into the directive. it is about co2 efficiency, it is about biodiversity, but it is also about social criteria. the remaining 4% of the 10% reduction is initially non-binding. this 4% is again divided into two parts. part 1 covers the cdm projects in the chain. reducing flaring of gas is one of the most effective ways to reduce greenhouse gases, but it is not always traceable to the petrol or diesel that comes onto the market in europe. therefore, cdm projects are allowed, subject to verification. the other 2% concerns new technologies, for example ccs and also electricity in road transport. electricity is promising, but the techniques have yet to be proven for large-scale commercial application. in 2014 this must be clear, and then the indicative targets can become binding. all in all, i think that with this directive we are making a substantial contribution to the reduction of co2 caused by road transport. it is good to know that this is in line with the choices being made in the united states today. the low carbon fuel standard from california is being emulated across the united states. i thank the shadow rapporteurs once again for their contribution and for the good cooperation, and i look forward to the debate.
the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. i really think the cooperation we've had with each other is responsible for the outcome. only by working together can you stand strong, and i think in this case, we did. it is very important to see the fuel directive in the context of the whole climate package. first of all, the report by claude turmes is of course of interest in this context: the renewable energy directive. we share the sustainability criteria, and i believe that sustainability criteria have made a very significant contribution worldwide. i think it is extremely important that we establish that together tomorrow. i share mrs sinnott's enthusiasm for the algae oil. i'm actually as excited about it as she is; the beauty of this fuel directive is that it gives a great boost to new technologies, which can be a real stimulus to the development of alginate technology. in principle, the production of algae technology involves much less co2, and this makes it very attractive for oil companies and others to invest in these technologies. in the context of the climate package, it is also very important to move on to the next report, the sacconi report on new passenger cars. we have been very vocal in our efforts to ensure that electricity is included in this directive. electric cars are the future. electricity in road transport is many times more efficient than the use of petrol or diesel. so that's where we really need to go. i have seen that mr sacconi's report also provides some incentives in that direction and that is how we solve the chicken and egg problem. both on the car side and on the fuel side, the right incentives must be given to really get to cleaner road transport and cleaner fuels. i hope that with these elements of the transport climate package we will at least make a good contribution to reducing greenhouse gas emissions. and again, thank you all very much for your cooperation.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). these agreements should have already achieved a substantial reduction in emissions. in practice, however, there is very little of this. the average co2 emissions of cars are not much different from those of ten years ago. that is why we must impose strict binding rules. however, a strong industrial lobby has weakened the original commission proposal considerably, which i regret. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the european union. the short-term commitments have also been watered down, partly by a phase-in on both volume and fines, which reduces the effort to be made. i cannot therefore support the agreement reached between the council and parliament and i regret that the commission proposal has actually died.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe-de group. mr president, one european voice, we cannot repeat it enough, is the most important thing in these times of financial crisis. i am in favour of full european harmonization in the field of deposit guarantee schemes. this is also the voice of the european parliament and of the rapporteur, mr ehler. full harmonization is very urgent in times of crisis for two reasons. first of all, it is our job to provide financial protection for the consumer. the credit crisis has brought uncertainty and fear of losing their credit and assets to many european citizens. this is a feeling we must counter. at national level, measures to provide financial protection to consumers have been introduced in several member states. this has been the case in ireland, but also in the netherlands, where the guarantee amount has been temporarily increased from eur 40 000 to eur 100 000. the dutch state, like the irish state, feels responsible for the protection of private and small enterprises. however, it is necessary to consolidate the measures taken in a single european directive on deposit guarantee schemes. we know all too well the consequences of a lack of cooperation and supervision. we must do so in a uniform way, and that is why i am calling for a single maximum amount. that will be in 2010, if we follow the rapporteur's proposal. a maximum of €100,000. the present situation, with member states such as germany and ireland providing unlimited cover, is a danger to europe. money from the netherlands and england, for example, is going across the border to the neighbouring countries because of this unlimitedness. this has a negative impact on stability. i am glad that the council has agreed to this. the rapporteur, mr ehler, has made good proposals, including those concerning small and mediumsized enterprises. these companies should also continue to be covered by the deposit guarantee scheme. i think that in this way we as the european parliament have achieved a good result for consumers and small and mediumsized enterprises and i hope that the commission will also support this.
first of all, mr president, i would also like to thank all the actors for their role in the budgetary process this year. we have once again succeeded in establishing a budget for 2009. the council has returned to the usual ritual of a day's sitting. i think that the commission has a duty to ensure that the community's financial interests are not undermined by the fact that the community is not able to finance its own activities. i don't see that it matters whether it's 0.88, 0.92 or 0.9, it should be enough. it may be that next year gross national product could fall. it would be above the 0.9 agreed now. would that really give satisfaction to the various groups in parliament? i don't understand that. so, payout percentages should be adequate, not more or less. and if not, there can always be a supplementary budget later in the year. i would like to mention one pilot project, namely a pilot project to study the justification of income supplements beyond 2013. i would warmly recommend that in the commission's interest, because when we start the discussions next year, i think it is important that we know why we are giving these income supplements. is that really payment for services rendered, yes or no?
president. - i should like to begin by congratulating the rapporteur, mr haug. it was a very pleasant cooperation and the committee on development and cooperation is satisfied. three things were important to us. firstly, that there was a response to the food crisis, and we are pleased that the commission proposal was amended and that a compromise was found which was supported by parliament and the council, making 1 billion available for the food crisis. what is ultimately at stake is a sustainable solution. and that means food security for the developing countries themselves, and that's what our pilot project is about. a major problem is that small farmers do not have access to micro-credit. it's hard for them to get good seed and fertilizer and to invest in irrigation if they can't pre-finance. we are therefore pleased that our proposal for the pilot project on micro-credit to small farmers has found broad support. we trust that the commission has the will to implement this pilot project. the second point concerns evaluation. we hear a lot of criticism of development cooperation today. to gain traction, we must not only explain what we meant, but above all what we have achieved. and it is a pity that the commission's reports are still mainly concerned with intentions. that is why we have called for more capacity in the commission to improve evaluation on results. we welcome the support of parliament for these proposals. thirdly, mr president, we continue to call for an increase in the budget for chapter 4, external expenditure. if we are to meet our ambitions and responsibilities, which are right when it comes to kosovo, the middle east, etc. where it is not just development cooperation but also conflict areas, then clearly more money and flexibility is needed.
in writing. − across europe there are forces which see the current lack of labour protection in the new eastern member states as an important competitive advantage which enables firms to reduce their labour costs. the working time directive, as the council wants it, will ultimately lead to everyone working longer hours for less money. voters are utterly unable to understand that cooperation between their countries within the eu is contributing to deterioration rather than improvement. this proposal allows for the use of temporary exceptions which extend working hours during peak production or tourist season throughout the year. waiting time for, for example, firefighters no longer needs to be rewarded. a majority of this parliament was prepared to agree to this earlier, on the condition that the opt-out which still exists would be completely abolished within a few years. the council, on the other hand, wants to make this possibility permanent and more extensive. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. it will only undermine better national arrangements in many eu member states. the european union is not a mere institution, but a political body. fortunately, a large majority today did vote in favour of ending the possibility of exemption within 36 months.
in writing. − drivers tend to drive faster abroad than in their own country and are also more likely to park incorrectly. in practice, fines for speeding and parking are rarely collected. this is particularly irritating to the inhabitants and the managers of the large cities. it is precisely in this area, equal obligations for all road users, that european cooperation should have been effective long before now. foreign toll charges are also payable by everyone, and for years there has been talk of the possibility of automatically collecting electronically registered tax liabilities in the place of residence of the person concerned. the same should apply to road fines. it is bad for road safety and the environment if motorists can conclude that in a foreign country they can break all the rules with impunity. we therefore support the proposal to facilitate the collection of road fines across borders. that does not change our view that criminal law is and must remain a national matter and that the european arrest warrant, which requires people to be handed over by their country to another state to be detained there for a sometimes very long period, is a new injustice.
i think that we are again having an important debate today, mr president, and one which is also leading on the position and role of the european parliament and the european union in promoting human rights. it also defines part of who we are. as europeans, we attach great importance to respect for the universal and inalienable rights of every individual, wherever he may be in the world. but while our shared values of equal opportunities and respect for fundamental rights are an integral part of the european treaties and thus the basis of the european union, it is far less natural for us to call on each other when things go wrong. this report by the rapporteur, mr catania, rightly points the finger at that, and i would like to congratulate the rapporteur and all those who have worked on it for that approach. i ask the commissioner what he thinks of the idea that we should think about it within the european union, whether we can not come up with some better rules to speak to each other, if we think that things are wrong with human rights. i should like to focus on a specific point in the report: the situation of the roma in the european union. roma are not only the most discriminated community in europe, they are also a transnational minority across borders. the fact that this is not in keeping with the strictly national responsibility to ensure that their rights are respected has been clearly demonstrated by the outbreak of violence against the roma in italy over a year ago. the first responsibility for the care of residents lies, of course, with the member states, but each member state is obliged to do so within the limits of the european and international conventions. but all too often, unfortunately, we find that the member states invoke the so-called non-intervention principle. minority issues are a national issue. i think that is no longer acceptable, that we should debate, as i said, whether we can also agree within the european union on how we deal with human rights violations within the eu and put an end to the policy of not talking to each other about them.

the european union is not a party to the treaty of rome, but it is a party to the treaty of rome. illegal migrants are intercepted by the authorities there before they reach the european union, and this is done with financial resources and logistical support from the european union for the local authorities. european aid to african countries to curb unwanted immigration to europe is thus having an effect. but is it the effect we expected? i appreciate that the frontex operation has saved many people from the sea, but i fear for the fate of those who have fled for political or religious reasons. we are receiving reports from amnesty international and the unhcr that people are being sent back to their countries of origin without any form of investigation. the member states are rightly concerned to prevent illegal migrants from having recourse to our legal system. it does raise the moral question whether this approach will allow political and other refugees to access the asylum procedure. is the refugee convention being respected by african countries? i therefore call on the commission, commissioner barrot, to link aid to african countries to the humane treatment of political refugees, in accordance with the geneva convention on refugees. amendment no 4 to the report by mr moreno sánchez has my support. the activities of frontex are therefore unintentionally creating a new problem. the full responsibility for this must be placed on frontex. i am in favour of making provision in the very short term to ensure that political and other refugees can continue to have recourse to humanitarian shelter. the success of frontex depends on that.

- i'm the reporter. - mr president, i apologise for the delay. these are difficult times for small and mediumsized enterprises. the crisis is not only affecting banks and listed companies, but the whole economy and is also hampering the sme job engine. it is therefore good that our european work should give this sector a heart. we're doing that through a series of measures that were introduced last summer under the small business act. i am working on a number of proposals in that series. the statute for a european company, more possibilities for micro-credit and the structuring of the option for a low vat rate for service providers targeting the local market of services to private individuals. a very important concern for smaller firms is the administrative burden. most of this pressure comes from national and local authorities, but in so far as european regulation plays a role, we have been actively working to clean up and alleviate this pressure. so is this file. the simplification of information requirements and the two directives on them, which are 25 and 30 years old and have been amended several times, are now being refined and simplified in a so-called fast track procedure. however, these measures are only a small step towards alleviating the burden. it takes a lot more than that. we have expressed our impatience with this in the legal affairs committee of this parliament. we have also tabled a resolution with this legislative dossier to urge the commission to speed up a much more thorough review of the rules for small and mediumsized enterprises. the commission was already working on this. the aim is to achieve greater harmonisation of european rules for small and mediumsized enterprises. we have also had a discussion and a report by mr radwan in the committee on economic and monetary affairs, in which we explicitly said that the international accounting standards board, which was also working on an ifrs for smes, should not be the route, but that we ourselves should achieve further european harmonisation on the basis of the legislation already in place. the debate was brought to the fore by mr lehne, who was the shadow rapporteur for this report, in order to give the member states an option to exclude micro entities, the really smaller companies, from this european regulation. i think that this is an emergency measure, which, although proposed by the stoiber group, does not lead to fundamental simplification in the long term, because it only gives the member states an option and will therefore lead to great differences between the member states. so the ultimate objective and option should be to achieve such far-reaching harmonization that even the companies below the micro-entity threshold have the same system and a very simple system. in that simple system, i would like to point out the possibilities of what is called xbrl - that is a system where entering data for different authorities can be done in a very simple way and where different authorities can use that same data, so that it also becomes much easier and simpler for companies to provide that kind of information and that information is then useful in a lot of different ways. in short, we must also have a good discussion in the coming months about the resistance in the accounting world to the exclusion of these micro-entities. the commission must come up with proposals. we must therefore consider what is the best method for mediumsized companies, but also for those very small companies, in order to ensure transparency and a good system of accounting which will enable them to do a number of things without imposing on them a huge administrative burden.
- i'm the reporter. - i think the signal from this parliament has been clear. we believe that the emergency solution of allowing member states to exclude micro-enterprises from european regulation is a short-term solution, but not the final solution. i hope that the commission has received this message. for these companies, and for smes in general, we want, also from europe, uniform regulations which also enable them to operate in the internal market, but which are at the same time simple regulations, as mr gauzès has also said, with only one desk and one set of rules, as with the xbrl, so that they are not, as now, burdened with enormous administrative burdens. because even if you give the member states the option of excluding them, that does not mean that the member states can impose their own rules, which are different in all the member states. that will not solve anything in the medium term. in the medium term, we would very much like to see a proposal which provides for a simple harmonised regulation which can be applied throughout the internal market and which does not impose a huge burden on small and mediumsized enterprises, and particularly very small enterprises, just a simple system for providing information on their annual accounts. that is the intention of this parliament, and i hope that message has been received.
author. - mr president, in the previous emergency debates on zimbabwe on 7 july 2005 and 24 april 2008, i pointed out why mugabe has managed to stay in power for so long. the people remember their country's violent past. to many, he remains the hero of the liberation struggle. according to this view, everything he does must be considered good in advance. anyone who opposes him will, as before, serve the interests of the foreigners and at home only those of the privileged white minority. if mugabe were to lose power, the country would be re-colonized and the majority of the population would be discriminated against. the reality is different. the sudden radicalism of mugabe a few years ago, who had never carried out a thorough reform of agricultural land tenure and who had perpetuated the gap between rich and poor in his country, was mainly intended to win new support among a younger generation and not alienate his old comrades. that approach has won him more fanatical and violent supporters, but certainly not the support of the majority of his countrymen. europe and america have misjudged zimbabwean relations for years, raising suspicions of colonial ulterior motives. it was the feeling of hostility that kept mugabe in power. it is only now, before everyone is aware of the incompetence and disastrous nature of his policies, that resistance is growing. without the killings and intimidation, he would not have won the presidential elections and would have turned the narrow parliamentary majority for the opposition into a government. we are now one step ahead of the previous emergency debates. the water supply is broken and the cholera is killing people. that is not a reason for triumphalism over the failing opponent, but for aid to the people of zimbabwe. they deserve better government, but it cannot be imposed by anyone from the outside. we can stop mugabe from getting outside support.
the european parliament has been a party to the european convention on human rights since the beginning of the century. unfortunately, that most democratic period was also marked by administrative incompetence, rampant privatization and corruption. the chaotic period under president yeltsin in the 1990s created the climate for a return to a strong central state authority without much room for opposition or critical investigative journalism. although, unlike in the communist era, several parties now participate in elections, power is once again in the hands of one party, other parties are systematically obstructed and there is personal glorification around the leader of the leading party. a significant part of public opinion is behind that leader and does not care about room for criticism, alternatives or opposition. and it will probably remain so as long as russia is unexpectedly rich and successful in exporting gas and oil. this severely limits the scope for genuine democracy. opposing the current majority view are those who point to the enormous contrast between poverty and wealth, the high degree of secrecy, intolerance, the curtailment of the rights of autonomous regions, the treatment of ethnic minorities, the violent situation in the north caucasus, the neglect of economically weak regions, the mistreatment of conscripts, discrimination against homosexuals, impunity for some murderers, the one-sided bias of the police and judiciary and the restriction of the freedom of non-governmental organisations and the media. our sympathy must be with the critics. we can help those critics by setting a good example ourselves and supporting human rights activists in their fight against disappearances, harassment, secrecy and murder. in practice, countries with well-functioning parliamentary democracies inadvertently contribute to the opposite. we are not helping the critics by creating a missile shield or by supporting the georgian attempts to occupy abkhazia and south ossetia. that is seen as hostile acts that only lead people to rally behind putin. nor are we helping them, in our need for gas and oil, by accepting human rights abuses in russia. the resolution rightly states that human rights, the rule of law and democracy must be the main priorities in relations with russia.
